# John

**BERITH PRESS · ENGLISH ANNOTATIONS · 1657**

# ANNOTATIONS ON THE GOSPEL

# ACCORDING TO ST. JOHN

**THE ARGUMENT AND CHAPTERS 1–21

**

**THE ARGUMENT**

The holy penmen appointed by God to write the Gospel of Jesus Christ were some apostles, as Matthew and John; and some apostolical men their disciples, as Mark and Luke.

John was one of those whom Christ named Boanerges, Sons of Thunder; James his brother, and son of Zebedeus (Mark 3:17). His calling is recorded, Matt. 4:21. He was the beloved disciple, usually present at Christ’s chief miracles; as, on Tabor (Matt. 17:1), in raising Jairus’s daughter from the dead (Mark 5:37), and so in his passion in the garden, and under the cross while he suffered, where Christ commended his mother to his care. He lived longest of the apostles. In Domitian’s time, he was banished into the isle Patmos, where he wrote the Revelation: after which, under Nerva, he was recalled to Ephesus (being aged about 97 years, which was the one hundredth year of our Lord), where he wrote this Gospel, some say, at the entreaty of the Christians of Asia, for the refutation of Ebion, Cerinthus, and others, who blasphemously denied the Deity of Christ. Having therefore read the other three evangelists, recording principally those things which concerned the humanity and miracles of Christ; that which they omitted concerning his Deity and doctrine, this high-flying eagle discovers; his main end being to show that Christ is indeed the very Son of God, God manifested in the flesh, coequal, coeternal, consubstantial with the Father; and that we believing in him may have eternal life, John 20:31: which he lays down by three main arguments: first, from the effects; as before his incarnation, in the creation of the world; after his incarnation, by his miracles. Secondly, from the testimony of men (Moses, the prophets; John Baptist, and the apostles) and of God the Father and the Holy Ghost, evidently declaring him such. Thirdly, from his doctrine peculiar to the Messias, speaking with power, as he that had the word of eternal life; not as the scribes and Pharisees, as Matthew noted, Matt. 7.

First therefore, he shows that Christ is very God, as very man: as also, how he was manifested; what disciples he first had, and what beginning of miracles, unto his first ascension to Jerusalem, chs. 1–2.

Secondly, how, about the first Passover there mentioned, he entered on his public ministry, casting the buyers and sellers out of the temple; instructed Nicodemus concerning regeneration; returned into Galilee; discoursed with the Samaritan at Jacob’s well, about true worship.

Thirdly, how at the feast he went again to Jerusalem, and healed the lame man at Bethesda, ch. 5.

Fourthly, how at another Passover he fed 5,000. Walked on the sea, and taught concerning the Bread of Life, at which many were offended, ch. 6.

Fifthly, how he went up to the feast of tabernacles, and taught in the temple, ch. 7. He dismissed the adulteress, by touching the conscience of her accusers, ch. 8. Heals the blind, ch. 9. Discoursed of the good Shepherd, ch. 10. Raised Lazarus, ch. 11. Returned into the city with great acclamations of the people, ch. 12. Washes his disciples’ feet: admonished concerning Judas’s treason, and Peter’s denial, ch. 13. Taught and comforted his disciples, chs. 14, 15, 16. Prayed to his Father, ch. 17. Suffered, chs. 18 and 19.

Lastly, how he rose again from the dead, and appeared to many, chs. 20–21.

## CHAPTER 1

**1. ***{In the beginning}* 1 John 1:1. *ἐν* *ἀρχῇ*, that is, *ἀπ*’ *ἀρχῆς*, from before the beginning of any creatures: *quod in principio erat, nunquam non erat*, Fulgentius *ad Thrasimund.* That which was in the beginning was ever. So Chrysostom, hom. 2, says *in the beginning was*, is nothing else but *εἶναι ἀεί*, &c., ever to be, from eternity, in the beginning, not temporal, determinate, or to be measured by time; but indeterminate, eternal, and before all time, Col. 1:17. *ἡ* *ἀρχὴ* *ἐν* *ἀρχῇ* *ἦν* *ὁ* *λόγος*, Greg. Nazianzen, orat. 35: the Word was the beginning, in the beginning: the meaning is, the Word, that is the Son of God, had not beginning with the creatures, but was before their beginning from eternity.

*{was}* Not made when the creatures were, but before any of them were, *he was*. This *ἦν* is as much as *ὑφίστατο*, *he was in being*. Being is most proper to God; who is *יהוה*; that name is of *היה* *Haia, fuit, extitit*: it signifies God, *qui fuit, est, & erit*. See Avenar. in *יהוה*. The Greeks express it by *ὁ* *ὤν*, *who is*, or *truly subsists*: by whom all things were made, in whom they have their being. See Rev. 1:4. See V. Shindler, *Pentaglot.* in *היה*. See Exod. 3:14. The 70 there have, *ὁ* *ὤν*, the being or existence, as Suidas gives it, *ὑπάρχων*; it is properly spoken *ἐπὶ* *Θεοῦ*, of God, and is as much as *αἴδιος*—eternal: for he is his own eternal being; so is none of the creatures, they have being of and in him, Acts 17:28, and had beginning in time from him.

*{the Word}* The promised Messias of whom God spoke before to all the fathers (it is usual with the Hebrews to put *דבר* *dabar* for a word, or work, and thing, as Gen. 24:50, where the 70 have *τὸ* *πρᾶγμα*; see Deut. 19:3; 1 Kings 6:19 and 12:24; Ps. 28:2, &c.), and he is the same who is the only interpreter of the Father, verse 18, by whom God revealed his will to the prophets, Heb. 1:1. See Jer. *Paulin.* ep. 2, l. 2. This is, *verbum substantiale, non prolativum*. As Fulgent. *ad Thrasimun.* l. 1 expresses a substantial or subsisting word, &c.

*{was with God}* Coeternal, consubstantial, coequal with the Father, *Æqualis vera divinitate*, equal in his true Divinity, less in his true humanity. Fulgent. *de fide orth. ad Donat.* See Prov. 8:22; 1 Cor. 1:24.

*{and the Word was God}* Christ is eternal God, *Deus Patri, ὁμοούσιος, and συναΐδιος*, consubstantial and coeternal with the Father. See the Nicene Creed. Athanas. Tom. 1, *ad Jovian. ep. de fide*. Christ the Son of God did not then begin to be, when he was manifested in the flesh; but was God from all eternity, made flesh in the last times. *Primam quippe nativitatem filii, nullatenus præcessit eternitas patris: cujus tamen secundæ nativitati præcessit temporalis nativitas matris*: for the eternity of the Father did not at all precede the first nativity of the Son; whose second nativity yet the temporal nativity of his mother preceded. Fulgentius, qu. 5, *de fi. orth. ad Donat.*

**2. ***{the same was, &c.}* Gen. 1:1. God of the same substance with the Father, God of Gods; not by multiplication of the essence, but by unity of the Deity: for as Greg. Nazianzen says, orat. 37, the Father was *that true light, &c.*, and the Son was *that true light, &c.*, and the Holy Ghost was *that true light*, *ἦν*, *καὶ* *ἦν*, *καὶ* *ἦν*, *ἀλλ*’ *ἓν* *ἦν*. *Φῶς*, *καὶ* *φῶς*, *καὶ* *φῶς*· *ἀλλ*’ *ἓν* *φῶς*, *εἷς* *Θεός*. Here is a *was*, and *was*, and *was*, and yet but one *was*, or being; *light*, and *light*, and *light*, yet but one light, one God. So that the Word was in the beginning with God, *ὡς* *μὲν* *πρὸς* *τὴν* *πηγὴν* *ποταμός*, as Athanas. Tom. 1, *ad Serap.*, expresses it, as the stream to (or in respect of) the fountain.

**3. ***{all things were made by him}* See Col. 1:16; Heb. 1:2, 3. This declares the Deity of Christ, for none can create, but God only. So is he equal with the Father, because all things were made by him; and less than the Father (in respect of his humanity) in that he was *made of a woman, &c.*, Gal. 4:4. All things subsisting were made by him: yet no sin was made by him, because that is no substance or nature; but the corruption of nature, as Prosper shows, *Epigr.* See Gen. 1 and 2. He only is the true God: false gods made not heaven and earth, Jer. 10:11. The creatures can make something of a preexistent matter; but to create, which is to make something of nothing, *οὐκ* *ἐξ* *ὄντων*, 2 Macc. 7:28, or rather as the 70 read it, *ἐξ* *οὐκ* *ὄντων*, of that which had no being before, this is peculiar to God; this neither men nor angels can do: hence there is proved the Deity of Christ.

**4. ***{in him}* In Christ the eternal Word; before he created the world: or *in* is put for *διὰ*; so it shows the cause of the creatures’ life *effectricem, and conservatricem*; effecting and preserving, Heb. 1:2, 3; Acts 17:25, 28.

*{was the light of men}* The fountain of reason and understanding to men, made to God’s own image: he speaks not here of angels, but of men; not of the Jews only, but of all sorts of men, as Chrysostom notes.

**5. ***{and the light}* Christ, who illuminates by the Holy Ghost, *ὁ* *Χριστός* *ἐστιν* *ὁ* *ἐν* *αὐτῷ* *φωτίζων*, Athanas. *ad Serap.* It is Christ who enlightens in (or by) him. And as the sun is not lessened, or made more dim, by giving his light to multitudes; so neither is Christ after he created the world, and these numberless creatures; *ἀνελλιπὴς*, *μένει* *μηδὲν* *ἐλαττούμενος*, *οὐδὲ* *ἀτονῶν* *ἐκ* *τῆς* *δημιουργίας* *τῆς* *πολλῆς*, Chrysost. hom. 4, *in Joh.* 1. He remains indeficient, perfect, neither diminished, nor languid by that great work.

*{shineth in darkness}* In man’s mind (after the fall, full of darkness, Rom. 1:21). Such is carnal wisdom, Rom. 8:7, and in the world, as that is a clear *speculum* of God’s wisdom, Rom. 1:20.

*{comprehended it not}* Understood it not, 1 Cor. 2:14. Neither acknowledged Christ, as verse 10, to know things appertaining to the kingdom of heaven, *non dat natura, sed gratia*, nature gives not, but grace, says Fulgentius *ad Theod.* ep. 6. See 1 Cor. 2:14.

**6. ***{there was a man}* Matt. 3:1; Luke 1:5, 17, 80 and 3:2. One truly man, though so excellent, as that Christ testified, Matt. 11:11, *among them that are born of women, there hath not risen a greater than John the Baptist*. Christ so being man, that, *lateret in eo Deus*, Aug., there was sent before him a great man, by whose testimony he might be known to be more than man, concerning whom he testified.

*{sent from God}* Therefore (Mal. 3:1) he is called *מלאך* *malach*, 70, *ἄγγελόν* *μου*, my angel, which signifies a messenger or ambassador of Christ (Mark 1:2, 3) to prepare his way, by manifesting him to Israel. See Luke 3:2. God assigned him to his office, and sanctified him thereto from the womb, Luke 1:15, and by his word which came to him in the wilderness, Luke 3:2.

*{whose name was John}* Luke 1:13, 60, 63. Syriac *יוחנן* *Juchannan*; which name (says Theophylact) is interpreted *Θεοῦ* *χάρις*, *the grace of God*; Hebr. *יוחנן* or *יהוחנן* *Jochanan* or *Johhanan* (see V. Shindl. pent. in *חנן*). Gr. *Ἰωάννης*, thence our name *John*.

**7. ***{witness of the light}* Christ the light; witnessing, and declaring, preaching, and testifying to Israel, that this Jesus Son of the patriarch Abraham, and King David, was their promised and expected Messias.

*{through him}* That is, John’s testimony and ministry: not because he wanted man’s testimony, as Chrysost. notes, hom. 5, but that men might believe: it was only for our sakes that he became man, and subjected himself to the testimony of man.

**8. ***{he was not that light}* John Baptist was not Christ, that increated light, which the Jews once supposed, Luke 3:15, 16.

**9. ***{that was}* Christ was the eternal light, before John testified of him.

*{the true light}* *αὐτοφῶς*, Light itself, Original light; which properly deserved the name of light, a light by nature; not only by participation, as the creature has light. The light promised to enlighten the Gentiles, *which sate in darkness*, Matt. 4:15; Isa. 42:6; 49:6; Luke 2:32; Acts 13:47.

*{that cometh into the world}* Which is born: he speaks of the common benefit of reason and understanding: no man is born without this light: though infants have little or no use of reason, yet have they a reasonable soul, apt to understand, though by reason of the infirmity of body, they little do.

**10. ***{the world was made by him}* As verse 3. Heb. 11:3. Who is the eternal Word, and wisdom of God. See Prov. 8:22, &c.

*{knew him not}* God’s wisdom, power, and glory shined in all the creatures, yet they knew him not, nor glorified him as God. See Ps. 19:1; Rom. 1:23. Of his manifestation in the flesh he speaks, verse 11. They of whom the world was not worthy (Heb. 11:38) knew him, but they who love the world, know not the Maker thereof. The worldly man acknowledges him not, though he know as much as shall serve to convince his conscience: here he calls the wicked multitude, ignorant and blind sinners, the world, as Chrysostom notes: the saints before his incarnation knew him, and expected salvation by him.

**11. ***{he came into his own}* To Israel his own peculiar people: though he were in the world before (for he made, and by the same power still sustains and continues all the courses of nature in all the creatures, Heb. 1:2, 3; John 5:17), yet he seemed not to be present *τῷ* *μήπω* *γνωρίζεσθαι*, for that he was not yet known, or manifested, Chrysost. hom. 7. He came to his own land, Ps. 33:12 and 78:71 and 79:1, &c., which he chose of all the world, for the people by whom, and the place in which he would be worshiped: so as being of the seed of Abraham, the land of Canaan was his own country; and as the Son of David, Jerusalem was his: to these he came, not because he had need of them; but because they had need of him their promised Savior and Redeemer; yet so foolish was this ingrateful people, that they would not receive him.

*{received him not}* Acknowledged him not for their Messias.

**12. ***{but as many as received him}* By true faith and obedience: which (says Fulgentius *ad Monim. de præd.* l. 1), because the evil will of man could not deserve, *non est ejus causa, nisi sola Dei voluntas bona*; there is none other cause thereof than the good will of God (see Phil. 2:13), who, though he would have all men good, yet compels he no man: but persuades and draws, as Chrysostom notes: making men willing of unwilling, by his preventing grace.

*{power to, &c.}* This dignity, right, or privilege, 1 John 3:1, adoption of sons, Gal. 4:5: to be counted worthy of the kingdom of God, 2 Thess. 1:5.

*{on his name}* In Christ, our Mediator and Savior.

**13. ***{the will of the flesh}* That corrupt nature of man which is contrary to the Spirit: not by any natural course. See Gal. 5:17.

*{but of God}* Of God, the fountain of regeneration and free grace.

**14. ***{the Word was made flesh}* Matt. 1:26. The Word, the Son, not the Father, nor the Holy Ghost, became man, not by confusion of natures, but by uniting the divine and human natures in one person of Christ, God and man. Thus would the eternal Word become flesh, *Quando, quod fecerat, factus, opus suum dignatus est reparare*. Fulgentius *de myst. mediator. ad Thrasym.* l. 1. When being made, such as himself made, he vouchsafed to (become man for the) repair (of man) his own work. He became flesh, that is, man of a reasonable soul and human body; for so the Hebrews by *בשר* *basar*, flesh (as the Greeks by *σάρξ*) understand the whole man, consisting of body and soul, Gen. 6:12; Job 12:10; Isa. 40:5; Rom. 3:20, &c. He became man, *non mutando quod erat, sed assumendo quod non erat*. August. *Pascent.* ep. 174. Not by changing the Godhead, but by assuming the manhood. So he became man as still remaining God: the Godhead became not a manhood; nor the humanity, a Deity: he was *made flesh*; that is, made himself, or assumed flesh in which, *manifestaretur sensibus hominum*, Aug. *de trin.* l. 5, c. 11, he might be manifested to the senses of men: the Nicene Synod excellently determined it, *ὥσπερ* *ἡμεῖς* *τὸ* *πνεῦμα* *λαμβάνοντες* *οὐκ* *ἀπόλλυμεν* *τὴν* *ἰδίαν* *ἑαυτῶν* *οὐσίαν*· *οὕτως* *ὁ* *Κύριος* *γενόμενος* *δι*’ *ἡμᾶς* *ἄνθρωπος*, *καὶ* *σῶμα* *φορέσας*, *οὐδὲν* *ἧττον* *ὢν* *Θεός*. (Athanas. Tom. 1, *Synod. Nicen. contr. Ar. decret.*) The meaning is, as we receiving the Spirit of God, do not thereby lose our own being: so the Lord Jesus, becoming man for our sakes, and bearing a human body, does notwithstanding still remain God unchangeable: his taking on him the form of a servant was not any lessening of his Deity; because (says Fulgentius *de incarn. & gr. Dom.* c. 2), *hoc habet, semper esse quod est*: he has ever the same being. Be comforted you sons of God in the sorrows of transitory flesh and blood: we now decay, and must see corruption; but who can fear the resurrection of this now corruptible flesh? Who can but seriously consider that Christ, God eternal, became flesh, to redeem this body (as well as soul) of ours? *Quid futurus est homo, propter quem, Deus factus est homo?* Prosper. sent. ex Aug. 310. What shall man once be, for whom God became man? And again, the Deity of the eternal Word, coequal with God the Father, became partaker of our mortality, that we might be made partakers of his Holy Spirit. And as Chrysostom says, hom. 10, the beloved Son of God became the Son of Man, that he might make the sons of men the sons of God.

*{and dwelt among us}* *ἐσκήνωσεν*. It signifies a dwelling in tents, or tabernacles: a short time he was bodily present with men, until his ascension: *quasi habuit inter nos tabernaculum*. Budæus, as if he had a tabernacle among us.

*{beheld his glory}* The manifestation of his divine Majesty, not only in his transfiguration, Matt. 17:2; 2 Pet. 1:19, but also in many works, which showed his power and glory, John 2:11 and 11:4.

*{as the only begotten}* *As* signifies not here *similitude*, but *identity*, the very truth: such glory as became the Son of God, and was a certain testimony of his Deity. Fulgentius, *object. Ar. discus.*, notes the difference between *unigenitus*, the only begotten, and *primogenitus*, the first begotten: in that divine and incomprehensible generation, in which before all times he is God of God, he is the only begotten, the Son by nature: but in this generation wherein he became flesh, he is the firstborn of every creature, or the firstborn among many brethren, Rom. 8:29, that is, of those whom he vouchsafed, through faith, to adopt his brethren.

*{full of grace}* See Col. 1:19 and 2:9. As if he said, he did indeed visibly converse, as man with man among us, but with that fullness of grace and truth, which never before appeared in any of the sons of men. By *grace*, understand all the essential properties of the Godhead, power, wisdom, righteousness, goodness, mercy, truth, &c., which were in the Son, by that eternal and incomprehensible generation, wherein the Father communicated his essence unto him. And *we saw his glory*, in many effects of his Deity, miracles, transfiguration, resurrection, ascension, and mission of the Holy Ghost, enabling us with gifts of tongues, and knowledge of the truth to preach the Gospel, and to confirm the same with miracles.

**15. ***{cried}* Freely and publicly, as verse 23. Not in a corner, nor privately, or before few that heard, but freely, boldly, and publicly: he alludes to Isa. 43; Isa. 58:1.

*{cometh after me}* As one baptized of me: before whom I am sent to prepare his way.

*{for he was before me}* More excellent than I: before me, as the Lord is before his servant: incomparably better than I: for when John preached these things, Christ was not much known by great works done, as afterwards: and the people inclined to think that John was Christ, Luke 3:15.

**16. ***{of his fulness}* Col. 1:19. Christ is the fountain of grace, then fully opened, and flowing out, when he came and showed himself to the world, Zech. 13:1; John 4:14 and 7:38, 39. Of whose fullness all we who believe have received the gifts of grace and sanctification, every one according to the measure of the gift of Christ, Eph. 4:7; Eph. 2:8.

*{grace for grace}* Or *grace upon grace*: abundance of grace, and increases of graces one by another, according to which Christ says, *to him that hath it shall be given, and he shall abound*, Matt. 13:13. A following grace, after a right use of the grace received: so the Gospel is the power of God *to salvation* to every one that believes, Rom. 1:16. God gives all, both the planting and increase; the talent and the right use thereof: and so in giving more and more grace, he does but crown his own gifts: as when he gives knowledge, and a heart to live thereafter; adding thereto, constancy, perseverance, patience, more lively hope of salvation by Christ, &c., here is an accumulation and giving of grace upon grace, and one grace by mediation of another; yet all is but his own gift to us, and work in us, who works all according to the good pleasure of his will, Eph. 1:5, 9; Phil. 2:13. On the contrary part also it is very considerable (which the learned Is. Casaubon, *Exercit.* 15.3, observes) that God in his just judgment punishes willful ignorance, with necessity of not knowing his will to salvation (see 2 Thess. 2:10, 11; Rom. 1:21, &c.), to which he cites the saying of a Rabbin, in *Pirke Anoth*, c. 4, *Merces præcepti est præceptum, & merces transgressionis est transgressio*; the meaning is, that God rewards the keepers of his laws, by giving them grace every day more and more carefully to keep them; and (justly punishing sin by sin) he gives over the neglecters and transgressors of his law to more and more negligence, and therein to final destruction. Fulgentius, *de præd. ad Monim.* l. 1, understands by *grace for grace*, the grace of eternal glorification, for the grace of undue justification; so as the grace of justification, by an undeserved benefit puts away all evil merit, and confirms the good by a continual assistance, &c. And it is therefore not unjustly called grace, because God not only rewards his own gifts with gifts, but also because there the grace of divine retribution so much abounds, that it incomparably and unspeakably exceeds all merit of man’s will and work, though good, and given of God. That all is of free grace, the same expresses, *de incarn. & gra.*, &c., saying, a man willingly has faith, *Sed deus, qui fidem donat, ipse hominis mutat & dirigit voluntatem*: but God who gives faith (Eph. 2:8) himself changes and directs the will of man, so that he may believe and obey the Gospel.

**17. ***{by Moses}* By the ministry of Moses, Gal. 3:19.

*{but grace and truth}* That is, the exhibition of those things which were promised and prefigured in the law: so he opposes the law, to grace and truth; and because the moral law did but show the disease, Rom. 7:7, which Christ only heals; and the ceremonial law only shadowed out that which Christ performed for us: so as Prosper says, the law was given, that grace might be sought for: grace was given that the law might be fulfilled, &c. *Grace and truth are by Christ*; forasmuch as, by the sacrifice of his death prefigured in the legal sacrifices, he satisfied the justice of God, for the sins of the elect, obtained favor and peace with God for them, and regenerates them with his Spirit. For *grace* is it, by which God renders, *malis bona*, good to evil (who as Prosper says, *de vocat. Gent.* l. 1, c. 8, *Opus suum, opere suo reparat*, repairs his own work, with his own work), whereby he freely prevents the evil to make him just, and follows the just (with supplies of grace) that he may not (again) become wicked: he prevents him that is fallen, that he may rise again; and follows him whom he has raised, that he may not fall away: *prevenit, donans homini bonam voluntatem: subsequitur bene volentem, operando in illo boni operis facultatem*, Fulgentius *de prædest.* l. 1. He prevents, by giving man a good will: he follows him willing well, by working in him ability to do the good work.

**18. ***{No man hath seen God}* 1 John 4:12; 1 Tim. 6:16. That is the essence of God: see hereof Augustine ep. 6. We do not (said Minucius Felix *Octav.*, answering a heathen idolater) show our God, whom we adore, neither do we see him: yea, hence we believe him to be God, because *cum sentire possumus, videre non possumus*: we perceive him present when he thunders, raises storms, restores calms, in all his works, but see him with mortal eyes we cannot, who is more pure than any sense can apprehend. The eyes say, if he be not colored, *per nos non intravit*: he has not passed in by us: the ears say, if he made no noise, he passed not in by us, &c. Augustine tom. 9, *Soliloq.* c. 31. *Lo he goeth by me, and I see him not*, Job 9:11. Would you see God with carnal eyes, when as yet you cannot see your own soul, by which you live, speak, (and feel)? The wind blows, you hear the sound, and feel the violence thereof, yet cannot see it; how much less can you in this dark house of clay see the incomprehensible being of God. When he appeared to Abraham, Moses, or the prophets, he did comply with their present infirmity, showing them his presence, by such an appearance as they could apprehend. We see him now in his works, we know and believe in him through Christ: for as Fulgentius says well, *Object. Ar. discus.*, true faith is not ignorant of that it believes, though as yet it cannot see that which it hopes and believes: we shall more clearly see him in the life to come, 1 John 3:2.

*{the bosom}* Most dear, united in nature, and acquainted with all his secrets. See ch. 13:22, 23. He is one with the Father, John 10:30. Therefore they have one omniscience, one will, which no creature could have known, except he had revealed it by Christ his eternal Word: this word, *bosom*, is accommodated to our capacity, and taken from our common use of speaking, who say a bosom-friend, when we mean one who knows, and is acquainted with all our desires, designs, and counsels.

*{declared him}* Gave us the knowledge of God, otherwise unsearchable.

**19. ***{who art thou}* For they doubted whether he were not the Christ, Luke 3:15. They not so much inquired of his name, or family, which probably was sufficiently known; he being the son of Zacharie the priest; as of his office, and authority, so (as he did) to preach, baptize and gather disciples and followers; they were not ignorant that in the time of Messias, the people should be baptized for remission of sin; according as it is written, Ezek. 36:25, *then will I sprinkle clean water upon you, and you shall be clean, &c.* Now this, as the prophecies concerning John Baptist, Isa. 40:3; Mal. 3:1; Mal. 4:5; the admirable things concerning John’s nativity; the inquiry of the wise men for Christ the late born King of the Jews, notoriously troubling Herod and Jerusalem, about thirty years then last past; the immane and barbarous murder of so many infants by Herod, the extraordinary austerity and course of John’s life, and common expectation of the people with all these forecited things, comparing the times for the coming of the Messias; concluded that either John was the Messias, or his forerunner, who would tell them some news of his approach, and manifesting himself to Israel, therefore the governors of the church sent to him to inquire.

**20. ***{he confessed}* Plainly and openly as Acts 13:25.

**21. ***{art thou Elias?}* They understood that literally which is spoken, Mal. 4:5, which Christ interprets of John Baptist, Matt. 17:14. And the angel plainly told his father Zachary, Luke 1:17, that he should go before the face of Christ in the spirit and power of Elias, *Non in anima ejus, nec in carne*, Tertul. *de anim.* c. 35, fine. Neither in his soul, nor flesh, but in that excellency of Spirit which the Lord would give him, as he gave of the Spirit of Moses to the seventy elders, Num. 11:16, 17, 25; John here denies that he is Elias in their sense.

*{art thou that Prophet}* Or *a prophet*; spoken of Deut. 18:15. They expected some notable prophet, verse 25; John 7:40, 41 and 6:14. Whereas Moses spoke of a succession of prophets, such as God would raise them, until the coming of Christ, the head of all prophets.

**22. ***{That we may give an answer to them}* To the Sanhedrin, or great council; whose authority they tacitly allege, that they might receive from him some satisfactory answer, to carry back with them.

*{what sayest thou of thyself?}* Seeing you are not as we supposed you; declare yourself; whom profess you yourself to be?

**23. ***{I am the voice}* Isa. 40:3; Matt. 3:3; Luke 3:4. One whose office is to lift up his voice, and cry in the ears of deaf sinners.

*{in the wilderness}* See Luke 3:2.

*{make straight}* *εὐθύνατε*, make plain: Isaiah, in the letter spoke of their marvelous deliverance under Cyrus, which was a type of our spiritual deliverance by Christ, as if he had said, though the obstructions and difficulties hindering Israel’s return from Babylon to Jerusalem be such as may make it seem impossible for them ever to pass through them, yet the almighty power and providence of God should so bring it to pass, as if so be the way of itself, rough and impervious, should have been evened by scavengers or harbingers of princes, to go that way, so that they should safely, quietly and easily overcome their voyage. See Isa. 41:18, 19 and 43:19, 20; Ezra 1:3, 4, 5, 6 and 7:7, 28 and 8:21, 23, 31, &c. And so shall it be in the return of Israel to God by Christ, and that was the thing which John was to proclaim. See on Isa. 40:3, 4.

**24. ***{The Pharisees}* A most strict sect of the Jews, Acts 26:5. See on Luke 14:1. And men of chief authority among the Jews, by dignity, priests and Levites, by sect Pharisees; no wonder that they peremptorily interrogated John concerning his baptizing, which (out of their opinion of singular knowledge, and authority) seemed to them unlawful, because they commanded it not, who for matters ecclesiastical had the primacy and chief authority in Israel.

**25. ***{why baptizest thou?}* By what authority? Or, who gave you authority, to institute a new sacrament, that indeed belongs to God alone, of whose covenants the sacraments are seals.

**26. ***{I baptize with water}* Unto amendment of life, Matt. 3:11, that you may repent, Acts 19:4. But Christ gives the inward effect, who is the author of baptism, whereof John was but a minister.

**27. ***{He it is who coming after me}* Matt. 3:11. See Acts 19:4. This *ὀπίσω* *μου*, *after me*, relates to that which Zachary said, Luke 1:76, *Thou shalt go before the face of the Lord, to prepare his ways*; the meaning is, he that follows my outward baptism with the inward effect thereof: not baptizing with water only, as I, who am but a servant; but with the Holy Ghost, not only exhorting men to repent, and leave their sins, but giving repentance, forgiving and washing away all their sins. See verse 15.

**28. ***{Bethabara}* Judg. 7:24. It was a passage into Canaan, and as well a type, as place of baptism, the entrance into the church of Christ, and the eternal rest which remains for us, Heb. 4:9.

**29. ***{Behold}* John shows and points out Christ present; herein he was more than a prophet; the prophets foretold of Christ’s coming after many years, but John had the honor to point him out then present, when he proclaimed him.

*{the Lamb of God}* Alluding to the paschal lamb: in Christ is the substance of all legal sacrifices to take away sins; which he did, suffering for them as man, and remitting them as God, he was the lamb metaphorically, in respect of his meekness and patience, see Isa. 53:7, and also as he was a sacrifice for our sins, *ὁ* *ἀμνὸς* *τοῦ* *Θεοῦ*: sent of God, and accepted of God: the article has much emphasis, to express his dignity and to distinguish him as from the typical lamb, and other legal sacrifices, so also to show his relation to all that the law prefigured, or the prophets foretold concerning him, as if he said, this is he, to whom all these point.

*{which taketh away the sin, &c.}* *ὁ* *αἴρων*, *who takes away*, as Luke 6:29, 30, or *who bears*: the Syriac interpreter gives it, *ille qui tollit*, &c. It was not possible that the blood of bulls and goats should take away sins, Heb. 10:4. This only is he who does it, he takes them away continually; for though he suffered but once, yet the virtue thereof shows a continual fountain to cleanse us from our sins, though not altogether, *ut non sint*, (for yet we are partly flesh and partly Spirit) yet *ut non imputentur*, that they shall be no more imputed: be comforted you penitent sinners who can at last repent; no length of time can prejudicate God’s mercy: no repentance (if true and serious) can be too late with God, in whose sight all things both past and future are present: if the continuance of sins could have overcome the mercy of God, Christ would not have come in the last age of the world to take away the sins thereof. Fulgent. *venanti.* ep. 7. He takes them quite away, *ita ut non sint, quorum memor non erit*. Ambros. l. 5, ep. 18. So that (in effect) they are not, which he will no more remember.

*{sin of the world}* The root (original sin) and the branch, the actual, which is mortified, in them by the Spirit, who in baptism put on Christ, the new man, and put off the old man, crucified, dead and buried: see hereof Rom. 6 and 7, where he mentions *σῶμα* *τῆς* *ἁμαρτίας*, the body of sin, *τῆς* *ἁμαρτίας*, sin; because he treats not there of remission of sins, as it is one part of justification, but concerning sanctification in us begun: which remission and sanctification, though they are never divided in the subject (for whosoever God justifies by faith in Christ, them he does also certainly sanctify by his Holy Spirit living in them), yet may they in reason be distinguished: here then understand a taking away of all sin, and all the works of the devil: he says *of the world*, that we may know that the benefit of redemption appertains not unto the Jews only, but to all believers, of any time, place, or age of the world, since the beginning to this present: therefore is he said to be the Lamb of God, slain from the foundation of the world, Rev. 13:8. See 1 John 2:2; Rom. 3:29, 30; John 17:20.

**30. ***{this is he of whom, &c.}* He whom you now see present: though he appear in the form of a servant, is incomparably more excellent than I.

**31. ***{I knew him not}* He knew him not by sight, but by revelation; till he came to be baptized, then he began to know him; first by an inward revelation; secondly by an external sign given him, verse 33. It was God’s all-disposing providence, that John should live in the desert far from any commerce, or acquaintance with Christ, until the time that he was to be manifested unto Israel, lest his testimony concerning Christ should fall under any suspicion of compact, by reason of kindred, or any former acquaintance.

**32. ***{John bare record saying}* Matt. 3:16; Mark 1:10. Christ was testified to be the Son of God, by the visible descension of the Spirit; the Father’s voice from heaven; and John’s witness on earth: see also 1 John 5:7.

*{I saw the spirit descending}* Mark says, he saw *the heavens opened, and the Spirit like a dove descending*, which some think was by a spiritual vision only; as Ezek. 1:1, the prophet saw the heavens open: others take this to have been a visible opening, so that John and the multitude present might perceive that it was no human, but some divine business in agitation; the Holy Ghost is God invisible, 1 Tim. 1:17; Heb. 11:27, and everywhere present, Ps. 139:4, &c. Therefore can never be seen with any mortal eye; nor ascend or descend; he cannot change place, who fills all places, and is comprehended of none: but the invisible and incomprehensible God, for the comfort and confirmation of his elect, manifested his presence in a visible shape: so, descended he in the form of a dove, not in the form of an eagle, vulture, or bird of prey: as, to intimate his goodness; so, to teach us of what spirit we ought to be, in harmless conversation. See Matt. 10:16; Jas. 3:17. See Tertul. *de bapt.* c. 8.

**33. ***{Upon whom thou shalt see the Spirit, &c.}* Upon what man you shall see the Spirit descending in a visible form, &c., he is the Christ: by this sign God revealed him to John, for he was otherwise not distinguishable from other ordinary men, being as we in all things, sin excepted, Heb. 4:15, having made himself of no reputation, and taken upon him the form of a servant, and being found in fashion as a man, &c., Phil. 2:7, 8.

*{with the Holy Ghost}* Who gives the effect of baptism; sanctification and cleansing from our sins. See Matt. 3:11.

**34. ***{That this is the Son of God}* *ὁ* *υἱός*, *That Son of God*: Chrysostom and Cyril note the emphasis of this article, *that Son of God*: the only Son by nature (not by adoption, as are the saints, 1 John 3:1, 2), the very Messias promised by the prophets, and expected by the faithful: this is he; look not for any other; there is not salvation in any other name, Acts 4:12.

**35. ***{John stood}* In Bethabara where he used to baptize.

*{two of his disciples}* He had many hearers; but some more intimate; whom he also privately instructed and prepared for the school of Christ, he names here only two in whom the effect of John’s present testimony concerning Christ more evidently appeared and wrought; possibly more might also hear the same.

**36. ***{Looking on Jesus}* Intentively, and doubtless with singular comfort, as knowing him to be the Lord and Savior of his elect, concerning whom himself was appointed of God, to testify, and whose way he was to prepare, by his preaching and baptizing.

*{behold the Lamb of God}* This is but a short sermon of John, but the sum of all that he had to say, whose office was to declare Christ now exhibited: and this he repeats to awake the attention of his drowsy hearers, who before did not much heed what he said; so use faithful ministers to repeat fundamentals of religion until their auditors mark and understand them, and make some good use thereof.

**37. ***{Two disciples heard him speak}* John gathered not disciples for himself, but Christ: these two are mentioned, who heard and considered what John said: they probably heard the same the day before; but we read of no effect then; now we find how they move towards Christ. God has his times to call; and his word like seed (though on good ground) does not presently spring up in all hearers; in due time it shall in all the faithful.

**38. ***{What seek ye}* This he says, not doubting, but affording them occasion of conference; though indeed it much skills what they seek, who pretend to come to Christ. See 1 Cor. 10:33; 2 Cor. 2:17; 2 Pet. 2:3; Phil. 2:21. Some seek honors, some riches, some other things of this life; Matt. 8:19; John 6:26; Phil. 2:21. Few seek Christ for Christ’s sake.

*{said unto him Rabbi}* This was a title which they used to give great and eminent men; but now it was commonly given to all teachers: he was called *רבי* *rabbi*, who by imposition of hands was declared a wise man. Syriac *רבן* *rabban*, and John 20:16, *רבוני* in some editions for the Greek *Ῥαββονί*, see Joh. Drus. *præter.* lib. 4, in John 20:16, and *ad vo. N. T. Rabbi*. Caninius, *de locis N. T.* Martin. Trostius, *Variæ lection.* in John 20:16. The evangelist interprets there, as here by *διδάσκαλε*, *master* or *teacher*: it is of the Hebr. root *רבב* *rabab*, great, thence *רב* *rab excellens*, or *peritus in aliqua arte, Magister, Doctor*: here we may conceive that though they yet understood not that Christ was the only teacher of his church, yet moved with John’s testimony concerning him, they esteemed him as a prophet and doctor, which was the first step of their hearing him.

*{where dwellest thou}* Or *abidest thou? Where lodgest thou?* Dwelling at Nazareth, it seems he was a stranger there where John was baptizing, and where now saw, and pointed at him; if any now inquire the like of him, let him come and see in his word and ordinance: every river, if you follow it home, will bring you to find the sea, whence it came: so every part of the Gospel will lead you (as the star the wise men, Matt. 2) to Christ.

**39. ***{Come and see}* Thus Christ readily entertains them who seek him in truth; he puts none of them off (see ch. 6:37), yea, he invites all that labor under the heavy burden of their sins, Matt. 11:28.

*{about the tenth hour}* That was two hours before night: the day they divided into twelve hours, which in the summer, they made proportionably the longer; in the winter, the shorter: but so little before night, they consider not the importunity, but in desire to hear Christ, they presently follow him: formal gospellers (like Felix, Acts 24:25) use to dismiss the word, and preachers thereof, unto their *convenient season*, which they will seldom find; but to those who indeed hunger and thirst for righteousness, no time is unseasonable to learn.

**40. ***{Simon Peter’s brother}* The evangelist here names him Peter, by way of anticipation, for he was not yet so named. See verse 42.

**41. ***{The Messias, &c.}* *The anointed.* So the Hebr. *משיח* *Mashiach*, of *משח* *Mashach unxit oleo*, now because kings, priests, and prophets were anointed with sacred oil, the confection whereof might not be imitated to any other use, therefore Christ in whom all these offices met, was by an eminence called the *משיח* *Messiah*, Dan. 9:26. So often in the New Testament *Messias* is put for, and interpreted Christ: *χριστός*, the anointed, or Christ, yet was he not anointed as they in the type with material oil, but with the Holy Ghost, Acts 10:38, and that *above his fellows*, Ps. 45:7. See ch. 3:34; Col. 2:9. Thus the Chaldee often interprets prophecies concerning him. Gen. 49:10, *until* *שילה* *Shilo come*, Targ. *משיחא*.

**42. ***{And he brought him to Jesus}* You must understand this of Andrew and Peter’s first coming to Christ, after which they returned to their fishing again; their calling to the apostleship, when they left their nets and followed Christ as apostles, was afterwards, and is recorded, Matt. 4:18, &c.; Luke 5:10, 11.

*{a stone}* Or *Peter*, Simon of Jonah (Augustin. *in Joh.* reads, *thou art Simon filius Johannis*: so Jerom. *de scriptor. eccles.* and Fulgent. l. 3, *ad Thrasimund.*, whence John Drusius, l. 4, *præterit.*, gathered, that the father of Simon *Peter* was called *יוחנן* or *יהוחנן* and contractedly *יונא* and not *יונה* *Jonah*, a dove), *thou shalt be called* *Κηφᾶς*, which is *Πέτρος*, that is, *Peter*, a proper name; or *πέτρος* an appellative, which is a stone; the Syriac has *כאפא* *cipha*, a stone from *כיף*, Job 30:6; Jer. 4:29. See Jo. Drus. *ad voc. N. T.* tom. prior. *Thou art Peter* (said our Savior, Matt. 16:18), *and on this rock will I build my church*: not on the person, but on the confession of *Peter* saying, *thou art Christ, the Son of the living God*: for (as Isidor. *de etymol.* l. 7 says) Christ was the rock on which foundation even *Peter* also was built. See Eph. 2:20, to which sense Gregor. l. 3, ep. 33 says, *vitam vestram in Petram ecclesiæ, hoc est in confessione beati Petri—solidate*, ground your lives on the rock of the church, that is, on the confession of blessed *Peter*: in Christ all the faithful are *λίθοι* *ζῶντες*, living rocks or stones, 1 Pet. 2:5. This name Christ gives *Peter*, intimating his firm constancy in the truth.

**43. ***{Follow me}* Be my disciple: and he presently followed him: so powerful is his word: he but said at first *let us make man*, Gen. 1:26, and man was made, and here, but *follow me*, and he (as others after) left his former calling, and followed Christ: profane Porphyry and the apostate Julian scoffed hereat, not knowing the power of Christ’s calling; nor believing that the dead shall once hear his voice, and come out of their graves, John 5:28, 29. Nor do wicked hearers feel the power of his word calling them to repentance and newness of life; because they are deaf adders and belong not to Christ, ch. 8:47; 2 Cor. 4:3, 4. Here is comfort on two firm grounds, first (as Chrysostom ho. 17 notes), God prevents our wills with his grace: our sanctification and salvation begins not of, or from ourselves, when, or how could we ever hope to be saved, if that work must not otherwise be accomplished than by our own ability and goodness? Secondly, though this work, in relation to man’s strength, be as impossible, as for a mortal man to climb up into heaven, yet says God our Savior, how easy is it? Why should I despair of his saving me, who can as easily repair me, as he made me? If he but say follow me, I shall follow him into eternal happiness: shall I doubt that he who has suffered the most bitter death for me, will not so much as speak the word to save me?

**44. ***{Philip was of Bethsaida}* A town near unto the lake of Gennesareth, to which Philip the tetrarch, Herod’s brother, Herodias’s husband, gave the honor of city, and called it by a new name, *Julia*. Joseph. *orig. Jud.* l. 18, c. 3. To this very place our Savior denounced so great woes, Matt. 11:21, that from so impious a place he vouchsafed to call out some to be chief apostles, it declares the admirable mercy of God taking some as brands out of the fire; as jewels out of the dirt.

**45. ***{Jesus findeth Nathanael}* He was of Cana in Galilee: John 21:2. All good is diffusive of itself, specially the truth of the Gospel, which as a living water, or swift stream cannot be dammed up: if we are effectually called to Christ, we shall not rest until we have called others to the same communion.

*{Moses in the law}* Gen. 49:10; Deut. 18:18. See John 5:46. Moses in his five books, by whose ministry the ceremonial law was given, which by many representations prefigured him. See Heb. 10:1; Luke 24:27.

*{prophets did write}* Isa. 4:2 and 7:14; Jer. 23:5 and 33:14; Ezek. 34:23 and 37:24, 25; Dan. 9:24; Mic. 5:2; Zech. 6:12 and 13:1; Mal. 3:1, &c. Hence appears the admirable consent of the Old and New Testament, represented (as Gregor. hom. 25, *in Evang.*, well notes) by the two cherubims, Exod. 25:17, 18, 19, 20, covering the *propitiatory* with wings stretched out, touching each other; both looking towards Christ our propitiation, 1 John 2:2, and with contiguous senses reaching one another.

*{the Son of Joseph}* As men supposed, Luke 3:23, but indeed he was, as God *ἀμήτωρ*, without mother; as man *ἀπάτωρ*, without father. Gr. Nazianzen, orat. 38, as Heb. 7:3.

**46. ***{Can there any good thing come out of Nazareth}* He had a prejudicate opinion of the place, either because he considered the prophecy, Mic. 5:2, which says that Christ should be born at Bethlehem: so the Pharisees reasoned, John 7:41, 42, or as they said (John 7:52) to Nicodemus, *search and look, for out of Galilee ariseth no prophet*: the place makes neither good nor bad: the earth is the Lord’s, who is no accepter of persons, Acts 10:34, 35.

*{Come and see}* Be not withheld from Christ by other men’s prejudicate opinion, but try. Philip could not, or would not contend with him; but appealed to his own further experience, as if he had said, do but come to him and you will see, that some good may come from thence.

**47. ***{No guile}* A man of sincere and simple integrity, which makes the difference between true and feigned Israelites.

**48. ***{wast under the fig-tree}* Christ sees us where we are, and when we think not of him: *ipse timendus est in publico, ipse in secreto*; Augustin. *de verb. Dom. in John*, ser. 46. He is to be feared in public, he is to be feared in secret.

**49. ***{Thou art the Son of God}* Christ’s word begat this faith, which broke out into a confession, of more than he heard of Philip: he called him the Son of Joseph, but Nathanael acknowledged him the Son of God.

*{king of Israel}* He knew not yet the extent of Christ’s kingdom, Ps. 2:8; Rev. 17:14. Therefore according to his measure he confesses.

**50. ***{Believest thou?}* This question is in effect a vehement asseveration, and as it were an admiration at his excellent faith.

*{thou shalt see greater things}* Which shall more evidently manifest me to you, and cause you to believe and know that I am the Son of God, and Savior of the world.

**51. ***{Verily, verily}* This repetition intimates the certainty of his assertion; concerning the word *ἀμήν* here used, see the marginal note of the learned Tremelius on this place.

*{heaven open}* The kingdom of heaven opened to men in Christ: which their sin and ignorance shut up.

*{and the angels of God}* He alludes to Jacob’s ladder, Gen. 28:12. See Col. 1:20; Eph. 2:19; Ps. 34:7; Heb. 1:14, and he signifies his divine power, which should be seen in the ministry of the angels to him the head of the church, and the elect his body, the sum is; they who were enemies are reconciled to us in Christ, and we are made fellow citizens with them, Eph. 2:19, and they our guardians, Ps. 34:7; Heb. 1:14. All this is the advantage of true believers.

## CHAPTER 2

**1. ***{Cana}* Cana the lesser, as Jerome says: of which was Nathanael, John 21, and Simon, Matt. 10:4.

*{Galilee}* The lower near the lake of Tiberias; called Galilee of the Gentiles, Isa. 9:1; Matt. 4:13, 15.

*{the mother of Jesus}* It seems that Joseph was dead before Christ began to preach: see Epiphan. *haeres.* 78.

**2. ***{both Jesus was called}* Invited as friends: Jesus came not into the world to abolish human societies, and civil customs; but to sanctify them with his holy presence: for as in him the right to the creatures (lost in Adam) was restored; so would he show, and by example teach men the right use of them: sometimes in a more plentiful (but sober) manner, as in feasts and festivities. See Deut. 16:14.

**3. ***{when they wanted wine}* Greek, *ὑστερήσαντος*, although the word *ὑστερέω* properly signifies, *sum posterior, sum inferior, tardius venio*, yet by a metonymy it is used for *careo, deficio, desum*, Mark 10:21. It seems these married couple were of a slender estate, and some of their guests unexpected: the main thing considerable is, God’s providence, disposing this want (as often he does with his) to his own glory; as it is of Lazarus’s death: ch. 11:4, and the blind man’s want of sight, ch. 9:3.

*{his mother said unto him, they have no wine}* Why she thus spoke is uncertain; Christ having yet done no miracle, to give her confidence that he could supply the want: possibly some other things spoken, not here recorded, gave the occasion: or that she, who long before had laid up in her heart, these things which were spoken of him (Luke 2:51), marked also what John Baptist testified of him; as also what the angel Gabriel told her: and it is more than probable, that in these many years, wherein Christ lived with her, she had often conferred with him, concerning the great work of redemption by him to be accomplished: and if there were here any place for conjecture; why may not we conceive it probable, that he might acquaint her with his gathering disciples, preaching and manifesting himself to Israel, by signs and mighty works? And that this declaration had the force of a petition, as had that of Mary and Martha, John 11:3, *Lord, behold he whom thou lovest is sick*: and his answer seems to make for this; for when he says, *mine hour is not yet come*, he seems not so much to deny a supply, as to blame the unseasonableness of the motion for it.

**4. ***{woman what have I, &c.}* Though he were incomparably greater, as being God, yet being the Son of Mary, as man, he spoke not any otherwise, than became the reverence due to a parent: but because he was not her Son in that nature, by which he would do the miracle he inhibits her prescribing God; and therein recalls her from all respects of motherhood, so far as they hindered the consideration of God’s power and glory, in the following miracle. See Luke 2:49; Matt. 12:48. Hebr. *מה* *לי* *ולך* *mah li valach*, 2 Kings 3:13, *what have I to do with thee?*

*{mine hour is not yet come}* The fit time appointed by God, which I use, when it shall best serve for his honor, the time that I should show myself by signs and miracles unto the world, *certis momentis peraguntur res Dei*, therefore he here intimates, that it was neither impotency, nor incogitancy to supply, the present want which caused him to delay, but because he would teach us to expect God’s appointed time, and to know *that it is not for us to know the times and seasons which the father hath put in his own power*, Acts 1:7. Christ knew it was not opportune to supply them with wine, by a divine and extraordinary power manifested, until the want thereof was manifestly known to the whole company, that all might be witnesses of the miracle, and thereby be moved to believe in him, that they might be saved.

**5. ***{whatsoever he saith unto you}* It is probable that she had an expectation of some extraordinary supply, because she prepared the servants to obey him in order to the same.

**6. ***{six waterpots of stone}* Wherewith they used often to wash and purify themselves. See Mark 7:3. Waters were very scarce in Palestine, fountains being there but rare, as Chrysost. notes, hom. 21, therefore the Jews kept such vessels of water filled by them. The heretic Hebion would have brought into the Christian church, this custom of the Jews, before long to be antiquated.

*{containing two or three firkins a piece}* Measures which the Syriac gives *רבעין* *rebbin*, of the Hebr. *רבע* *rebah*, *a quarta, continet quadrantal octavam partem cadi, sive modii. Tremel.* Gr. *μετρητὰς* *δύο*. Some conjecture that these six contained about half a tun, each vessel being about half a tierce: so that Christ gave a bountiful supply, which set off the excellency of the miracle.

**7. ***{fill the waterpots with water}* He said not, fill the wine casks, but those waterpots there standing; into which they used not to put any wine: neither does he bid them fill some other, the like pots (that might have admitted some jealousy of a compact) but those out of which they had now drawn water for their use; all this, for the clear evidence of the miracle. See 1 Kings 18:44.

*{filled them up to the brim}* Though these waiters knew not what Christ meant to do, yet thus the providence of God directed it, lest any should object, or conceive that some wine might happily be afterward secretly conveyed into the same vessels, and so deceive the guests, with an appearance of a false miracle.

**8. ***{governor of the feast}* The steward, or ruler of the feast, as, Gen. 43:16. Among them it seems his office was only to order the feast, not as a master of the family, but as a steward, for that time and occasion, to dispose of the meat, order the dishes set on the tables, see they were supplied with wine and all necessaries which were guests; to appoint the waiters to take away again, &c.

**9. ***{water that was made wine}* Water converted into wine; by which he showed himself the God and Lord of nature; here doing that in an instant, which he does every year in the vine, by turning water and the moisture of the earth, by slow and insensible degrees, into the juice of the mature grape whence the wine is pressed: he could do these without their ministry, as easily have turned the air in the pots into water, as the water into wine; but he would so do this miracle, that all present might be certain witnesses thereof.

**10. ***{well drunk}* That is, liberally, as Gen. 43:34, for the word *שכר* *shachar* with the Hebrews answering to the Greek word *μεθύειν*, is not always to express intemperance, as Gen. 9:21; 2 Sam. 11:13, but sometimes to signify, only a liberal use of wine without excess, as here, and in the forecited place, Gen. 43:34. See on Luke 1:15.

**11. ***{This beginning of miracles}* Christ adorned this wedding with his presence and first miracle, among those innumerable miracles which Christ wrought in the days of his flesh, whereof many are recorded by the evangelists, this of his turning water into wine at the wedding in Cana of Galilee, was the first.

*{and manifested}* He wrought this miracle so evidently, that his divine power appeared therein.

*{disciples believed on him}* For the end of miracles, is to bring us by faith to Christ: whereas lying miracles carrying men away from Christ into superstition, are Satan’s delusions, 2 Thess. 2:9, 10, 11.

**12. ***{Capernaum}* A rich city on the seacoast, in the borders of Zabulon and Nephtali, where Christ dwelt when he left Nazareth, Matt. 4:13.

*{and his brethren}* That is, his kinsmen, whom the Hebrews called brethren, Gen. 13:8 and 14:14; Matt. 13:55.

**13. ***{Jews’ Passover was at hand}* See Exod. 12:2 and 13:3 and 23:14, 15, and the moral thereof.

**14. ***{and found}* Christ appointed to purge the temple, Mal. 3:1, 2, 3, presently repairs thereto: this was the first Passover after Christ’s baptism; and therefore this is not the same history which is recorded, Matt. 21:12; Mark 11:15; Luke 19:45, that was when his passion drew near.

*{in the temple}* In the outward court of the temple.

*{sold oxen}* They that dwelt far from Jerusalem, were by the law, to make their offerings into money, and to furnish themselves there, Deut. 14:25, 26, but not in the temple, which was the house of God, or the house of prayer.

*{changers of money sitting}* To fit all strangers with exchange of moneys there current; to buy sacrifices.

**15. ***{of small cords}* Probably, those which bound the oxen and sheep, brought to the altar to be offered (as it is written, Ps. 118:27, *bind the sacrifice with cords, even unto the horns of the altar*) which done, probably they were cast by; so Christ might take up some of these, of which he made a scourge to drive out the sheep and oxen, that they might not buy and sell in the temple.

**16. ***{make not my Father’s house an house of, &c.}* He blames them not the sale of sheep and oxen and doves (the law allowed that) but that they sold them within the court of the temple, and so made it a house of merchandise: that he condemns: because, that through it, it was not lawful so much as to bear a burden, Mark 11:16. If Christ thus purged his temple from elsewhere lawful merchandise and profanation, when yet he well knew it should shortly after be utterly demolished; if he would not have them buy and sell there, those things without which they could not then serve him according to his own law; those things which doubtless many bought with a sincere purpose for the service of God; with what scorpions will he at last revenge the profanations of those houses of prayer, which have been so shamefully abused among us?

**17. ***{The zeal of thy house}* Ps. 69:9. Zeal is here taken for the heat of indignation conceived at anything unworthily done or attempted, against those we love: as also 2 Cor. 7:11. Sometimes it imports fervent affection; as Col. 4:13. Sometimes good intention, though without knowledge or right level, Rom. 10:2; Phil. 3:6. Sometimes envy, ch. 7:9 and 13:45 and 17:5. Sometimes for emulation, Gal. 5:20. So Clemens Romanus, *ep. ad Cor.* p. 6, 7, says *ζῆλος* *καὶ* *φθόνος*, zeal and envy (that is, anger and envy) were the cause of Cain’s killing his brother; no such zeal had Christ, who had no sin: but a holy zeal to the service and honor of God his Father.

*{eaten me up}* As it were a secret fire feeding on my thoughts, so that it will not suffer me to be mindful of myself: as Ps. 102:4 and 119:139.

**18. ***{that thou dost these things}* Whereby it may appear, that you have divine authority, to do and say such extraordinary things.

**19. ***{destroy this temple}* See Matt. 26:61. If you destroy, or when you destroy: thus he shows his divine prescience, foretelling of his passion: these things he spoke, not plainly, because he knew they would not have believed, the disciples could not yet, how much less the vulgar?

**20. ***{Forty and six years was this temple in building}* This was not the temple built by Solomon, 1 Kings 6:37; 2 Chron. 3:1, that was destroyed by the Chaldeans, 2 Chron. 36:19, but the second temple built after their return from Babylon, Ezra 4. So much hindered, that it was forty-six years in hand. See hereof Is. Casaubon, *exercit.* 13, n. 38.

**21. ***{of the temple of his body}* That is, his human body, the temple of the Godhead, Col. 2:9.

**22. ***{his disciples remembered, &c.}* For then he opened their understanding, that they might know what he had said and done, Luke 24:45. Thus God’s word (which Christ compared to seed sowed, Matt. 13) sometimes lies long (as under frozen clods) not appearing, but as dead and lost; yet in God’s good time it is quickened, and brought to memory again, and the use and fruits thereof show an admirable life of the Holy Ghost working thereon.

**23. ***{many believed in his name}* With a temporal faith, such as usually is theirs, who depend on miracles: it was no sound or justifying faith; which Christ would not trust.

**24. ***{did not commit himself unto them}* He trusted them not; neither with the secret mysteries of his doctrine, as Chrysostom, Augustine, and Theophylact conceive; nor with his own sacred person: he did not securely converse with them, nor did he trust himself with them, as he did with his disciples, and well-known friends, for he knew from the beginning, how they would at last betray him, and deliver him into the hands of sinners to be crucified; which though he willingly permitted in the appointed time, yet having many things, as to his manifestation to Israel, to perform and accomplish, he declined and avoided as importune until that time was come, and all things in their time and order, duly and really finished.

*{he knew all men}* What they had in the secret of their heart; what they had been, were and would be, ch. 13:18 and 7:71.

**25. ***{needed not that any}* Christ being the searcher of all hearts, had no need that any should inform him concerning man.

## CHAPTER 3

**1. ***{Pharisees}* See on ch. 1:24.

*{a ruler of the Jews}* Either one of their Sanhedrin or great council; or one of the rulers of their synagogue, the former sense, from ch. 7:45, 50, appears most probable.

**2. ***{by night}* For fear of the Jews, John 19:38. See ch. 9:22.

*{Rabbi}* Master, or teacher. See on ch. 1:38.

*{come from God}* Divinely, and extraordinarily sent to teach us.

**3. ***{born again}* Or, *from above*, regenerated by the Holy Ghost, crucifying the old man; mortifying and abolishing the carnal affections, and renewing a man to holiness of life; that is to say, the whole man, body and soul: for as the whole man from his natural birth is corrupted, so must the whole man in his regeneration be renewed, 1 Thess. 5:23. Though that renovation, be not so completed, in this mortal life, but that there are in the best men, some remains of the old Adam. See Gal. 5:17; Rom. 7:24, yet, such is the mercy of God toward his, that whatsoever sinners were before their regeneration, yet therein all is cleansed, and so washed away, that, it shall not be imputed to them to condemnation. See 1 Cor. 6:11. This regeneration may be discerned by the fruits and effects thereof. See Gal. 5:19, 20–22, 23. First then here note, that the most noble birth, or of the most holy parents, without new birth, avails nothing toward salvation; no not for them, who could truly say, *we be Abraham’s seed*, John 8:33; Matt. 3:9. See 1 Cor. 1:26. Secondly, seeing regeneration is of the whole man, content not yourself when you find in you a mortification of some evil affections, which possibly are but changed for others as bad, as lust of youth for malice or avarice of age, &c. Neither will Agrippa’s—*half persuaded to be a Christian*, Acts 26:28, do you any good; God will not accept any parcel-sanctity; every part and faculty of man needs repair. Thirdly, it must incite us to pray, and use God’s ordinance for the evidence of our new birth, without which we cannot see, nor enter into God’s kingdom: God’s word is the immortal seed, 1 Pet. 1:23, 25, the sincere milk, by which if we grow (1 Pet. 2:1), our regeneration will appear; for which, we must lay aside *all malice, and all guile, hypocrisies, envies, and evil speakings*.

*{see the kingdom of God}* That is, enter into it, as verse 5, or understand the mysteries of eternal life, and God’s kingdom, of grace here, and glory in the world to come. See 1 Cor. 2:14; 1 Cor. 15:50.

**4. ***{How can a man}* To examine spiritual mysteries by carnal reason, is a main let to the understanding thereof.

**5. ***{of water}* Of baptism the sacrament of regeneration, which is the ordinary way into the church and kingdom of God, though his grace be not tied to external means: and it is the contempt, not the privation of the sacrament which condemns. See Titus 3:5.

*{spirit}* Which gives the inward effect of baptism.

*{kingdom of God}* Of grace here, and glory in the life to come. See on verse 6.

**6. ***{born of the flesh}* The corrupted nature of man, (opposite to regeneration) concerning which the apostle says, *it cannot inherit the kingdom of God*, 1 Cor. 15:50. The whole sense is; no man can know the mysteries of the kingdom of grace, without a regenerate mind; and therefore no man can be saved without regeneration and renovation of the whole man: we are born aliens to the kingdom of God (Eph. 2:1, &c., 12, 13) and mere enemies (Rom. 5:10) until we are born again of water and the Holy Ghost; hence is it, that the apostle requires, *a new man, a new creature*, 1 Cor. 5:7; 2 Cor. 5:17; Gal. 6:15; Eph. 4:24; Col. 3:10. The unregenerate man perceives not the things of the Spirit of God, neither can he, because *they are spiritually discerned*, 1 Cor. 2:14. The carnal man, says Theophylact (in Luke 14), *ὁ* *μηδὲν* *ὑπὲρ* *φύσιν* *πιστεύων*, he believes nothing supernatural: now as the creature can be but once properly created; a man can be but once born; so neither can he be more than once regenerate: *quando enim uterus non potest repeti, sic nec baptismus iterari*, says Prosper.

*{is flesh}* Is carnal, subject to sin and death: understand herewith, the wisdom, will and affection of the natural man. See Rom. 7:14, &c. and 8:7. Why of holy parents, carnal sons are born, see August. to. 7, part 2, *de nuptiis & concup.* c. 18. Of the necessity of regeneration, see Prosp. *contra Collator.* c. 18.

*{is spirit}* Is spiritual: subject to the Spirit, sanctifying man, and styling him spiritual; notwithstanding the remains of sin striving against the Spirit, and those works of the flesh, like the wild fruits growing on the suckers of the stock, on which the adopted branches are engrafted, bearing fruits of regeneration.

**7. ***{born again}* Or, *from above*.

**8. ***{wind bloweth where it listeth}* That which the Hebrews call *רוח* *ruach* (with them a *πολυώνυμον*) the Greeks express by the word *πνεῦμα*, signifying among other things wind, or a blast of wind: so Aristot. *de mundo*, Thucid. l. 4, and other authors use it: in Scripture it often signifies the Holy Ghost; the meaning here is; as the power of God is perceived in the wind moving the air, though we cannot see it; so is it in the changing and renewing of us, though the manner be hid from us: *where it listeth*: signifies, without any restraint by the power of man: this manner of speaking is taken from creatures, voluntarily moving themselves, as they say, *libero ac vago impetu*.

**9. ***{How can these things be?}* Christ had preached this point unto him, that except a man be regenerate, he cannot see the kingdom of God; and Nicodemus is now an example of the same: being so great an example of the same: being so great and learned a scholar, yet being not regenerate, he could not understand the very rudiments of that kingdom.

**10. ***{and knowest not these things}* These words are quick and reprehensory: as if he said, it is a foul shame for a professed teacher of Israel, to be ignorant in the very fundamentals of religion; the more, seeing this point is laid down in many places of Scripture, Jer. 31:19; Ezek. 11:12 and 36:26; Ps. 51:10. Circumcision signified it: it is the end of all the law, purity of heart, and sanctimony of life; it is that which puts difference between sinful worldlings, and the holy sons of God. Now, the otherwise learned Nicodemus knew not these things, because they are no otherwise than spiritually discerned, 1 Cor. 2:14.

**11. ***{Verily, verily}* *Ἀμήν*, *ἀμήν*, the word is from the Hebrew *אמן* *aman, verax, verus, fidelis*: and *אמן* *amen*, as much as, so be it; they used it as a ratification of their prayers, Jer. 11:5, and desiring the fulfilling thereof; so Deut. 27:15, *all the people shall say* *אמן* *amen*. 70. *γένοιτο*, *let it come to pass*, or be so; sometimes it was doubled to the more vehement confirmation or asseveration, as Num. 5:22, *the woman shall say* *אמן* *אמן* *amen, amen*; hence in the primitive church after prayers, and some other recitations in their assemblies, the people answered, *Amen*, 1 Cor. 14:16. *how shall he say, τὸ Ἀμήν?* See Rev. 1:7, *ναὶ* *ἀμήν*, *yea, so be it*. And so our Savior often used it doubled, for a vehement asseveration; as if he said, *faithfully, truly*, or *assuredly it is so*.

*{we speak that we do know}* In that Christ says *οἴδαμεν*, *we know, &c.*, the cause and sense is, I and all the prophets (which spoke by my Spirit, and of me) well know what we speak and teach; thus joins he with their ministry; whence appears their error, who would have the authority of Scriptures to depend on the church: that were to subject the truth of Christ to the examination and approbation of men.

*{that we have seen}* This is to express the certainty of the Gospel: because, with us the sense of seeing is most certain; we use to say we have seen a thing, when we will affirm a certainty of knowledge.

*{ye receive not our witness}* That is, few of you receive it. So ch. 1:11, his own received him not; that is, few of them received: it has been the misery of all times: *Lord, who received our report?* Isaiah 53:1. Let us not think the worse of truth, because it has few sincere followers; man’s unbelief cannot make void the truth of God, Rom. 3:3. Let them take heed, who out of opinion of new light, contemn the old invariable truth of Christ; who will be teachers of the law, and yet understand not *what they speak, neither whereof they affirm*, 1 Tim. 1:7. It is but just with God to send them, to their damnation, strong *delusions, that they shall believe lies*, who receive not the *love of the truth*, that they might be saved, 2 Thess. 2:10, 11. Read it in Ahab’s example, 1 Kings 22:20, &c.

**12. ***{earthly things}* Natural things, as concerning the wind: or in a plain and common manner, as he delivered his heavenly doctrine: for it is most true which Chrysostom notes, hom. 26, in regard of his hearers’ weakness, he attempers to them.

**13. ***{came down from heaven}* By assumption of the human nature, ch. 1:14.

*{the Son of man}* Christ, God and man: Son of man is often put for man, Ps. 8:4; Isa. 51:12.

*{which is in heaven}* Not because the human substance of Christ was diffused to a ubiquity; but because one and the same Son of God, and Son of man, very God of his Father, and truly man by the assumption of our flesh and blood, though according to his true humanity, *localiter tunc esset in terrâ*, he was then locally on earth, yet according to his deity, which cannot be contained in place, he filled heaven and earth; and was then in heaven, in respect thereof, whose properties are attributed to the person of Christ, consisting of those two united natures; hereof, see Fulgent. *de person. Christi ad Thrasimund.* l. 2.

**14. ***{as Moses lifted up the serpent}* Num. 21:6. As the brazen serpent lifted up, had the true form of a serpent, but not the venom; so had Christ the true form and nature, but not the sin of man: that serpent was a sign of grace, and a remedy against evil, that all who were stung with fiery serpents, might see him, and be healed; so was Christ lifted upon the cross, ch. 12:32, and is now lifted up in the word and sacraments (Isa. 49:22; Gal. 3:1) that the faithful may look into heaven after him, and be healed: this he did not openly speak, but remitted his hearers to the old type, as Chrysostom noted, hom. 26, to show the admirable consent between the Old and New Testament; and to intimate, that he went not unwillingly to his passion: also to help the faith of the weak: if any doubt, how can they who believe in Jesus crucified, be saved? Let him consider how he that but looked on the similitude of a serpent, was healed; how much more shall they, who with true faith look upon Jesus, the life, through the veil of his crucified flesh? There the serpent stung, and the serpent healed; here death (whose sting is sin, 1 Cor. 15) in the first Adam, destroyed: here the death of the second Adam Christ saved and gave us life.

**15. ***{whosoever believeth on him}* Whosoever; without respect of persons (Acts 10:34, 35), believes that Jesus Christ, the true Messias, and Savior of the elect, died for our sins and rose again for our justification, Rom. 4:25, and in assurance of the truth of God’s promises promised in the Gospel, apprehends his particular interest and propriety therein, as ch. 20:28.

**16. ***{For God so loved, &c.}* In this verse Christ shows these four concurrent causes of our redemption and salvation. 1. The principal, first, impulsive; the love of God, which can have no cause but itself. 2. The material, or price of our redemption; *his only begotten Son*. 3. The instrumental faith in Christ; *that whosoever believeth in him*. 4. The final; *eternal life*.

*{the world}* That is, mankind, for Christ speaks not here of that common love of God, whereby he wills the good of conservation to the creature; so he loves all the creatures; but of his special love, whereby he wills man should be saved by Christ, and he is truly said to love the world, because they whom he loves to eternal life are in the world, a part of the world, and gathered by his word, and Holy Spirit into the body of the church, out of all ages and parts of the world. God loved all that he made: he loves not sin, which is neither in him, nor of him; he loved a sinning world (for when we were enemies, he reconciled us, Rom. 5:10) but not the sins of the world: if we speak of God’s peculiar love which is to eternal life; we must understand the elect only, opposed to that part thereof, concerning which it is said; *the whole world lieth in wickedness*, 1 John 5:19, and, *I pray not for the world*, ch. 17:9. Christ prayed for those whom he loved to salvation; that is, for the elect only, *I pray for them*, I pray not for the world, for the elect are in the world, not of the world (ch. 17:16), that is, of that party which lies in sin and unbelief; so two sorts of men are intimated, *sub uno mundi nomine*; under this one name *world* (says Prosper) *sicut enim dicitur mundus in impiis, ita dicitur mundus in sanctis*—the wicked are called the world, and here the saints are called the *world which God loveth*. God loves us, such as we shall be by his gift, *non quales sumus nostro merito*. Augustin. *de C. D.* l. 17, c. 4, not such as we are by our desert; he loved, *quod fecerat & elegerat in filio*, says the same, tr. 110, in John 17, that which he had made, and chosen in his Son; out of whom he loves no man to eternal life: a prince may be truly said to love his subjects, specially then, when he punishes the perverse, rebellious, and seditious among them; and a physician his patient, specially when he most endeavors to correct the corrupt and malignant humors which disaffect him: so neither can it prejudice the love of God that he cuts off and condemns reprobates, when with severe corrections, he fetches in, amends, and saves those whom he loved and elected from eternity: thus then, the Creator, and Lord of lords, loved the world; that is wretched mankind dispersed, upon the face of the earth, without difference of nation, dignity, sex, or age: all the world with his common love (of which all have some taste), the elect only, according to the effect thereof, his purpose of saving them in Christ. See more concerning the acceptation of the word (world), Augustin. to. 7, *Contr. Jul. Pelag.* l. 6, c. 1.

**17. ***{For God sent not}* Ch. 12:47. God gave, and sent his Son by his incarnation: he confirms that which he said, *should not perish*, verse 16, by declaring the end of his present coming into the world.

*{to condemn the world}* Christ’s coming condemns not, but unbelief and contempt of Christ, ch. 9:39 and 12:47, he came not now to judge (that shall be at his second coming) but to propose terms of peace, and mercy; that they might believe and be saved.

*{that all the world through him, &c.}* Not only Jews, but Gentiles also, as many as should believe in him.

**18. ***{Is condemned already}* By the ancient sentence, Gen. 3:19 and 2:17; Gal. 3:10, which must needs stand, except it be reversed by Christ apprehended by faith; so is the believer absolved and pardoned; but the unbeliever (by nature the child of wrath, dead in trespasses and sins, Eph. 2:1) stands already condemned, as Chrysostom says, *κἂν* *μὴ* *τῷ* *πράγματι* *τέως*, *ἀλλὰ* *τῇ* *ψήφῳ*, if not actually and declaratively, yet in the (universal) sentence, on Adam; and in respect of his own actual sins; for whosoever sins, is guilty before God, and so under punishment.

*{in the name of, &c.}* That is, in Christ. See ch. 1:12. In the name of, &c., here imports, in Christ, named and being so, preached and declared to be the Son of God, he that believes not in sacred name, cannot but perish, because there is no other name given under heaven, whereby we must be saved, Acts 4:12, and indeed he that believes not in Christ, *hath made him a liar, because he believeth not the record that God gave of his Son*, 1 John 5:10; Matt. 3:17; 2 Pet. 1:17, 18; Matt. 17:5.

*{the only begotten}* All the saints are God’s children by adoption in Christ; but he is the only begotten Son, by nature. See on ch. 1:14.

**19. ***{The condemnation}* The cause of condemnation.

*{their deeds were evil}* Man’s wickedness is the cause of his rejecting the Gospel of Christ (which as a light discovers it, and convinces sinners) and his neglecting and hating the same either in the word of truth, or ministry thereof: so Ahab, sold unto sin, hated Micaiah, 1 Kings 22, and incestuous Herodias John Baptist.

**20. ***{Hateth the light}* This is a proverbial speech, which Christ accommodates to his present purpose which was to show the immediate cause of the Jews’ unbelief, rejection of the Gospel, and their not coming to Christ, the light, which was their sin and incorrigible wickedness, sin would be concealed in darkness; it abhors nothing more than the light which discovers its deformity, which is such as cannot hurt, except it be loved, nor possibly be loved, if clearly seen. Ahab took Elijah for his enemy, 1 Kings 21:20, because he discovered and reproved his sin: still truth begets us enemies, the word of God to the wicked, is as a true glass to the ugly; you offer an odious affront if you show it them.

*{reproved}* Or, *discovered*, the sinner’s own conscience reproves him, and therefore loves the darkness, that not only others might not see his sins (in which he is resolved to live) but that himself might not know them, that he might more securely enjoy the pleasures thereof for a season.

**21. ***{Doeth the truth}* That does sincerely and holily.

*{wrought in God}* As they do, who set God only before their eyes, and follow the rule of his word: *hoc autem in Deo facimus, quod Deo in nobis faciente, datur nobis ut faciamus*. Fulgent. *de praed. ad Monim.* l. 1. And we do this in God; which, God working in us, gives us ability to do.

**22. ***{The land of Judaea}* Into the territories of Judea: from Jerusalem into the villages, and places near.
