*{and baptized}* By his disciples’ ministry. See ch. 4:2.

**23. ***{baptizing in Aenon}* A town in the tribe of Manasse, near Jordan (there was another of this name near Damascus, Ezek. 47:17), and near Scythopolis on the other side of Jordan (see Is. Casaubon, *Exercit.* 13, n. 42), and Salim. See Jerome, *de loc. Hebr.* at the end.

**24. ***{John was not yet cast into prison}* See Matt. 14:3, &c.; Mark 6:17, &c.; Luke 3:19.

**25. ***{And the Jews}* Chrysostom reads *μετὰ* *Ἰουδαίου*—with a Jew: so the Syriac, *with a certain Jew*, very likely that the controversy began with one, and after grew more general? So are national quarrels oftentimes kindled, by one or some few, which afterward are, from small sparks, fomented and blown up into masterless flames.

*{about purifying}* How they might be purified before God, which the washings under the law did, and baptism now does represent.

**26. ***{was with thee beyond Jordan}* They were affected with perverse zeal and emulation, fearing that their master’s credit should be impaired by Christ.

*{to whom thou bearest witness}* Ch. 1:7, 34. They come with this complaint, that all the people now ran after him whom their master baptized; as fearing an eclipse of their master’s honor, by his interposition, to whom (it seemed to them) their master had lent his light.

**27. ***{A man can receive nothing}* Or, *take unto himself*; nothing, no gift: no man ought to usurp a ministry, which God has not given him, Heb. 5:4, as the Syriac interpreter has it, *ex voluntate sui ipsius*, *of his own will*; or *at his own pleasure*, nor can be fit for it, except God give the ability, 2 Cor. 3:6.

**28. ***{I am not the Christ}* Ch. 1:20. See Luke 3:15, 16, and John 1:20, &c.

**29. ***{is fulfilled}* In that he heard the voice of Christ the bridegroom speaking to his spouse the church: it is as if he said, now my work is done; my office fulfilled; whereof I rejoice. I have done my part and rejoice in the desired effect.

**30. ***{He must increase}* I came to prepare his way, and to manifest himself to Israel: the confluence of people to hear and follow him is the end of my proclaiming him, and testifying concerning him, he must be exalted and all the people shall follow him, I must be obscured; as a star at the rising of the sun.

**31. ***{Is earthly}* Nothing but man, mere dregs in comparison with Christ.

*{and speaketh of the earth}* Savors not the things of God, but of men: as Matt. 16:23, his doctrine is grounded on earthly or carnal principles: such are we all by nature, and cannot receive, or apprehend the things of the Spirit of God, 1 Cor. 2:14, until we are illuminated by the Spirit of adoption and regeneration.

**32. ***{No man receiveth his testimony}* That is, as Chrysostom interprets *οὐδεὶς*, very few believe in Christ, concerning whom John bore witness, that he is the Son of God, if he had simply meant, none; why should he have added, *he that receiveth his testimony hath sealed*, &c., verse 33, and how should we think that the disciples and others were not herein concerned? But this he says, to show his disciples, why they, and the other Jews yet believed not, because Christ was heavenly and they earthly.

**33. ***{He that hath received his testimony}* That is, *διδασκαλίαν* *αὐτοῦ*; Theophylact, the doctrine of the Gospel.

*{hath set to his seal}* See Rom. 3:4. Theophylact interprets it by *ἔδειξεν*, and *ἐβεβαίωσεν*, he has demonstrated, and confirmed, that God is true; he has so professed it, as if he had set to his seal for the more certain confirmation of his writing, or that which is given under his hand.

**34. ***{He whom God hath sent}* God herein sent his Son, as the sun sends out his rays to the earth; in that they are not in their emission divided from their fountain; *ita de spiritu spiritus, & de deo deus*, says Tertullian, *Apol.* cap. 21, so is he Spirit of Spirit, and God of God, that there is but one truth, as one God, so that he who believes him that is sent of God believes God; as on the contrary, he that believes not in him that is sent, believes not God who sent him. See Luke 10:16.

*{speaketh the words of God}* This shows a reason why they should believe him, because he speaks only the words of God; which is the only ground of faith in which there can be no falsehood; opinion may be on probabilities; but faith is on certainties, because they are revealed by God, who cannot deceive. See Titus 1:2.

*{not the spirit by measure}* But an unmeasurable fullness, Col. 1:19 and 2:9. He means by Spirit, the gifts and graces of the Holy Ghost: to every one of us is given grace *according to the measure of the gift of Christ*, Eph. 4:7, but his powerful working is, as Chrysostom speaks, *ἀμέτρητος*, *unmeasurable*: for he has *τὸ* *πνεῦμα* *ὁλοκληρόν*, and *οὐσιωδῶς*, the whole Spirit, substantially; the prophets and apostles had, and the faithful ministers have a *measure*; but Christ, the fullness whence all receive grace and truth, has it above all measure. See Rom. 12:6; Eph. 4:7, &c.

**35. ***{The father loveth the son}* Matt. 11:27; 10; Matt. 3:17; Matt. 15:5; 2 Pet. 1:17. The Father loves the Son, with an incomprehensible love, infinitely; his love not being an accident in him, but the essence of him, 1 John 4:8.

*{all things unto his hand}* That is, has given the rule and power over all things in heaven and earth, to him. See Matt. 28:18.

**36. ***{He that believeth, &c.}* Hab. 2:4; 1 John 5:10.

*{hath everlasting life}* The Syriac interpreter has it, *est ei vita, quae est in aeternum*, it seems indeed to express a present and indefeasible interest and possession; not only apprehensible by hope, but even now in being to present sense; for as much as the elect regenerate do now feel the power of the life of Christ in them, and the daily motions of his Spirit working the decay and death of sin, and enabling to some comfortable measure of sanctification. See Gal. 2:20. They by experience know, how Christ is made of God, their wisdom, righteousness, sanctification and redemption, 1 Cor. 1:30, wherein his kingdom is established in them; that is, the kingdom of grace and life eternal, the kingdom of life and glory begun here on earth to be perfected in heaven.

*{shall not see life}* Shall not enjoy; no nor so much as lay an eye upon it. See verse 3 and ch. 8:24, he shall neither be sensible of the life of grace here, nor of the life of glory in the world to come; the Syriac interpreter gives it, *qui non acquiescit filio*: *he that rests not, or depends not on the Son*, in faith for his justification: but seeking righteousness by the works of the law, &c., he shall not be saved.

*{but the wrath of God abideth on him}* For none but those only who are justified by faith in Christ have peace toward God, Rom. 5:1. Out of Christ there can be no reconciliation, by the wrath of God; he means the whole curse of the law, and whatsoever it threatens impenitent sinners. Salvian expresses it well, *de Gub. Dei* l. 1, *ira est autem divinitatis, poena peccati*: God’s anger is the punishment of sin, and as Fulgentius, *de incarn. & grat.*, *ira igitur ejus, nostræ iniquitatis est meritum*, his anger is the desert of our iniquity, he says not God’s anger shall come, but it abides: all are born children of wrath, Eph. 2:1, because in sin: but this is done away in our regeneration, wherein we are reconciled to God in Christ only. See Acts 4:12; Rom. 5:1, but it abides on the impenitent, and unbelievers, because they are not reconciled to God by faith in Christ.

## CHAPTER 4

**1. ***{When therefore the Lord knew}* When Jesus knew; that is, Jesus knowing what the Pharisees heard, and consulted, how they might destroy him, which was the usual result of their consultation concerning him, and his actions which they with an impious diligence observed, that they might have something whereby to calumniate and accuse him.

**2. ***{Jesus himself baptized not}* He baptized inwardly by the Holy Ghost giving the effect of the outward baptism here spoken of: and as Optatus l. 5 says, he baptized indeed; but by the apostles’ hands, *quibus leges baptismatis dederat*, to whom he gave the laws (or rites) of baptism; so Tertul. *de bapt.* c. 21, *semper dicitur is facere cui præministratur*, he is ever said to do a thing, for whose service it is done.

**3. ***{he left the Jews}* To avoid their rage: that mean must be held in our ministry, neither to be discouraged by causeless fear, nor rashly to provoke, or expose ourselves to enemies.

**4. ***{He must needs go}* It was his way from Judea to Galilee, and lay between them.

**5. ***{A city of Samaria}* See on Luke 17:11. Herod, in honor of Augustus Caesar, called it Sebaste, according to the Greek. Jero. *La. s. Epitaph. Paul.* Christ thus leaving the proud and malicious Pharisees, opened the treasures of grace and mercy to the despised Samaritan.

*{Sychar}* Sichem, or Shekem, Gen. 33:18. *שכם* *shechem*. 70. *Συχέμ*. See Gen. 48:22; Josh. 24:32. Some think it called since, Neapolis, Naples, or Naploza. See Is. Casaubon, *exer.* 13.

*{that Jacob gave}* Gen. 33:19 and 48:22. See Gen. 50:24; Josh. 24:32.

**6. ***{Sate thus on the well}* Weary as he was: or, because he was weary, for he traveled with his disciples on foot; and in the form of a servant, which for our sake he had assumed, would have experience of all our infirmities without sin, therefore he hungered, thirsted, was weary, &c.

*{the sixth hour}* About noon: in the heat of the day.

**7. ***{give me to drink}* Thus he that is rich and Lord of all, became poor for us, that he might make us rich, 2 Cor. 8:9. We had lost all right and interest in the creatures, so that we could not so much as (in our own right) take a cup of cold water, but as usurpers: until Christ restored us that interest and right in the creatures: which we forfeited by our attainder in Adam: and thus he asks, who gives to all creatures, Ps. 104:27, as to refresh himself in his weariness and thirst; so specially to give occasion of discourse, for her salvation and many others.

**8. ***{Into the city}* Sychar, verse 5, that they might there be accommodated with meat, or other necessaries which they wanted.

**9. ***{For the Jews have no dealing, &c.}* The Samaritans claimed kindred of the Jews in their prosperity, but in their public afflictions, would never acknowledge them. Joseph. *Antiq. Jud.* l. 11, c. 8. See also l. 9, c. ult. The Jews esteemed them wicked and profane, likewise the Samaritans thought themselves polluted, if they touched a Jew. See 2 Kings 17:6, &c. They mutually hated and despised each other; hence was that blasphemous calumny against Christ, whom the Jews called Samaritan, &c., John 8:48.

**10. ***{gift of God}* That excellent gift specified ch. 3:16, or ch. 7:38, 39, or himself sent to convert her; or the opportunity of salvation hereby offered her.

*{living water}* The graces of the Holy Spirit, which shall never fail. See ch. 7:39; Jer. 2:13; Joel 3:18; Zech. 13:1. Cyril understands here, the love of God, and the quickening gift of the Spirit; so Theophylact, *τὴν* *χάριν* *τοῦ* *ἁγίου* *πνεύματος*, the grace of the Holy Spirit; which fails not, but brings to eternal life.

**11. ***{Nothing to draw with}* And the Jews and Samaritans held it a pollution, to use the vessels of each other: this it seems, she spoke, as despising and contradicting that which he said.

**12. ***{Art thou greater, &c.}* As if she had said; whom make you yourself? Jacob that great patriarch was content with this water, for himself and his family; what better water speak you of?

*{than our father Jacob?}* Thus these mixed and degenerate people bragged (see 2 Kings 17:24). Thus hypocrites ever boast of their fathers and ancestors. See ch. 8.

**13. ***{whosoever drinketh of this, &c.}* As if he said, hence you may gather whether I am greater than Jacob or not, if you understand the difference between that mystical and spiritual water which shall nourish to eternity (which Jacob could not give) and this which Jacob gave his sons, which can only quench a present thirst, but not free you from a future.

**14. ***{The water that I shall give him}* The grace and Spirit of regeneration, of which v. 10 and ch. 7:38, 39.

*{shall never thirst}* It shall so refresh the weary soul, that all desire of good shall be satisfied, in the presence of God: see Ps. 37:8, 9; Isa. 49:10.

**15. ***{Give me this water}* It is likely that she perceived that he spoke of spiritual water, but under the notion of a Jew, she was disposed to slight all that he spoke: so hard it is to believe their word, of whose authority we are not well persuaded; probably she spoke this ironically; this also is an example of that which Christ said, ch. 3:3, *except a man be born again, he cannot see the kingdom of God*.

**16. ***{Go, call thy husband}* Christ having a while patiently borne with her perverse answers, will now smite her conscience with the sense of her sin; and his Divine Majesty before whom she deported herself so securely and audaciously: so are overconfident sinners to be wounded that they may be healed, she knew that she lived in sin, yet her conscience could not awake, until a divine hand seemed to touch the wounds thereof: so 2 Sam. 12:13. Here also the tender mercy of Christ is to be observed: he said not, go you adulteress, and call your partner in sin, your pretended husband, that you may hear your dooms (as 1 Cor. 6:9, 10): but he mildly styles him husband, whom he knew to be an adulterer, that she might neither presume any longer to contest with the searcher of hearts, nor despair of his mercy, whose lenity in reproving spoke him merciful to cure and amend; not busy, or malicious to find fault, or accuse.

**17. ***{I have no husband}* Thus securely dallies she in so great an interest as her own salvation; it is hard for sinners (not only asleep, but) dead in sins and transgressions, to awake at the first call of God and to acknowledge his voice therein: she supposing that she talked with mere man, thought with her womanish artifices to elude him, denying her matrimony, and dissembling her adultery; but no captions or fallacies can deceive God.

*{thou hast said well}* Christ tells her plainly of her faults (who could not before understand what he said) in mentioning the fifth man, whom she now had in place of a husband, but truly an adulterer.

**19. ***{I perceive that thou art a Prophet}* The mentioning of her sin, smites her with a greater reverence of Christ, as of a prophet, who could know those errors of her former life.

**20. ***{in this mountain}* Mount Garizim, near Shekem, Judg. 9:7, in which Sanballat, by Alexander of Macedon his leave, built them a temple. Joseph. *antiq.* l. 11, c. 8. See Is. Casaubon, *exer.* 13.

*{Jerusalem is the place, &c.}* See Deut. 12:5, 6; 1 Kings 8:29; 2 Kings 21:4; 1 Kings 14:21. Thus would she divert the discourse concerning her sin and shame, by embarking in a national controversy, which had long been handed between the Jews and Samaritans.

**21. ***{nor at Jerusalem}* The public worship of God shall be no more restrained to that place, which was appointed thereto for a time, to exercise their faith in expectation of Christ in whom alone we can appear acceptable to God. See Mal. 1:11; 1 Tim. 2:8; Eph. 2:14, 15.

**22. ***{Ye worship ye know not what}* See 2 Kings 17:29, &c. This Christ seems to speak that he might check her insolency: as if he had said; you make comparisons with the Israel of God, who worship you know not what: what skills it, where they worship, who are ignorant both of the object and manner of the true worship? Devotion born of ignorance, and blind obedience, are strangers in the kingdom of God, *the father of lights*, James 1:17. Our Savior says, John 17:3, *this is life eternal, that they might know thee, the only true God, and Jesus Christ whom thou hast sent*; it is true concerning the heavenly Jerusalem, which was said of the fort of Sion, 2 Sam. 5:8, *the blind and the lame shall not come into the house*: the ignorant, who know not Christ, the true light, John 1:9, and such as halt between God and Baal, 1 Kings 18:21. Therefore as God began his work of creation, Gen. 1:3, with a, *let there be light*; so begins he the admirable work of our regeneration, by his Spirit of illumination, calling us out of darkness, into his marvelous light, 1 Pet. 2:9. See Rom. 10:14, 15, 17.

*{for salvation is of the Jews}* He seems to allude to Isa. 2:3. The meaning is, that the doctrine of saving health, is that which the prophets have taught.

**23. ***{in spirit and in truth}* Spirit is here set in opposition to ceremonies, which are called carnal, Heb. 7:16, which prefigured that which Christ performed: he requires now (says Euthymius) *non corporaliter, sed spiritualiter immolantes*, those that sacrifice not bodily, but spiritually; remission of sins being accomplished in Christ’s one oblation of himself once offered, there remains no more sacrifice for the sins of the living or the dead. See Heb. 10:18. There remains still spiritual sacrifice of prayer and praises by us to be offered, 1 Pet. 2:5; Rev. 8:4, but no more bodily, proper or external sacrifice: he will be worshiped now, not in the figure (as under the law) but in the truth prefigured: and herein he secretly taxes the Samaritans’ blind errors, for that they held no certainty from God’s word, neither was their pretended zeal and devotion according to truth and knowledge, both which are fundamental to the acts of religion. See Isa. 8:20. And in this comparison, Christ shows the preeminence of the Jewish religion, grounded then on God’s infallible word, and the vanity of the Samaritan superstition and will-worship, built on traditions, and opinions of men, which are ever uncertain: for ignorance of the truth, as Lactantius well notes, makes *incertam, labentemque sententiam*, an uncertain, and slippery assertion: will-worship is no religious act; neither are good intentions enough; we must be certain of God’s revealed will, to direct us in his worship, otherwise we cannot do it in faith.

**24. ***{God is a spirit}* 2 Cor. 3:17. These words show the reason of that he last spoke concerning spiritual adoration: God requires adoration in spirit and truth, because himself is a Spirit, and truth: God is an incorporeal substance, invisible, incomprehensible, and he is the *veritas prima*, the *light, in whom is no darkness*, 1 John 1:5. Therefore he requires such adoration as agrees with his own nature: he commanded bodily worship to Israel in the ceremonial law, which was to be as a schoolmaster, to bring them to Christ, whom the ceremonies thereof prefigured, Gal. 3:24—*until the time appointed of the father*, Gal. 4:2, and he confined them to Jerusalem, and the temple there; but now in the fullness of time, Christ shows that his worship should no longer be confined to Jerusalem, than all things therein prefigured Christ, and the work of man’s redemption by him to be wrought, should be accomplished, the Jews therefore, though the peculiar people of God, and in their sacrifices, doing those bodily things, which God for a time commanded them to do, except they, by the spiritual eye of faith, looked to Christ the end of the law, neither worshiped God aright, neither were by those legal rites cleansed from their sins, *for it is not possible that the blood of bulls and goats, should take away sins*, Hebrews 10:4. They therefore who to this day adhere to the antiquated ceremonies of the law, not believing in Christ, who did indeed come in the fullness of time, and fulfill all things concerning man’s redemption therein prefigured, cleave to a killing letter and ministration of condemnation.

*{must worship him in spirit}* This shows wherein God’s service principally consists; excluding the Jewish antiquated ceremonies, but not that bodily worship which is requisite, either for our acknowledgment of God before men, or order in his public worship, or the stirring up our own affections in private.

**25. ***{which is called Christ}* These words (omitted by the Syriac) seem to be the evangelist’s interpretation of the name Messias: though the Samaritan religion was very impure, confused, and mingled with sundry errors, yet held they this principle concerning the coming of the Messias: so manifest was this light, that it shined to them that sate in darkness, the very heathens, the Samaritans out of Moses’ writings, Deut. 18:18, the impious Balaam, Num. 24:17, and the devil himself acknowledged some sense thereof: it is probable that this woman understanding by the speech of Christ, that some extraordinary change of the church, and ecclesiastical state was nigh, presently thought of the Messias; the rather, because the marks of his coming, specially the departure of the scepter from Judah appeared to the world: in her referring the certainty of all things in religious worship to the magistery of Christ, preferring him before Moses and all the prophets, she is a good precedent, where we may learn in him to look for all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge, Col. 2:3, and to hear him, Matt. 17:5.

**26. ***{I am he}* When the Jews pressed him, saying, *how long doest thou make us doubt* (or hold us in suspense)? *If thou be the Christ, tell us plainly*, ch. 10:24, he answered not so positively; but here he manifests himself: he that saw all hearts knew that the Jews asked not to believe and be saved, but captiously, and to find some calumny against him; and that this present hearer should convert, and be a means of the conversion of many others, when she should know who it was that talked with her. Christ is never wanting, where he knows the revelation of himself will profit to salvation, ch. 6:64. Where his Gospel is hid, it is hid to them that perish, 2 Cor. 4:4.

**27. ***{Upon this}* Even as he spoke these words.

*{came his disciples}* From Sychar, verse 5, 8, into which they went to buy meat: thus long by God’s providence they were absent, but so soon as he had declared himself to be Christ, they came; lest out of zeal, not yet well informed, they might (as sometimes they endeavored, Matt. 19:13, 14; Mark 10:13, &c.) hinder a work of mercy.

**28. ***{left her waterpot}* Having a taste of those waters of life of which Christ spoke, she quickly forgot her thirst and secular necessity, which brought her out to Jacob’s well; and hasted into the city, to draw others to Christ: when our affections are indeed taken up to things above (Col. 3:1, &c.), our worldly cares, in a great measure, fall off like Elijah’s mantle when he was taken up to heaven, 2 Kings 2:13. See Ps. 69:9 and 102:4; Matt. 17:4; Matt. 4:20, 22; Matt. 16:27; Job 23:12; Ps. 19:10 and 119:127.

**29. ***{Come see a man}* Such is the excellency of heavenly treasures (ever most diffusive of themselves) if one find them, there is no rest until he communicate the same to others: if one find any secular treasure, he will carefully conceal it; because the more communicate therein, the less must every sharer have: but the more by you are brought to Christ, the more you have of that divine riches. She says not, *go out and see a man, &c.*, but *come and see*: the seed of this knowledge cannot lie dead in our hearts. See Ps. 116:10; 2 Cor. 4:13. It is like the widow’s oil, 2 Kings 4:5, 6, increased by pouring out: they have but a doubtful share in Christ, who pretending to send others to him, will not go themselves: nor does she take upon her to define, or teach; she only leads them to hear Christ, contented to persuade them to see what she had found.

*{all things that ever I did}* My great and many sins: that is, *multa quae erant occulta*, Euthymius: many things, which were so reserved, that I am confident, he could have told me all; thus she seems, not to spare her own reputation, that she might thereby bring others to Christ. See 1 Tim. 1:15; Acts 22:3; 1 Cor. 15:9; Gal. 1:13.

**30. ***{went out of the city}* Out of Sychar, verse 5. It was by the instinct of God’s Spirit, that they came, therefore the vility of the reporter hindered them not: we ought not to have the Gospel in respect of persons, nor so much to regard, who speaks, as what is spoken: they have little affection to believe in Christ, who are so intent to the messenger’s personal failings, that they thereby chiefly value their message: these do not object her former sins, or infamy, because they were willing by any means to be led to Christ: a contrary mind was in those who said to the man that had been blind, John 9:34, *thou art altogether born in sin, and dost thou teach us?*

**31. ***{Master eat}* Of these things which we have bought. This they said because Christ (subject to human infirmities and necessities for the time assumed) was weary with his journey, ver. 6, and it was now about noon: therefore they out of their care for their Master, pray him to refresh himself with such viands as they had bought for him and themselves.

**32. ***{I have meat to eat that ye know not of}* That which I most desire; or more hunger for than meat; to wit, that I may do the will of God, in saving sinners. See Job 23:12. Because he would make his disciples teachers of the word; he thus teaches them by his own example, to have a greater care of others’ salvation, than of their own bodily necessities; and of the kingdom of God, than of all worldly emoluments: he says not, I do not hunger; but *I have meat, &c.* Thus would he erect their thoughts, and make them attentive to that great work, which they were presently to see in the conversion of the Samaritans: thus would he teach all, rightly to order their desires: first for God’s kingdom, and then for things necessary to this present life: this one thing duly observed; would both open the windows of heaven to pour us out blessings, and supplies of all necessaries, and make our store a blessing, which without this one method so often proves a mere curse and occasion of damnation to many.

**33. ***{hath any man brought him ought, &c.}* They could understand nothing but concerning bodily food: so hard is it to raise the thoughts above carnal reason. See also Matt. 16:7; John 11:11.

**34. ***{to do the will of him}* That is, to prosecute the calling of the Samaritans to the knowledge of the Gospel and to salvation. See ch. 6:40, which he desired more than bodily food: a document is here to the ministers, of diligence, and fervency, in the execution of their charge; and also of consolation to us all; Christ who then so much zealed the salvation of Samaritans then to be called, has not less care for us whom he has called.

**35. ***{Yet four months}* *ἔτι* *τετράμηνόν* *ἐστι*, the Syriac interprets, *post quatuor menses*; after four months: it is a Hebrew manner of expression; as Jonah 3:4, *עוד* *ארבעים* *יום*, *hod arbabim jom*; yet forty days, Aquila, Symmachus, &c., *τεσσαράκοντα*, forty days. See Augustin. *de C. D.* l. 18, c. 44.

*{white already to harvest}* Matt. 9:37. The spiritual harvest is now ripe, as Luke 10:2, therefore as the ripe corn cannot bear delay (without the owner’s great loss) but must be gathered in, so must the Jews and Gentiles now ready for God’s kingdom: by *regions white to the harvest*, he means (as Chrysostom, Theophylact, Euthymius, &c., interpret) *τὸ* *πλῆθος* *τῶν* *προσερχομένων* *Σαμαρειτῶν*, the multitude of the Samaritans coming to him; and their cheerful minds, ready to believe: Cyril more generally and better understands, those who being prepared by the voice of the prophets, were to be instructed in the faith of Christ: we may not unaptly say of them as Ambros. l. 10, ep. 8, says of Elijah; *Prophetam Domini agnoscebant corvi, quem non agnoscebant Judaei, pascebant corvi, quem genus illud persequebatur*: the ravens acknowledged the prophet of the Lord, whom the Jews acknowledged not: the ravens fed him whom this generation persecuted.

**36. ***{that reapeth, receiveth wages}* The doctrine of the prophets is compared to sowing, and the ministry of the Gospel, to gathering of ripe fruits; in either, the faithful servant shall have his reward.

**38. ***{other men labored}* Meaning the prophets, and John Baptist. See on verse 36. Moses, the fathers, prophets, and teachers, have labored very much in teaching and instructing this stiff-necked and obstinate people, drawing them from idolatry unto the true service of the Lord, expounding the law, declaring the promises of God, concerning their expected Messias and Savior, &c. Now the Jews being hereby prepared toward salvation, the apostles were sent to perfect the work, by gathering believers to Christ, which by the power of the Holy Spirit, they admirably performed after Christ’s ascension, as Acts 2:41, when there were added to the church of Christ in one day, about 3,000 souls. Here Christ seems to propose two arguments, or motives to their ministration. First, the examples of the foregoing prophets who sowed the seed of the Gospel. Secondly, the utility and greater facility of the work: all know that it is more easy to reap, than to prepare the earth and sow, and that the profit accruing to the husbandman is in the harvest.

**39. ***{many of the Samaritans, &c.}* This was a *praeludium* to the calling of the Gentiles; as were, the coming of the wise men to seek Christ, and the centurion, &c., Matt. 8:5. Howbeit the fullness of the time for their general calling, being not yet come, he gave in charge to his disciples, not to go into the cities of the Samaritans, &c., Matt. 10:5, for he was a minister of the circumcision, Rom. 15:8, sent specially to the lost sheep of the house of Israel, Matt. 15:24. Therefore he would not yet give the Jews matter of offense.

**40. ***{besought him that he would tarry}* They who have any true and real sense of their interest in that which the Gospel proposes to them cannot but love and desire their company and presence who preach the same: therefore the Samaritans, hearing the gracious words of Christ himself, desired to entertain him: Paul desired to die, that he might be with him, Phil. 1:23.

**41. ***{more believed}* For faith is by hearing, Rom. 10:17, which, whoever plants and waters, God gives, 1 Cor. 3:7. For Christ spent those two days (verse 40) teaching them the mysteries of salvation to be now shortly after to be divulged not to the Jews only, but also to the Samaritans and all other nations of the world for the remission of their sins, comfort, and salvation: and it is very probable, which some conjecture, that in that time he resolved them concerning their national question, about the place of true worship; and on that occasion he might teach them many things concerning the law, temple, worship of God, and the Messias; opening the ceremonies of the law typifying him, and the oracles of the prophets, foretelling those things which were to be accomplished by him: and no wonder if many believed on him who preached with that divine evidence and fullness of the Holy Spirit, which not only sounded in their ears; but converted their hearts, and begat faith and obedience in them.

**42. ***{not because of thy saying}* *λαλιά*; commonly importing talkativeness: Suidas interprets it by *δημηγορία*; which Budaeus expresses by the oratorical fluentness of tongue in those who are more studious to please than to profit: as Suidas instances in Arcesilaus, *satis in loquendo liber*; as the Hebrews aptly expressed it by *איש* *דברים* *ish debarim*, a man of words; that is, *eloquent*, as Exod. 4:10. So Cant. 4:3, *מדברך* *midbarech*, *thy speech is pleasant*: where the 70 give it by the word here used, *ἡ* *λαλία* *σου*, *thy speech*, &c. God, complying with our infirmities, has many ways and means to bring us to believe: he will provoke the Jews by emulation of the Gentiles, Rom. 11:11, others, by the honest and harmless conversation of professors, 1 Pet. 3:1. Some by the ordinary means, preaching of the word and administration of the sacraments: some by the authority and credit of the church; whereas, who, or whatsoever the occasion be, the word of God is the ground on whose certain authority we rest, and for which we believe: as here, at the woman’s report, they believed, and came forth: but all that served only to bring them to Christ: so says Augustin. *Tract.* 15 in Joh. Christ is preached to those who are without, and yet not Christians; by Christian friends, *tanquam illa muliere, hoc est, Ecclesiâ, nunciante*, as it were that woman, that is, the church, declaring, one might well say of himself, *I had not believed the Gospel, except I had believed the church*: because the voice of the church (as this woman the Samaritan) first moved him to come to Christ: not that he finally built his faith on the authority of men, much less preferred it before the authority of God’s word; but because he acknowledged the means (the ministry of man) by which God brought him to believe his divine word, which only is infallible; as a man shut up in some dark labyrinth, being thence led out by some obscure lamp carried before him, to the clear and open sun, might truly say, I had not come to the light of the sun, except I had followed this lamp; yet could it not be thence concluded, that now in the sunshine, he must still trust to the light of the lamp, or that the sun’s light should depend on the dim light thereof; human reason looks on immediate means and occasions as a cause, *sine quâ non*; between which, and the supreme cause, in a clear view, it discerns a vast distance; we honor the authority of the church, and the ministry of men, but put much difference between the authority of these, and God, on whose infallible truth our faith is grounded.

*{Saviour of the world}* Christ having preached to them, that the salvation which he brought, was universal without respect of persons; they apprehended their right and interest therein, and so conclude, that he brought conditions of peace to them, who were indeed far off from the covenants of grace, Eph. 2:17. Their admirable improvement of two days’ hearing; to the apprehension of this sum of the Gospel, must needs rise in judgment and condemn, not only the Jews’ two years’ fruitless hearing Christ, but also our many years hearing the same word without fruits of faith and repentance.

**43. ***{departed thence}* From Sychar in Samaria; he would not now stay long there, for the infirm Jews’ sakes, his course being not finished in Judea.

**44. ***{Jesus himself testified}* The evangelist renders the reason why Jesus went not into his own country Nazareth; they honored him not as they ought. See Luke 4:24; Mark 6:4.

**45. ***{the Galilaeans received him}* The proud Jews despised this people, ch. 7:52, so did they the Samaritans, yet both Samaritans and Galileans received Christ, when the Jews rejected him and his doctrine; many times they, whom arrogant hypocrites despise, as impious and unconsiderable, more happily receive Christ, and the Gospel than proud boasters.

**46. ***{made the water wine}* Ch. 2:1, &c.

*{a certain noble man}* A courtier, or ruler of Herod’s house, or some principal officer, counselor or chieftain; the Syriac styles him *עבד* *מלכא* *habed malco*, a servant of the king, a president or viceroy; so the interpreter of the Syriac has it, *gerens vices regis*. (See Is. Casaubon, *Exer.* c. 13, n. 55.) For though Herod Antipas was but a tetrarch and no king, yet doubtless he was by his friends and followers vulgarly styled king, and so the evangelists themselves accordingly call him, Mark 6:14, &c.

**47. ***{when he heard}* Having heard of the fame of Jesus healing all diseases; and hearing also where he now was; he addresses himself to him for help for his son.

**48. ***{ye will not believe}* Yet he believed, otherwise he would not have come to him for help; but he believed not aright in him, who thought he could not help, except he were bodily present; had he been well informed, he would have believed him to be God everywhere present. Greg. hom. 28, in Evang.

**49. ***{come down ere my child die}* This shows the weakness of his faith relying so much on the bodily presence of Christ, as if his son could not otherwise be cured: here were two infirmities of his faith discovered. First, in that he thought that Jesus could not heal his son except he were bodily present: he came not to the excellency of the centurion’s faith, Matt. 8:8—*speak the word only, and my servant shall be healed*. The second infirmity appeared in that he thought Jesus would come too late, if he came not before the child died; he believed not so much as Martha professed—*I know that even now, whatsoever thou wilt ask of God, God will give it thee*, John 11:22, yet Christ showed mercy to the weak.

**50. ***{the man believed}* The man specified, verse 46, so that here Christ at once cured two; the child of the fever, and the father of unbelief; the man departed, relying on Christ’s power and mercy.

**51. ***{as he was now going down}* In the way between Cana and Capernaum: which stood upon the lake or sea of Galilee, on the bank of the river Jordan, in the borders of Zabulon and Neptalim.

*{his servants met him}* Being ambitious to comfort their master with speed, by bringing him the welcome news of his son’s recovery, it is probable, that contending among themselves who should be the messenger, more came to meet him, where one could as easily have brought the tidings.

**52. ***{Then inquired he of them the hour}* Desiring further confirmation of that which he so much wished; and comparing the time and hour in which Christ said, *thy son liveth*, verse 50, with the servants’ report of the child’s recovery, he found that Jesus not only declared that which was then done in his bodily absence (which a prophet might possibly have done) but that Jesus had cured his son.

*{seventh hour the fever left him}* At one of the clock in the afternoon; for their day was divided into twelve hours, John 11:9, and began at sunrising; whence they computed their first hour of the artificial day, so that their seventh hour must fall with our one of the afternoon.

**53. ***{and his whole house}* The master finding his son recovered, by the word of Christ, bodily absent, believed the divine power of Christ; and relating the whole passage of the business to all that were in his family, they also believed with him.

**54. ***{This is again the second miracle}* The second which he did in Galilee, in Judea he had done many. Thus will Christ confirm that which he has begun; and so precious are the souls of a few believers in his sight; we read here but of one house gained to the faith; yet he (who foresaw all events to come) for that one’s sake, came again to visit what he had planted; he has regard and care to save one Noah in a perishing world, one Lot in five cities, Gen. 18:32. He expresses it in two parables, Luke 15.

## CHAPTER 5

**1. ***{After this}* Lev. 23:2; Deut. 16. After that he had cured the nobleman’s son, ch. 4:50. But it seems that this was not immediately after that, there having been other intervenient things which the other evangelists relate, and this omits.

*{was a feast of the Jews}* Chrysost., Cyril, Euthym., Theophylact, Lyranus, Bonaventure, Fab. Stapulensis, M. Calvin, W. Musculus, and others think it was Pentecost. Irenaeus l. 2, c. 39, Rupertus, Oecolampadius, Beza, Rolloc., Brugensis, Scaliger, *de emendat. temp.*, Pelargus, Chemnitius *harm. Evang.*, &c., understand it was the feast of Passover; one thing is most probable, that he came up to the feast, as to satisfy the law on that behalf, so also that in that usual confluence of people, he might find the better opportunity, to divulge the Gospel, concerning their several feasts. See Lev. 22; Num. 29; Deut. 16.

**2. ***{by the sheep market}* Or *gate*, *ἐπὶ* *τῇ* *προβατικῇ*, the first translation understands *ἐν* *ἀγορᾷ*, *in the market*, because there was near, a sheep-market, or beast-market, as Matt. 20:3, to accommodate sacrifices, who might there buy; the second translation understands *πύλῃ*, gate: for, says an author, by it the beasts to be offered in sacrifice, were brought in, Neh. 3:1 and 12:39. We read *שער* *הצאן* *shahar hatzon*. 70. *τῆς* *πύλης* *τῆς* *προβατικῆς*: *the sheep-gate*. So Junius and Tremelius give it, *portam gregis*: this gate was to the southward of the city, in that part of the valley which looks toward the front of the temple, and city of David. Adricomius, *Jerusalem*, n. 70, calls it, *forum pecuarium*.

*{Bethesda}* Syr. *בית* *חסדא* *beth chesda*, *the house of beneficence, or bounty*: for, God there bountifully exercised his power in curing all diseases of his people.

*{having five porches}* The word *στοὰ* here used, has divers significations; Suidas expresses it by *ταμιεῖον* (a barn, or granary where corn or fruits were laid up), *διὰ* *τὸ* *περίμηκες* *εἶναι*—because it was a long room (so Clem. Alexandr. *Paed.* l. 2, says, *οἷς* *οὐκ* *ἔστι* *ταμεῖον* *καὶ* *ἀποθήκη*). The Latins commonly give it, *porticus*, which we call porches, galleries, or walks, to shelter from violent heat or rain: it seemed they had made such about this pool, to shelter sick and lame men, repairing to that place, and watching for the moving of the water, which being sudden, and at no fixed time, they could not have been able to attend, had they had no cover to defend them from the weather, and place to rest in.

**3. ***{blind, halt, withered}* The evangelist mentions some, to declare that they were no easy or common cures there wrought, but even of the greatest and most desperate maladies.

**4. ***{an Angel went down at a certain season}* Not in any visible shape, but by the sudden moving of the water, and the admirable effect following in the present cure of him that first went into the water after that motion, it appeared to be the extraordinary work of God, who as he has made his angels ministering spirits for the good of his, so does he powerfully work by them, in extraordinary dispensations.

*{at a certain season}* There are divers conjectures concerning this season; but when, and how often he descended, we have no certainty.

*{whosoever then first, &c.}* God could have cured all at once in a moment, but in his wisdom he instances his presence, power, and mercy, in a few, and at certain times, that all may depend on his providence, and patiently expect him, in respect both of the time and manner of their relief. Here are sundry things considerable in these cures. 1. That all diseases, how old, great, or desperate soever, were here cured. 2. That, in a moment, or very quickly. 3. That but one at once was healed, of so many who expected God’s mercy therein, one only attained health, and all the rest, to their no small grief were unhelped. 4. That he only was helped who first stepped in, or was put in; after the moving of the water, as if that one, took up all the healing virtue. 5. That the same water could not always heal, but at certain times, when it had been troubled, as a wrought sea, or from the bottom to the top: for so the word, *ἐτάρασσε*, imports, as *ἐτάραξε* *δὲ* *πόντον*, Hom. Odyss. *ε*. 6. That no other moving, but that of an angel descending from heaven, made those waters healing: all was to teach us to cast our cares on the Lord, to depend on his providence, who can help with ordinary means, without them, and contrary to them. See 2 Kings 5:10 and 2:20; Exod. 14:22 and 16:15, 35; Judg. 3:21 and 15:15, 19; 2 Kings 4; Mark 8:23; John 9:6.

**5. ***{had an infirmity thirty and eight years}* Christ chose out this man, whose condition and disease might seem most incurable, by reason of the long continuance thereof, and he most unlikely to have benefit of the water, who neither was able to move himself, neither had any man to carry him into the pool in due season, this was, as to set off God’s goodness in the eminency of the miracle; so in this pattern of patience, to teach us the prophet’s rule, *though it tarry, wait for it, because it will surely come, &c.*, Hab. 2:3 (God often delays that which he will certainly give at last), also to make the afflicted seriously consider their faults for which God punishes them; see Lam. 3:39, 40, 41, by a divine disposal it comes so to pass, that, *prolixiora vitia, aegritudo prolixior curat*. Greg. hom. 19, in Evang. Long sickness burns out long vices. God delays to help, to exercise the faith and patience of the afflicted, to kindle their zeal to prayer, to render his mercies more dear and remarkable; see John 9:1, 21; John 11:39; Matt. 9:20; Luke 13:11.

**6. ***{wilt thou be healed?}* There seems no doubt but that he (who had with thirty-eight years’ patience endured) would gladly be healed: but this Christ said, to make both him and the spectators the more attentive to that which he was now about to do: thus he prevents us with his mercy, see Isa. 65:24. This indeed is proper to the Almighty and true God, *inexorata beneficia praebere fessis, &c.* Arnob. *adv. Gent.* l. 3, to give unbegged benefits, to the weary and infirm: which when he does in matters spiritual, he makes us willing to receive who freely gives of his own good will, so that here, as Synesius *de insomn.* says, *καθαρμῷ* *τὸ* *μέγιστον* *μέρος* *ἡ* *θέλησις*, the greatest part of the cleansing is the will, to be clean, which God works in us: *of his good pleasure* (Phil. 2:13), by his preventing grace.

**7. ***{I have no man, &c.}* So uncharitable was that Jewish nation become; they thought all religion consisted in sacrificing, but held not charity, the ground and sum of the law: in so great a city, so much pretending to religion, no man, in so long time, showed so much mercy to this poor man, as to help him into the water: no wonder that Christ sent the learned doctors to learn that necessary lesson, *I will have mercy, not sacrifice* (Matt. 9:13; 1 Sam. 15:22), that is, mercy rather than sacrifice.

**8. ***{take up thy bed}* For the evidence and manifestation of the cure: so Mark 2:11; Matt. 9:6. This he says, that passing through the multitude with his bed on his back, whom many of them probably might know so many years to have there lain helpless, lame, and not able to move himself for help, the evidence of the miracle might appear.

**9. ***{immediately the man was made whole}* Though he had been so many years lame, Christ presently healed him, *in the same hour*, as the interpreter of the Syriac gives it: and this also clears the miracle from all ground of calumny, and suspicion of compact, that this man now cured was lame some years before Christ was born: compare verse 5 with Luke 3:23.

*{was the sabbath}* This also made the miracle more notorious; because all that saw him carrying his bed on the Sabbath, on which none among the Jews used to bear any burden, would take special notice of the person and fact; had it been on any other day, few would have noted it, or said anything to it: but now every one that saw him carrying, blaming him for doing that which they judged unlawful, and he excusing it by the command of him that cured him, the fame of the cure grew more public.

**10. ***{the Jews therefore said}* They pretend religion, to overthrow the ground and fundamentals thereof, faith in Christ, and charity to men (see 1 Tim. 1:5; 1 Cor. 13:2, 3), because Christ healed on the Sabbath, whereas indeed that shows, that works of mercy are works of the Sabbath made for man. The Jews of whom he speaks here, it seems were those by whom he passed carrying his bed to his house.

*{it is not lawful for thee to carry thy bed}* Jer. 17:22. It was not lawful to carry, except that carrying had been within the compass of sanctifying the Sabbath, as was the advancement of God’s glory, a work of mercy and necessity, in which Christ showed it lawful.

**11. ***{He that made me whole}* He replies not concerning works of the Sabbath, but cites the authority of him, who had divine power to cure him: take heed of rash censure; and consider not only the letter, but also the intent of the law; which is faith and charity. See on verse 10; 1 Tim. 1:5.

**12. ***{What man is that}* They would not acknowledge God’s hand in this work, but inquire after a man by them pretended to oppose the express commandment of God concerning the Sabbath; as if they said, do but name that man and whatever he be, he shall not go unpunished, thus were they preposterously zealous, and superstitiously religious, not considering the sense, but the letter of the law.

**13. ***{conveyed himself away}* This shows the reason why the man that was healed was ignorant who had healed him and had not inquired concerning the author of so admirable a blessing on him bestowed: the unexpected sense of perfect health, and strength suddenly recovered, might possibly take up all his thoughts in admiration; and while he was rising and taking up his bed as he was commanded, Jesus went away through the present multitude.

*{a multitude being}* Or *from the multitude that was*. The Syriac gives it, *had hid himself in the multitude*: which might easily be, seeing there were many of sundry statures there; and in multitudes it is not easy to discern one from another: the word here used is *ἐξένευσεν*, which properly signifies, to swim out; the multitude is often compared to fluctuant waters, Rev. 17:2, 15.

**14. ***{Sin no more}* He thus warns to show him the cause of his infirmity, and the right use of health, which is, amendment of the former sinful life: thus good physicians not only cure, but prescribe for the avoidance of a more dangerous relapse: God delights not in our sorrows but therefore smites us with infirmity, that he might not condemn us with the perishing world; and certainly worse must needs befall them, who will not be warned by lighter corrections, either in this life, or that to come. That which Christ says here, *Sin no more, lest a worse thing come unto thee*, must not be understood of a simple and absolute intermination of a more grievous affliction to come upon him if he sinned any more at all; for *there is no man that sinneth not*, 1 Kings 8:46, but in the apostle’s sense, Rom. 6:12, *Let not sin reign therefore in your mortal bodies, that you should obey it in the lust thereof*. See also ch. 8:11; 2 Pet. 2:20, 21.

**15. ***{told the Jews, that it was Jesus}* They asked him, who it was, verse 12. Now being informed (probably by inquiring of his disciples) he tells them: showing them that divine and admirable Physician, if possibly they also might want him for themselves or friends.

**16. ***{Therefore did the Jews persecute Jesus}* For it is the property of hypocrites, under pretense of zeal for religion, to execute their private malice, which was the true cause why they persecuted Christ; truth ever stimulates it to envy and revenge, but their pretense was zeal to the law of God, which they would have the people believe that he violated, who healed on the Sabbath day: they persecuted him in calumnies, blasphemous speeches, detractions, and also designing his death; as appears verse 18. See Gal. 4:29; Gen. 21:9.

**17. ***{my Father worketh hitherto}* My Father is beneficent in preserving you all on this very Sabbath: and my works are his, and so, no violation of the Sabbath: I work by the same power, which not only at the beginning created, and still preserves all the creatures (Heb. 1:2, 3), but also healed this man, and so, as Hil. *de trin.* l. 7, says well, *in eo ergo quod operatur filius, opus patris est*: therefore the work of the Father is in that which the Son works; they have one power and work. God in the beginning rested the seventh day, which he sanctified: he ceased then (says Aug. *Consent. ep.* 146.9.2, *ab ipsis naturis condendis*—*non ab earum administratione*) from creating natures or new species but not from his administration, and preservation of those kinds which he then created, for as the same says, l. 12 *de C. D.*, c. 17, *novit quiescens agere, & agens quiescere*, he knows how to work resting, and to rest working; who does all things without labor, and lassitude, only by his word.

**18. ***{Because he had broken the Sabbath}* This relates to the Jews’ sense: he had not broken the Sabbath, for he came not to destroy, but to fulfill the law, Matt. 5:17, but they thought he had broken it.

*{that God was his Father}* This was proper and peculiar to him, as a father by nature, not adoption.

*{making himself equal with God}* They understood not that he was God of God, coequal, consubstantial and coeternal with the Father.

**19. ***{Nothing of himself}* Or, *as distinct from the Father’s work*: seeing, as they are one God, so have they one will and one working: for, says Fulgentius *de fide orthod.*, in that nature of the Trinity *sic totum unum est, ut nihil ibi possit separari vel dividi*—so is all one, that there nothing can be separated or divided: so is all equal, that nothing greater or less can there be found.

*{what things soever he doth, these also doth the son likewise}* Whatsoever the Father does, the Son does; because they are one God, have one will, power, and working. So Nazianzen, orat. 36, well interprets, this *υἱὸς* *ὁμοίως* *ποιεῖ*, *doth the Son likewise*; *οὐ* *κατὰ* *τὴν* *τῶν* *πραγμάτων* *ὁμοίωσιν*, *ἀλλὰ* *κατὰ* *τὴν* *τῆς* *ἐξουσίας* *ὁμοτιμίαν*—not for the similitude of those things they do, but in respect of the same power and authority of both, the Son does not imitate, but cooperate; he works not the like, but the same work which the Father does.

**20. ***{Sheweth him all things}* By a divine and unspeakable communication. The Father works so, as that the Son who is one with him, ch. 10:30; 1 John 5:7, not only sees or knows what the Father does; but also cooperates in all that he does: and the Father works by him.

*{greater works than these}* That is, he will yet do more admirable works than these which you have already seen; works which shall more evidently set forth his glory, and declare his divine power working by me.

**21. ***{Quickeneth whom he will}* *Ζωοποιεῖ*, *maketh alive*, or gives life; understand here by life, righteousness, all the gifts of the Holy Ghost, and all things belonging to our salvation, with all the parts and degrees thereof; he finds us all dead in sins, Eph. 2:1. Therefore in the work of our salvation he must needs begin from our first resurrection to the life of grace: and these words, *raiseth up*, and *quickeneth*; show that he both raised up, and delivers from sin and also sanctifies us by the same Spirit (see Rom. 8:10, 11), which he does (as all other acts of mercy, or, *ad extra*) with the Father: therefore also when he would raise Lazarus he prayed, and gave thanks to his Father, ch. 11:41, that we might know, that they jointly wrought in the same work.

**22. ***{the Father judgeth no man}* Ch. 8:15. He says, *I judge no man*: that is, in point of civil judicature: or, I alone judge no man: and indeed he came not then into the world to judge, but to preach the gladsome tidings of salvation: so the Father judges not alone, because he judges by the Son, Acts 17:31; Rom. 2:16. The word (judges) here, as verse 27, 30, signifying full power to rule in heaven and earth: as Matt. 11:27 and 28:18. He says the Father judges no man, as they imagined, who separated him from the Son; by whom as he created all, so rules he, and judges he all; so he says he came not to do his own will, but the will of his Father. See verse 30, and ch. 6:38; ch. 7:16, not simply, but in their sense who would divide the Father and the Son, so he oftentimes speaks. See ch. 9:3.

**23. ***{Honour the Son}* In the unity of the Godhead; neither of them can be truly worshiped without the other; as the following words expressly show.

**24. ***{but is passed from death unto life}* That is, shall as certainly, as if it were already done.

**25. ***{the dead shall hear the voice}* In the first, or spiritual resurrection. See Rev. 20:6, and compare therewith, Eph. 2:1; 1 Tim. 5:6.

*{and they that hear shall live}* They that believe, and obey the voice of Christ, shall live eternally.

**26. ***{so hath he given to the Son}* He renders a reason of that he said, the dead shall hear the voice of the Son of God, and live: because God the Father, who is the eternal fountain of life, communicates his whole essence to the Son: so that, as he is the eternal Word and Wisdom of God, he has all things of himself: but as he is the Son of man, he has received all things of the Father, as, authority and power to execute judgment; which understand principally of the last general judgment, as that ch. 3:17, God sent not his Son into the world, to condemn the world, &c., that is, not now in his state of humility.

**27. ***{To execute judgment}* Supreme power, to govern and administer all things.

*{because he is the Son of man}* Not only as he is God, but also as he is man, that all men may see their Judge, Rev. 1:7.

**28. ***{All that are in the graves}* That is, all the dead, in sea and land: this he says to show that he will not only raise the believers to the life of grace here, but also to the life of glory in the world to come: and though the obstinate sinners would not hear his voice, preaching conditions of mercy and salvation to them now; yet shall they against their wills, hear his voice and feel his power raising them from the dead, and summoning them to judgment.

**29. ***{They that have done good}* See Matt. 25:46, wherein they shall be rewarded according to, not for their works; life eternal is God’s free gift, not man’s merit. See Rom. 6:23.

**30. ***{as I hear, I judge}* As I know my Father’s judgment; as before verse 19 he uses the word, *Seeing*, so now he uses this of *hearing*: which signifies his receiving wisdom, ability to judge, and his whole nature (as God) of the Father; as man he receives it of the Deity, the *Father, Son*, and *Holy Ghost*.

*{because I seek not mine own will}* Either in judging, or doing: I do my Father’s will in all, and therefore is my judgment just, because according to the will of my Father: men in judging too often do their own wills, not God’s; and therefore err: but he that judges according to the most holy and just will of God, is just.

**31. ***{if I bear witness of myself}* Ch. 8:14. If I only testified, you might distrust; but now, God, the only sufficient witness of himself, testifies of me.

**32. ***{There is another}* That is, God the Father, as ch. 7:18, he acknowledges me his only Son in whom he is well pleased, Matt. 3:17 and 17. So that I arrogate nothing to myself, which God my Father does not avow.

**33. ***{ye sent unto John}* He presses them with John’s testimony, which they durst not deny: and with their own judgment, who sent unto him to be resolved concerning Christ; why should you send to him except you would stand to his testimony? He gave witness to the truth, which you condemn as falsehood.

**34. ***{receive not testimony from man}* I ground not on man’s witness: man can no more prove God’s truth, than a lamp can show us the sun.

**35. ***{he was a burning and a shining lamp}* He was not that light of lights mentioned, ch. 1:8, but one bearing witness of him: he was an eminent light, ardent in the zeal of faith and piety: so that the unbelieving Jews were willfully darkened, and voluntarily erred in their inconstancy, wherein they never liked any long, were they never so admirable lights, in sincerity of zeal, and truth of doctrine.

*{for a season to rejoice}* Until you distasted his testimony concerning me.

**36. ***{I have greater witness}* See Matt. 3:17; Matt. 17:5; 1 John 5:7. He says, greater, not truer, both were true; but the works which Christ did, more evidently concluded that he was the Son of God, and are so said to testify of him, as the heavens are said to declare the glory of God, Ps. 19:1. See John 10:25.

**37. ***{Hath borne witness of me}* Matt. 3:17; 2 Pet. 1:17.

*{nor seen his shape}* See on ch. 1:18; Deut. 4:12.

**38. ***{ye have not his word abiding in you}* They had the true word of God (as many now have) in their closets, but not in their hearts: they neither understood the meaning thereof, nor did they set their hearts to obey it, see ch. 8:19, 37. They who receive not Christ, are no true disciples of Moses and the prophets.

**39. ***{search the Scriptures}* Or, *ye do search*, as Acts 17:11. He proves himself to be the Messias, 1. By the Father’s testimony. 2. By John Baptist’s witness. 3. By his works. 4. Now by the Scriptures, the word *ἐρευνᾶτε*, may be either the indicative, and so translated, *ye do search*, or the imperative, and so it is to be given, *search ye*, the first sense implies a just reproof of their preposterous diligence; as if he said, you do indeed read and search the Scriptures, rightly thinking that the doctrine of salvation is thence to be drawn: yet you obstinately disbelieve him of whom the Scripture witnesses, that there is salvation in none other: and here, that which Hil. l. 7 *de trin.* says is very considerable: it cannot (says he) be denied, that from misunderstanding, the controversy concerning faith arose, *dum quod legitur sensui potius coaptatur, quam lectioni sensus obtemperat*: while that which is read is rather fitted to the sense (of the reader) than (his) sense subjected to that he reads: and again (*contr. Constant.*) he says there is none of the heretics which does not feign, or falsely pretend that he preaches according to Scriptures; even those things in which he blasphemes: they all speak Scriptures, without their sense, and pretend faith, without faith; *Scripturae enim non in legendo sunt, sed in intelligendo*: for the Scriptures are not in reading (or the letter) but in understanding (that is, inward sense and true meaning), and again; *optimus enim lector est, qui dictorum intelligentiam expectet ex dictis potius quam imponat, & retulerit magis quam attulerit; neque cogat id videri dictis contineri, quod ante lectionem praesumpserit intelligendum, &c.* For he is the best reader, who rather expects the meaning of the sayings from the words, than imposes it (on them), and rather carries away from, than brings sense (to that he reads); and who forces not the words to seem to carry that sense, which before his reading, he presumed must be understood; therefore when the speech shall be concerning God, let us yield God the knowledge of himself, and attend his words with holy reverence: for he is a competent witness of himself, who is not (cannot be) known, except he reveal himself. The second sense imports an exhortation to search the Scripture, not superficially, but as Euthymius after Chrysost. says, he commands them to dig deep that they may find: *ἐν* *τῷ* *βάθει* *κείμενα*, those things which are in the depth; for it is not in the *superficies*, *ἀλλὰ* *ὥσπερ* *τις* *θησαυρὸς* *ἐν* *πολλῷ* *τῷ* *βάθει* *κατάκειται*: but as a treasure hid in great depth.

*{ye think ye have eternal life}* He says not, *ye have*, but *ye think ye have*: teaching that they (as now too many) deceived themselves, in opinion that hearing, or reading the word was sufficient without faith and obedience thereto: see Rom. 2:13; Heb. 4:2.

**40. ***{he will not come to me}* The meaning is, that they labor in vain in reading the Scriptures, who make it not their principal aim, to find Christ therein: and that it was their malice which hindered them from receiving the benefit of eternal life offered them in the Gospel; in saying *ye will not come*, he taxes their contumacy as the chief cause of their ignorance and blindness; opinion of having in themselves sufficient sanctity to save them, is a dangerous obstruction; they who think themselves healthy, seek not to the Physician.

**41. ***{I receive not honour from men}* That is, not human applause (had I been ambitious thereof, I would never have taken on me the form of a servant). Christ is greater, than that opinion or judgment of men can either take from, or add to his glory: to an infinite, nothing can be added, nothing can be taken from it.

**42. ***{ye have not the love of God in you}* As if he said, I well know why you come not to me, because you have no true love of God in you; you may blasphemously pretend other causes of your not believing in me, you may impose upon an ignorant vulgar; but I know you: he takes the love of God here, for the ground of all true religion and sanctity, the sum of the law, Deut. 6:5. God sees the hypocrisy of those, who have God, truth, and godliness in their tongues, but devilish malice, pride and vainglory in their hearts.

**43. ***{If another shall come}* If a false prophet should come, you would be too apt to receive him: so did they in Trajan’s time follow their Barcocheba, whom almost all the Jews took for their Messias: this impostor caused great effusion of blood between the Jews and Romans: but when they perceived the mischief he had led them into, the Jews called their Barchocheba (which signifies, *the son of a star*) Barcoziba, that is, *the son of a lie*. See Pet. Galatin. *de arcan. Cathol. verit.* Jo. Drus., *praeterit.* l. 4, in Joh. 5:43, and ib. *ad V. N. T.* par. ult. Joh. 5:43.

**44. ***{how can ye believe}* That is, you cannot believe, because of your ambition, which is contrary to the love and honor of God. It is impossible that you should believe in me the Son of God and only Savior of the elect now under the form of a servant, so long as the height of your ambition is only external pomp, secular glory, and applause of men, which you mutually give each other, whereof you find nothing in me, nor desired of me.

*{which receive honour one of another}* Which love the praise of men more than the praise of God, ch. 12:43, one flattering another with the characters of just, wise, and godly: desirous only to please men, and to seem holy to them, that they may speak well of them, not valuing the judgment or praise of God. See Rom. 2:29; 1 Cor. 4:5.

**45. ***{there is one that accuseth you}* Moses, or the law (in the works whereof you trust to be justified) shall condemn you, who continue not in everything written therein, neither believe in Christ of whom he testified.

*{Moses in whom ye trust}* They boasted that they were Moses’ disciples, and opposed him to Christ (ch. 9:28, 29); he tells them that the law given them by the ministry of Moses, will accuse and condemn them.

**46. ***{If ye had believed Moses}* He shows why Moses should accuse and condemn them; because they believed him not; so dangerous is it to pretend to that which we will not obey, nor believe.

*{he wrote of me}* Gen. 3:15; Deut. 18:15; Gen. 49:10; Num. 24:17, &c. Indeed the whole scope of the ceremonial law was to prefigure Christ to come, suffer for our redemption, and cleanse us from all our sins: for he is the end of the law to every believer: see the whole aim of the Epistle to the Hebrews, and Acts 7:2, &c.; Luke 24:27.

**47. ***{how shall ye believe in me?}* That is, seeing you pretend highly to reverence Moses, and yet believe him not any further than you find in his writings compliance with your own opinions, how should you believe in my words of which you have no such esteem? This veil yet laid over their heart, in reading the Scriptures, shall be taken away, when the fullness of the Gentiles is come in. See 2 Cor. 3:16; Rom. 11:25.

## CHAPTER 6

**1. ***{The sea of Tiberias}* The lake of Genezareth: Tiberias, Bethsaida and Capernaum, were on this side the lake, in respect of Galilee: but it is here said, that he passed over, because he crossed some creeks running up into the land, or some bays, that he might go the nearer way: see on Luke 5:1, and on John 21:1.

**2. ***{because they saw his miracles}* This was the cause why they followed him into the deserts: seeing his admirable power, healing all diseases, they conceived that he was some great prophet sent of God: hence was that zeal to attend him so, as that they neither minded their want of meat, or lodging in the desert: which must rise in judgment and condemn our carelessness towards God’s word, from which the least let uses to detain or distract us, though we are not to follow Christ now into the desert mountains; his word is near us, Rom. 10:8.

**3. ***{went up into a mountain}* Probably for the same reason which is expressed, Mark 6:31; that they might rest a while. A mountain in the desert of Bethsaida, Luke 9:10.

**4. ***{and the Passover}* Lev. 23:5; Deut. 16:1. See ch. 2:13. This must be the third Passover after Christ’s baptism, if that feast mentioned, ch. 5:1, was the Passover.

**5. ***{lift up his eyes and saw a great multitude}* Thus he undertakes the care for them, who for his sake neglect themselves. See Matt. 14:15, 16, 18, 19, &c.

*{saith unto Philip}* He spoke to Philip, because it seems he spoke the sense of his fellow disciples: we may not conceive that Christ thus spoke, as soon as he saw this multitude: for the other evangelists show that he first taught them many things concerning the kingdom of God, and healed those who were infirm, by which time it was almost night; then took he care to feed and dismiss them. See Mark 6:37; Luke 9:13.

**6. ***{This he said to prove him}* Not that Jesus knew not beforehand what Philip would answer, but, as Gen. 22:1, God tempted, or tried Abraham, to make Abraham know his own faith and obedience; so here to make Philip know his faith, whether (seeing in the ordinary way of nature there were no means) he would have recourse to Christ’s omnipotency; of which he had seen so much experience? And to show him the defects thereof in the following miracle.

**7. ***{two hundred penny worth}* As if he said, neither know we where timely to buy so much for them before night, neither is that a little money in our cash.

**9. ***{there is a lad here which, &c.}* He had said first unto them, how many loaves have you? Go and see, Mark 6:38, which when Andrew had informed himself of, in the name of the rest, he says, *there is a lad, &c., five loaves*, that is, to sell: for it is probable that their own store, after their repast in the mountain, was very slender: this sets off the truth of the miracle of feeding so many, so fully, when there was neither in the disciples’ keeping, nor among the multitude, any more, than so incompetent a store for them all.

*{what are they among so many}* Neither did he yet think of any more than human and ordinary supplies. He knew not that Christ would by his divine power increase this small store to a sufficiency to feed this great multitude, and therefore answers; as relating to second causes, by whose proportions, it was no competent food for 5,000 men. We here very much live and are guided by sense, and therefore usually determine and judge, according to the principles thereof, and so, not so constantly rely on the omnipotent providence of God, as indeed we ought; when Elijah, 1 Kings 17:10, &c., said to the widow at Zarephath, *Bring me I pray thee a morsel of bread in thy hand*; she had ready to say, *as the Lord thy God liveth, I have not a cake, but an handful of meal in a barrel, and a little oil in a cruse: and behold I am gathering two sticks, that—we may eat it and die*. Experience made her better understand the power of God’s providence.

**10. ***{make the men sit down}* When these loaves and fishes were brought him; he does not here reprove their unbelief, but confirm their faith by the miracle done. He would have them thus sit down on rows, as Mark 6:39, 40, that the disciples might the more readily distribute to every one a portion; and that the number appearing, the miracle might be the more evident unto them all then present; see Luke 9:14.

*{So the men sate down}* *ἀνέπεσον*, *they fell* or *lay down*; as their manner was to eat: they were uncertain of the event; but yet readily obeyed his word: such readiness was also in the waiters at the Cana wedding, to fill the waterpots with water when they wanted wine, ch. 2:7. This is a trial of faith, when God commands them to do that, whereof for present they apprehend neither event, nor reason: man’s reason would have said to this multitude, hie you away lest you be famished here, by a little more delay; but sitting down here as he commanded, where there appeared no probability of supply, they found abundance to suffice them all: thus will he teach us not to rely on our own wisdoms, but to depend on his providence and word, who never frustrates the hopes of his. See Matt. 14:21.

**11. ***{when Jesus had given thanks}* That is, as the Syriac has it, *וברך* *ubarec*, *and blessed it*: so also Matt. 26:26; Luke 9:16.

*{he distributed}* By the ministry of the disciples, to every one as much as fully satisfied them to a perfect refection: which is added to set out the truth and excellence of the miracle, those victuals being not sparingly, but fully and freely given to every one present.

**12. ***{gather up the fragments}* This he commanded, as for the evidence of the miracle; so also that none of God’s blessings might be lost wherein would have appeared a contempt or undervaluing of them: to teach us a holy frugality in the use of God’s creatures: of which we are but stewards, and must once give account; and that we must rely on God’s providence, who can make the least measure sufficient.

**13. ***{filled twelve baskets}* So wonderfully had he increased the small store. See also 1 Kings 17:15, 16, and 2 Kings 4; Matt. 14:20.

**14. ***{this is of a truth that Prophet}* *ὁ* *ἐρχόμενος*, the Prophet which was to come; that is, whose coming we have hitherto expected: or as the Syriac interpreter has it, *qui venit in mundum*, *who is now come into the world*: this is the Messias promised by God, Deut. 18:15. Such is the vulgar, more sensible of bodily and temporal benefits (which they expected in Christ) than spiritual and eternal: we read not that they thus acknowledge him in his most divine and unparalleled preaching to them the mystery of eternal life: but when he feeds their bellies, then they cry, *this is that Prophet*.

**15. ***{to make him a king}* This also shows how much they regarded their bellies, more than their souls: when he gave them the best spiritual food they offered him no such honor, but because he thus fed them, they would proclaim him the King Messias; under whom they dreamed of health, wealth, fullness, victory, protection and all secular blessings. So much lived they to carnal sense; so few understood the true end of Christ’s coming into the world, which was that he might suffer for us, and redeem us from the power of Satan, death and hell.

*{into a mountain himself alone}* Where he used to pray: his kingdom is spiritual, not of this world; therefore offended at their perverse service, who would thus honor him, he withdrew himself from them.

**16. ***{when even was now come}* Matt. 14:23, and that now having sent his disciples aboard before him, he might the more conveniently dismiss the multitude, and withdraw himself into the mountain.

**17. ***{went over the sea}* That is, some creek or arm thereof, as verse 1. Mark 6:45, he bade them go before to Bethsaida, while he sent the people away (because Bethsaida was in their way as they went toward Capernaum), while he was dismissing them, it seems they were conspiring to make him king, and therefore he went from them into the mountain alone.

**18. ***{and the sea arose}* *Swelled*, as the Syriac has it; *was rough and high running*.

**19. ***{about five and twenty or thirty furlongs}* Above three miles from shore; so little way had they made from the evening to the fourth watch of the night. See Matt. 14:25.

**20. ***{It is I, be not afraid}* They were troubled for his absence, and terrified with his presence, when they thought they had seen some ghost walking on the water; therefore he discovers himself to them: thus are the faithful secure and full of comfort in the presence and protection of Christ; at whose voice the wicked tremble, and are cast down: ch. 18:6.

**21. ***{they willingly received him}* Though they were afraid of him at first, when they knew him not, yet hearing his voice, they were glad of his saving presence, and received him into the ship, Matt. 14:32; Mark 6:51. John shows another part of the miracle by them omitted, that is, the sudden arrival of the ship, which before Christ came aboard, with all their art and labor, rowing, and whatever they could do made so little way. See verse 19. So Christ not only comforts in word, but also in real deliverance of his.

**22. ***{which stood on the other side}* Which lodged thereabouts, and supposed that Jesus had been there still, where the miracle was done.

*{but that his disciples were gone away alone}* That is, by themselves without Jesus with them: it is doubtful with which of the two, the disciples (as in this our translation) or with the noun collective, the multitude, this verb *ἀπῆλθον*, *went away*, is to be construed: the latter seems probable, and the sense may be, when the multitude saw that the disciples only took shipping, and that Jesus went not *with them*, they, that is, the multitude, went away or departed.

**23. ***{they also took shipping}* That is, in some of those which came from Tiberias, so the Syriac has it, *they went into these ships*: we read of no storm in their passage, but only upon the disciples’ ship: God exercises the greatest gifts with the greatest trials. It bore a type of the world sailing before the wind with a fair gale, while they that are Christ’s feel the storms and wrestling winds of afflictions, the more to confirm them in their deliverance.

**25. ***{when camest thou hither?}* Seeing we have waited on you, and marked that there was none other ship here, save that in which your disciples went over before you; when, and how could you come over sea? Theophylact’s conjecture is probable; they might possibly suspect, that he went on foot to the other side miraculously.

**26. ***{but because ye did eat}* He answers not to their question, but knowing their thoughts, reproves them, for that they valued not his doctrine, or confirmation thereof by miracles, so that they might believe and be saved, but cared principally for their bellies; the fault that our carnal gospellers are commonly guilty of: they think they do excellently seek Christ when their chief aim and care is to find ease, peace, wealth, credit, or some temporal blessings in their practice, or profession of the Gospel: thus would the covetous Jews have impropriated Christ himself unto secular uses; so unworthy a value set they upon that inestimable mercy of God in giving them his only begotten Son, as if he had only given them some secular, and soon-perishing blessing in him, and not eternal life and salvation: so hard a matter is it to cast off the importunate cares and desires of flesh and blood, that we may affect, and receive the unvaluable mercy of God for our salvation in Christ, *Vix quaeritur Jesus propter Jesum*, said Augustine on this place; rarely is Jesus sought for his own sake: but indeed, then the heart is right to God, *quando propter Deum quaeritur Deus*, Prosper *sent.*, when God is sought for his own sake: and how great should our happiness be in heaven, if we did not with profane Esau, undervalue it for temporal supplies? See on Matt. 6:2, and Matt. 5:12. Seek in Christ first the kingdom of God and his righteousness, and be confident, he that for your salvation assumed a reasonable soul and human body, that he might suffer such things therein for your redemption, will not lose you again by leaving you destitute of temporal necessaries. See Rom. 8:32. By this examine whether you seek God for love of God, or out of self-love: do you patiently bear afflictions, in regard that God thereby tries you?
