Do you love and depend on God as well smiting you, as giving temporal blessings unto you? But if you be impatient, or desperate in your sufferings, as 2 Kings 6:33; Job 2:9, your seeking God has been for your own sake.

**27. ***{Labour not for the meat which perisheth}* Or *work not*, and not forbidding labor for things necessary to this life. See Gen. 2:15; Gen. 3:19; Gen. 9:20; 2 Thess. 3:10; Eph. 4:28, but preferring the care for things eternal; as Matt. 6:33. So Matt. 9:13, he excludes not sacrifice, but prefers mercy.

*{that meat which endureth unto life everlasting}* That is, those things which are appointed by God to refresh and sustain their souls to eternity.

*{for him hath}* Matt. 3:17. He shows why we must seek that food which perishes not but endures to everlasting life, in Christ only, there’s no other name given under heaven, the Father has assigned him only unto this office, him only has he commissioned hereto, by many signs and wonders, as it were under his own signet deputing him to that great office.

*{God the Father sealed}* Or *allowed and confirmed to the office of our redemption*: a simile derived from them who give commissions under hand and seal.

**28. ***{the works of God}* Pleasing to God, or which he requires, that we may thereby find that meat you speak of, remaining to eternity. Some new works they dreamed of, that they might find that Lord of eternal life: Christ spoke not of any works more than in the general; but they thought of something which they had not yet learned in the law, which must be done by them, that they might gain it, therefore they speak to this sense; we are ready and most willing to learn, if you please to teach us herein.

**29. ***{This is the work of God}* 1 John 3:23. His commandment and will: as also his work and gift, Eph. 2:8, that which God requires of you, that you should believe in me: having spoken of works and life eternal; lest they should imagine it could be merited or gained by the works of the law without Christ (for if so, Christ had died without cause or in vain, Gal. 2:21), he recalls them to faith in him, wherein only is eternal life.

**30. ***{What sign shewest thou}* This one thing manifested how little they profited by the miracles they saw: admiring his works, and thence being persuaded that he was the Messias, insomuch as they would have made him their king, yet now they require a sign, as if he had done nothing among them: Chrysostom here notes their stupidity, who having present signs, yet ask a sign: so inconsistent are the desires and votes of the giddy vulgar: because Christ would not comply with their fancies, and become such as they dreamed their Messias should be (but on the contrary reproved their sensuality and spiritual blindness), they contest as with one no further to be heard or followed: as if they had said, What show you, that we should thereby be persuaded to forsake Moses’ law, in which we and our fathers have been brought up; to follow you, and cast ourselves and the hope of our salvation on you? If Christ will not answer their desires, he shall no longer be a Master for them.

**31. ***{Our fathers did eat Manna}* Exod. 16:15; Num. 11:7. As if they said, under Moses’ conduct whom we yet follow, our fathers were fed forty years in the wilderness: what greater work do you, that we may cleave to you?

*{he gave them bread from heaven}* Wisdom 16:20. So is the manna called, Ps. 78:24, *דגן* *שמים* *dagan shamaim*, the corn of heaven. So Jerome after the Hebrew, *triticum coeli*: the 70 give it *ἄρτον* *οὐρανοῦ*, the bread of heaven; so it is called *לחם* *אבירים* *lechem abbirim*, 70, *ἄρτον* *ἀγγέλων*, the bread of angels. Hebr. the bread of the mighty or the strong. So Jerome after the Hebrew, *panem fortium*, not that the angels do eat, but because (say some) God by their ministry fed Israel therewith. Some understand *אלהים*, *elohim*, as if it were the *bread of the mighty God*, or as others, the bread of princes and potentates, the most excellent bread. See V. Shindler, in *אבר*. Jerome understands Christ (of whom the manna was a type), *ex hoc enim pane coeli* (says he) *sancti reficiuntur & angeli*; with this bread of heaven saints and angels are refreshed and fed; this *ἄρτον* *ἐκ* *τοῦ* *οὐρανοῦ*, understand as *ferae è sylvis* (i.e.) *sylvestris*. So Joh. Drus. *praeter.* lib. 4, because it distilled from the clouds, not that it was heavenly bread nourishing to eternal life; that Christ denies, affirming himself only to be that bread. So 1 Cor. 10.

**32. ***{Moses gave you not that bread}* Moses gave not your fathers, so much as that perishing bread the manna, which was for a temporal use, but God gave it; and he now gives you in me the bread of life.

*{the true bread from heaven}* That which being apprehended by faith shall nourish you to eternal life; here this *ἀληθινὸν*, is put in opposition to the type which their fathers did eat in the wilderness, in the type true; to typify, as the thing figured, so the figure; for that manna was a figure of Christ, that same *ἄρτος* *ζωοποιὸς*, *life-giving bread* (Nazianzen, orat. 35), truly but spiritually and mystically to feed our souls to eternal life.

**33. ***{He which cometh down from heaven}* That is Christ, v. 35.

**34. ***{evermore give us this bread}* Either they spoke ironically, and scoffingly, as the Samaritan, ch. 4:15. So Matt. 27:42, or they herein discover, that their minds were all on their bellies.

**35. ***{cometh unto me}* By true faith. See verse 36. This same *ὁ* *ἐρχόμενος* *πρὸς* *μὲ*, *cometh unto me*, imports an accession to (as of them that give their names to Christ by baptism) and a communion with, and participation of Christ, as they have who truly believe in him.

*{shall never hunger}* See ch. 4:24. In these terms, *shall never hunger—shall never thirst*, he shows them the vast difference between the manna and Christ: that satisfied the eaters for a little time, but this, forever; that nourished, and sustained the mortal body for a short season, as verse 49, but this shall sustain to all eternity.

**36. ***{ye also have seen me and believe not}* He notes their voluntary blindness (as Matt. 13:13, 14, 15), who sufficiently seeing the admirable effects of his divine power, yet would not believe in him: the curse was upon them which was threatened, Isaiah 6:9. So, *auribus apertis, surdi erant*, Augustin. They were deaf with open ears.

**37. ***{all that the Father giveth me}* All whom he elected, shall believe in Christ, and obey the Gospel, and so not perish. He prepossesses an objection which some might have made: behold we here present have heard and followed you; why appears not this effect, of believing in you in all of us? He first intimated that some of the multitude already did, and others afterward should believe, and therein have a blessed refreshing rest, and peace of conscience in Christ (Rom. 5:1, &c.). Secondly, he derived the matter unto the fountain, God’s free election to faith in, and salvation by Christ. See Rom. 8:29, 30.

**38. ***{I came down from heaven}* The deity could not descend by changing place, because he comprehends all, and can be comprehended of none; neither came his human body from heaven, but was truly of the seed of Abraham (see Heb. 2:16), but he is said to come down from heaven, in respect of his incarnation, wherein the deity united to the humanity, makes one person of Christ, God and man.

**39. ***{This is the Father’s will, &c.}* Because they understood not, what he meant by that will of God, for which he came from heaven, he expresses it here, to be, that he should save all the elect.

**40. ***{and believeth on him}* He here shows the means of salvation, faith; as Eph. 2:8, and who are they to whom the Father has given him; those only that believe in him: here look for your calling and election. See Rom. 8:30; 2 Pet. 1:10; 2 Cor. 1:22; Eph. 1:13.

**41. ***{murmured at him}* Because they understood the words of Christ carnally; when he filled their bellies, they had him in the opinion of their long expected Messias, they would have then made him their king: but when he tells them the truth, and teaches them divine and heavenly things, they murmur and start back: observe here the seed and beginning of their fearful apostasy and falling from Christ; it was murmuring at the truth through unbelief: it is commonly so still.

**42. ***{Is not this Jesus the son of Joseph?}* See Matt. 13:55; Luke 4:22. Because they expected a magnificent and externally glorious Messias; they were offended at Christ in the form of a servant, and man of sorrows, though so coming to save them: and probably they understood him, as if he had said, that he was not born in this world among men, but came down as an angel from heaven; they knew not that he was truly God, as truly man: so perverse is carnal reason; that which should have been an argument of gratitude (that he vouchsafed to make himself of no reputation, to honor them with the title of the sons of God), they make the ground of their impious contempt of him.

**43. ***{murmur not among yourselves}* As if he had said, my words contain no matter of scandal; the fault is in yourselves, who can neither believe, nor understand them; because God has justly given you up to spiritual blindness and unbelief, for your pride and obstinacy.

**44. ***{No man can come to me}* No man can understand and believe these things, except God teach, enlighten, and give him faith; no man can know God, *nisi Deo docente*; that is, *sine Deo, non cognosci Deum*. Irenaeus l. 4, c. 14.

*{except the Father, &c.}* As if he said, no marvel that you believe not; your bellies, not my Father, drew you hither; *to come to Christ*, is here, to believe on him, and to depend on him; as verse 35. By *drawing*, he means illuminating; and framing men’s hearts to faith and obedience; which God does by the Holy Spirit inwardly, working in his word and ordinance, as verse 45. So true is that of Prosper, *quod duce Deo, veniatur ad Deum*, *de vocat. Gent.* l. 1, c. 24. God leading us, we come to God: here then is left no place for pride or contempt of others; for as the same well says, q. 5, c. 25, *tales fuerunt, qui sunt attracti, quales hi, qui in suâ duritie sunt relicti*: they who are drawn (to Christ by the Father) were such as these who are left in their hardness of heart.

**45. ***{it is written}* Isaiah 54:13; Jer. 31:34. Lest they should think (as perverse men are ever apt to do) that all must needs be new doctrine which they understood not, he shows what he had affirmed out of the prophets; and withal, the reason why the perverse hearers came not to him; because the Spirit of God taught them not, neither moved their hearts to faith and obedience.

**46. ***{not that any man hath seen the Father}* Meaning, you may not look for a call from heaven, or vision of God, by whom you should be admonished to believe in me; he calls by me, and by his Spirit: thus speaks he to a carnal people, lest they should mistake him, as in the rest they did. See 1 Cor. 2:10; Matt. 11:27; John 1:18.

**47. ***{hath everlasting life}* He thus speaks in respect of the certainty of God’s election, and the seals thereof in our regeneration: they have eternal life here begun, to be accomplished in the world to come.

**48. ***{I am the bread of life}* In the participation whereof only, is eternal life, and salvation.

**49. ***{and are dead}* Because it is the shadow, or figure, but Christ the substance, which can nourish to eternal life.

**50. ***{this is the bread}* Meaning himself; of whom he speaks here in the third person comparing himself with the manna; the manna could not save any of the eaters thereof from death: but Christ only saves us from death eternal, the second death damnation.

**51. ***{the living bread}* Which lives and gives life to the world. That is, all the elect; see for the better understanding hereof, and of that which follows, Augustin. to. 3, *de doctr. Christian.* l. 3, c. 5, 10, 16—where he says, *Si—flagitium aut facinus videtur jubere*, it (the Scripture) seems to command some impious or wicked act,—it is there a figurative speech, *Except*, says he, *ye eat the flesh of the son of man, and drink his blood, ye have no life in you*: *it seems to command an impious and wicked act, therefore it is a figure, commanding, or teaching us to communicate of the passion of the Lord, and sweetly and profitably to lay up in memory, that his flesh was crucified and wounded for us*.

*{If any man eat of this bread}* If any man believe in Christ: for as Augustin says, tract. 26 in Joh., *credere enim in eum, est manducare panem vivum*: for to believe, is to eat the living bread; and again says the same, *qui manducat intus, non foris, qui manducat in corde, non qui premit dente*: he that eats within, not without, he that eats with (or in) his heart, not he that crushes it with his teeth.

**52. ***{how can this man give us, &c.}* They understood that carnally, which he spoke in a spiritual sense.

**53. ***{except ye eat the flesh}* Except you spiritually apprehend Christ by faith. See on verse 51.

**54. ***{whoso eateth my flesh}* By faith: for properly, and carnally, we cannot eat living bread, and such is Christ, verse 51, nor could that bread which came down from heaven, be that flesh of Christ carnally to be eaten; but whole Christ, God and man (see on verse 38), the bread of life spiritually to be received.

**55. ***{is meat indeed}* Or *truly*, but spiritually; for that which is spiritually so is truly so.

**56. ***{dwelleth in me}* This shows it to be meant of a mystical and spiritual eating; because to eat his flesh, is to dwell in him; and that as bodies are sustained with meat and drink, so are our souls nourished with the body and blood of Christ.

**57. ***{he that eateth me}* That believes in me. See on verse 51 and 56, and on verse 54.

*{even he shall live by me}* For though faith (by which the just shall live) looks straight on the flesh of Christ crucified; yet it well knows that the power of the merit saving us, depends on the dignity of the person crucified, who is truly God and man; therefore Acts 20:28, the apostle says concerning the church, that *God had purchased it with his own blood*: so 1 John 1:7, *the blood of Jesus Christ his Son cleanses us from all sin*. So 1 Cor. 2:8, they would not have *crucified the Lord of glory*.

**58. ***{which came down from heaven}* As verse 50. See on verse 38.

*{not as your Fathers did eat Manna}* It shall not be with him that feeds on me by faith, as with your fathers in the wilderness, when they had eaten manna, they yet died; but he that believes in me shall be saved, to eternal life.

**59. ***{in the synagogue}* Openly in the church or place where the Jews weekly assembled for the public worship of God.

**60. ***{This is a hard saying}* *σκληρός*, troublesome, or irksome to be heard; because they took Christ’s words in a literal sense, hard to be understood, says Chrysostom, hom. 46.

*{who can hear it}* Who can endure to hear it? Who would not be offended at it? To eat the flesh and drink the blood of man, is unnatural and therefore the more stupid they to take those words literally; mark here the wretched effects of careless hearing and misunderstanding the word of God.

**61. ***{doth this offend you}* Probably he spoke this to them after he came out of the synagogue, and heard their murmurings, and censuring his sermon.

**62. ***{What and if}* Ch. 3:13.

*{ascend up where he was before}* Are you troubled that I attribute such virtue to the eating my flesh? That I affirm it to be the bread of life which came from heaven? You shall see me shortly put off this assumed state of humiliation, and be exalted above the heavens. See Rom. 1:4; ch. 3:13; Eph. 4:9. They thought he would give them his body, carnally to eat, but he affirmed (says Augustin) *se ascensurum in coelum, utique integrum*: that he would ascend into heaven, and that entire: then should they know how he gives his body, *quia gratia ejus non consumitur morsibus*: and that his grace is not eaten by bites: that they should see the Son of man ascend: understand it of the witnesses thereto appointed: possibly some of them who now murmured might be some of them: these words, *where he was before*: understand according to his deity: that the Son of man should be seen ascending where he was before: understand by considering the usual communication of properties, whereby that which is proper to either nature, is attributed to the whole person of Christ: so it is true: Christ suffered, his humanity did, his deity could not: so the Son of man ascended where he was before: his deity was in heaven before, but his humanity then first arrived in heaven, when after his resurrection he ascended up into glory.

**63. ***{it is the spirit that quickeneth}* You must understand these things spiritually, not carnally; for they are spiritual (as Chrysostom interprets) and the Spirit united to the human nature gives life.

*{the flesh profiteth nothing}* That is (says Augustine) *sola caro*: the flesh only, as the apostle says, *knowledge puffs up*, that is, *sola, sine charitate*, if it be alone without charity.

**64. ***{some of you that believe not}* He says not, who understand not these things; but he tells them the cause of their misunderstanding, which was unbelief: he taxes not all; lest if any present were curable, they might otherwise have been cast down to despair.

*{for Jesus knew}* This the evangelist inserts, lest any should think that Christ rashly censured them of unbelief: many hypocritically followed him, which though men knew not, Christ well knew.

*{and who should betray him}* Who would betray him, as if he said, he did not only know all things past and present, but also all things to come.

**65. ***{therefore I said unto you}* He repeats what he said (verse 44), with application to the unbelievers: as if he said; therefore are you offended through unbelief, because my Father has not given you faith to believe in me to your salvation: you are joined in bodily presence, but not in spirit; not in faith.

**66. ***{from that time}* *ἐκ* *τούτου*, the Syriac has it; *from that word*; for those things which they could not understand concerning eating his flesh, and drinking his blood; many who had given their names to him deserted him: thus was the prophecy fulfilled, which foretold that he should be a rock of offense, Isaiah 8:14. See 2 Tim. 2:15; 1 John 2:19; 1 Cor. 11:19. No wonder if many are scandalized at our doctrine, and separate from our ministry; they that were not elected, were offended and separated from Christ.

**67. ***{Will ye also go away}* Which he said not that he was ignorant of the future (for he knew all from the beginning, ver. 64), but to occasion a clearer confession of Peter in the name of the rest, and to propose his, as an example of constancy: also to admonish them not to join themselves with apostates and separatists, which none shall do that are grounded in Christ: and not to follow multitudes to evil and forsaking of Christ. It may teach us not to be offended, when many fall away, who seemed heretofore very zealous followers of Christ, and professors of truth: for as multitudes can give no patronage to sinners; so neither shall the paucity of believers hurt the faithful: they only who continue to the end shall be saved; that is, they only to whom God gives perseverance; for (as Augustine says, tom. 7, *respons. ad art. fals.* 8), *It is the will of God, that one should continue in a good will, who also before he be forsaken forsakes no man, and converts many to him, who had forsaken him*; he says, *will ye also?* It is in our power to fall; but not to rise again: that we do not fall away, it is only God’s mercy preserving us; and Prosper says well, *de vocat. Gent.* lib. 2, cap. 21, *Ideo liberum esse discedere, ut non discessisse sit praemium*, therefore it is free to depart, that it may be a reward not to have departed.

**68. ***{the words of eternal life}* The Gospel teaching us all the mysteries of eternal life and salvation, which if we believe and obey, we shall have eternal life through the merits of Jesus Christ, whom the Gospel declares unto us. See ch. 3:16; Rom. 1:16, &c.

**69. ***{and believe}* See Matt. 16:16. See ch. 1:14; 1 John 1:1.

**70. ***{Have not I chosen you, &c.}* We must understand the one party to be chosen *per misericordiam*, and the other *per judicium*, as Augustine says, *de cor. & gra.* cap. 7, to. 7. Those he chose to preach, and obtain his kingdom; but this to shed his blood.

*{is a devil}* A malicious wicked man, an informer, a traitor, possessed and put on by the evil spirit: this he foretells, that they might not be scandalized, when they should see his end.

**71. ***{Judas Iscariot}* *Ἰσκαριώτην*. Hebr. *איש* *קריות* *ish Kirioth*; Jerome’s first conjecture is probable, *Iscariotes vel à vico, aut urbe in quâ ortus est, &c.* (wherein Chrysostom, hom. 33 in Matt., agrees with him). Cariot is a town in the tribe of Judah, Josh. 15:25. Some therefore will have him so called, as if it were said, Judas a man of Cariot. Some derive the name from that which signifies defection, for a reward.

## CHAPTER 7

**1. ***{Walked in Galilee}* Went about from place to place, to preach the Gospel, and to heal the sick for confirmation thereof.

**2. ***{feast of tabernacles}* Lev. 23:34, 42, 43. In this feast, they were to dwell seven days in booths and tents, in remembrance of their dwelling in tents in the wilderness, Exod. 23:16. It is called *חג* *האסף* *chag haasiph*, the feast of gathering fruits, and *חג* *הסכות* *chag hassuccoth*, the feast of coverings or tabernacles, 70, *ἑορτὴ* *σκηνῶν*; *σκηνοπηγία*: Chrysostom reckons it five months after the Passover; but Lev. 23:34, ib. verse 5, it appears six.

**3. ***{that thy disciples}* That is, those whom you would have to follow you: for his disciples who now did follow him, had seen his miracles: this they said probably in derision: for in the fifth verse it appears that they believed not in him: faith is not by any carnal prerogative, but a special gift of God, ch. 1:13.

**4. ***{shew thyself to the world}* Go up to the feast, and do your miracles in such great confluences of people, that thereby your name may be spread abroad to all other nations also.

**5. ***{Neither did his brethren believe}* His kindred. See on verse 3.

**6. ***{My time is not yet come}* My opportunity is not yet. So verse 8, for the word here used is *καιρὸς*, that is, *opportunitas temporis*, or *tempus constitutum* (as Luke 20:10; Rom. 5:6), that is, as Theophylact interprets, *ὁ* *τοῦ* *σταυροῦ* *καὶ* *θανάτου*, of my death and passion, before which he carefully avoided the malicious violence of the Jews, that he might perform all those things which concerned our redemption, confirmation of our faith, and salvation; but when the appointed time was come, he went before his disciples toward Jerusalem, where he well saw, and foretold that he must suffer, as willingly offering himself to death, in order to his Father’s will, and our salvation.

**7. ***{the world cannot hate you}* That is, the unregenerate do not hate you, because you are of them; the world loves her own: the priests and Pharisees do not persecute you, because you communicate with them in their unbelief, they embrace you as their obedient children: me they hate, because I reprove their sins. So dangerous a vice is pride, it can abide no reproof: so impossible is it to have peace with the world, except we will share with them in their wickedness; and impossible it is to preach the truth sincerely, without the malice of impious men, whose sins we must reprove. The Jews pretended that they were angry with Christ, only because he profaned the Sabbath, &c., but the truth was, it was because he discovered, and spoke against their sin and hypocrisy; for he profaned not the Sabbath.

**8. ***{Go ye up unto the feast}* This says he, justly leaving them to themselves, who would not believe in him. See Ps. 81:12.

*{I go not up yet}* *Ἐγὼ* *οὔπω* *ἀναβαίνω*, *I do not yet go up*: see the reason verse 6. My opportunity is not yet come. I do not go up with you, who believe not in me.

*{for my time, &c.}* Ch. 8:20, and verse 6.

**9. ***{He abode still in Galilee}* He stayed a while after they were gone up to the feast, until he knew it fit time.

**10. ***{but when his brethren were gone up}* The Galileans were wont to go up to those solemnities, by kindreds, and companies of neighbors, Luke 2:44. Christ would not now go up so publicly; but when he knew that the malice of the Jews was something more allayed, then he went up privately with his disciples, and some few followers.

**11. ***{the Jews sought him}* That is, the people who had formerly heard him, seen his miracles, or heard of him: and possibly the high priests and rulers of the Jews, who were the main sticklers against Christ, these studying by all means to destroy him, laid out, and inquired for him, being confident they should have him at the feast: God appointed these solemn festivities, as for thankful remembrance of his mercies showed them; so also for occasions of their public rejoicing therein, and the maintenance of love, charity and unity among them; but they herein took occasion to lay wait for blood, and to persecute their Savior: such is the impious perverseness of men; the devil is never more constantly served, than on the Lord’s day.

**12. ***{much murmuring}* *γογγυσμός*, whispering, or secret talking concerning him, muttering, some maliciously blaspheming him; some praising and defending his parts, but secretly, for fear of their malicious rulers and high priests.

**13. ***{for fear of the Jews}* No man spoke well openly, for fear of the rulers and priests. See ch. 9:22. Nor possibly durst they speak evil freely and publicly, for fear of the people.

**14. ***{about the midst of the feast}* About the fourth day of this feast, verse 2. Now was his time to preach the Gospel: what he now preached the evangelist records not: it was some divine learning, and to the admiration of the auditors, as appears, verse 15.

**15. ***{How knoweth this man letters}* That is, *τὰ* *ἱερὰ* *γράμματα*, the Holy Scriptures, as 2 Tim. 3:15. They would not in their unbelief acknowledge him to be the Messias of whom the prophet Isaiah 11:2, 3 said, *the spirit of the Lord shall rest upon him, the spirit of wisdom and understanding, the spirit of knowledge, &c., and shall make him of quick understanding in the fear of the Lord*. Which the apostle also teaches, Col. 2:3. They admiring the doctrine, and knowing that he had it not by ordinary and usual means, should have concluded that it was divine, and immediately from God, and so have acknowledged the evidence of the Spirit therein: but so miserable a blindness does unbelief bring with it, that all (the most indisputable) arguments, are there retorted and perverted, to the destruction of the wicked. See Acts 2:17; Mark 6:3; Matt. 13:55. Neither is here any patronage for those who neglect or despise learning. See 1 Tim. 4:13, 15, 16; Titus 1:5, 9.

**16. ***{My doctrine is not mine}* He speaks, as he is man: I learned it not of man, but of God: and here accommodates his answer to the Jews’ opinion of him, who thought him to be mere man; but it was the truth and doctrine of God; so there is but one truth of God the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost, which Christ God and man preached: this *οὐκ* *ἔστιν* *ἐμή*, *is not in me*, is thus to be understood: I invent no new thing, I speak the eternal truth of God whose messenger I am, the only mediator between God and man (1 Tim. 1:5). I speak the doctrine and word of him, who sent me so to speak.

**17. ***{If any man will do his Will}* He secretly touches the cause of their misunderstanding his doctrine that is their want of the true fear of God and obedience to him (see ch. 14:15, 16, 17, 21, 23). Take away your doubting anger, envy and vain hatred towards me, *καὶ* *οὐδὲν* *τὸ* *κωλῦον* *ὑμᾶς*, nothing shall let you to know this, that my words are the words of God. Chrysostom.

*{whether I speak of myself}* See ch. 14:24. The meaning is, if any man truly fear God, and will indeed set his heart to believe and obey him, he shall be satisfied, and clearly resolved, of the divine truth of the Gospel: it must be a sound and well affected palate which must judge of tastes, the sick and disaffected cannot: the precepts of righteousness are bitter to evil livers. Lactantius l. 1. So also Jerome l. 1, ep. 16, *ita se natura habet ut amara sit veritas*; it comes naturally to pass that truth is bitter; that is, to the wicked. See 1 Cor. 2. Here Christ intimates that the Jews were incompetent judges of his doctrine, because their malice and wickedness had blinded them.

**18. ***{he speaketh of himself}* He who teaches his own inventions, affects the praise of men.

**19. ***{did not Moses}* Exod. 24:3. He answers their objection concerning healing on the Sabbath, by their own practice in that they circumcised on the Sabbath, v. 22, 23.

*{why go ye about to kill me?}* Ch. 5:18. This speech he addressed to the priests and governors, watching their opportunity, and taking counsel how to destroy him, as a profaner of the Sabbath; this interrogation is very emphatical; as if he had said, how unjust are you who would kill me as a violator of God’s law, profaner of the Sabbath? Suppose that were so, that all work on the Sabbath, were profanation of the Sabbath: how unjustly do you condemn that in me which yourselves both do, and allow for good? You circumcise on the Sabbath, when the eighth day, on which circumcision was to be administered, fell on the Sabbath day: is not circumcision more near to the nature of a servile work, than without any more labor to cure by speaking the word? Must (in your sense) a precept of the moral law give place to a ceremonial? Must God’s law give place that Moses’ law may be fulfilled by your circumcising an infant, and may not the ground and sum of the law, charity and showing mercy, be practiced on the Sabbath?

**20. ***{thou hast a Devil}* This seemed a common manner of speaking among the Jews, when they would express a man furiously distracted, or dangerously erring, which they blasphemously apply to Christ.

**21. ***{and ye all marvel}* Because I did it on the Sabbath day, *θορυβεῖσθε*, *ταράττεσθε*, Chrysostom, you clamor and are troubled.

**22. ***{Moses therefore, &c.}* Lev. 12:3. But indeed Moses did not first prescribe the law of circumcision, he received and prescribed therein only that which God commanded Abraham and his posterity to do in their generations, until Christ the end of the law to every believer, should come. See Gal. 5:2, &c.

*{but of the fathers}* Gen. 17:10. Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, and the fathers before Moses.

**23. ***{that the law of Moses should not be broken}* Or *without breaking the Law of Moses*. The law given by the ministry of Moses.

**24. ***{judge not, &c.}* Deut. 1:16. Judge me not according to any outward appearance to be mere man; but consider the divine power in my works, which you calumniate: he calls that righteous judgment which is without respect of persons, according to truth and equity; in which the Jews in these their rash and blasphemous censures, vehemently erred; supposing that all was right which they did, however erroneous; and all evil and censurable, which Christ did, who neither did nor could err anything: their ground being, because they, the high priests, scribes and Pharisees were in prime authority and honor among men, and Christ in his state of humiliation and form of a servant, seemed to them but a mere, despicable, poor man: so they valued not the truth, and equity of things, and how agreeable they were to God’s law, but of what authority and dignity the speakers or actors were, with men.

**25. ***{then said some}* They wondered that the authority of the rulers could not yet prevail against him, but yet acknowledged the power of God therein, that they could not with all their power silence him.

**26. ***{do the rulers know indeed, &c.}* Do our high priests and elders certainly know that he is the promised Messias, and therefore suffer him thus publicly and freely to control and reprove them? If not, how easily might they take him; so might it seem to men, who considered not herewith the coercive power of God limiting their fury, until the appointed time, that Jesus was to suffer for our redemption.

**27. ***{no man knoweth}* This tradition seemed to have beginning from some misunderstood prophecies, as Isaiah 53:8.

**28. ***{whom ye know not}* He upbraids their confidence of knowing him, seeing they know not his Father, nor his unspeakable generation: you know of what human parents, of what family, as man, I am; but you know not him that sent me to be *a minister of the Circumcision for the truth of God, to confirm the promises made unto the fathers*, Rom. 15:8.

**29. ***{but I know him}* As if he said, you think you know me and my Father, but you are deceived, not knowing that I am coequal and coeternal with God the Father; and that I took not this honor of being Mediator and Savior; but that in the fullness of time, by my incarnation, came I into this world, appointed thereto by God. See Heb. 5:4, 5, and ch. 1:18; Matt. 11:27.

**30. ***{but no man laid hand on him}* Because the issues of life and death, with the time and manner, are only in God’s hand: see how their malicious fury is bridled, as Chrysostom said, *ἀοράτως*, invisibly, by a secret and unseen hand of divine providence: so God still protects his, restraining the precipitate malice of the enemy.

**31. ***{many of the people believed}* Though many were hardened in their obstinate unbelief, yet the word of God was not without good effect with others, whose hearts he had opened. It is one and the same sun, which gives light to sound eyes, but blinds the infirm; one and the same truth preached, hardens and more blinds some hearers, and causes others to believe and obey to salvation, Nazianzen, orat. 17. It seems by their speech, that Christ wrought many miracles among the people, which the evangelist mentions not.

*{more miracles}* This evangelist speaks not of many besides (the turning of water into wine, ch. 2, the discovery of the Samaritan’s sins, ch. 4, and the healing the ruler’s son: curing the paralytical, ch. 5, feeding 5,000 with five loaves; &c. John 6, his walking on the sea), therefore probably many more are omitted. See ch. 20.

**32. ***{Pharisees and chief priests sent officers}* These desiring to appear the chief zealots, most opposed Christ, envying the people’s good opinion of him and conceiving their own honor eclipsed thereby; this vexing them, they break out into open violence; the usual armor of hypocrites, where they cannot prevail with arguments: thus is authority (appointed to defend truth) abused to impious tyranny. See Acts 22:24.

**33. ***{yet a little while}* Till the appointed time, I shall preach and do my Father’s will, whether you will or no.

**34. ***{ye shall seek me}* Ch. 13:33. In your extreme calamities shortly to come upon you, you shall wish me with you again, but I shall return to him that sent me, and be secure from your malicious inquisition.

**35. ***{among the Gentiles}* Or *Greeks*. Will he go teach the Jews who are scattered here and there, among the Gentiles? Or possibly they might here mean the Gentiles in general; for so they sometimes called all nations, which were not of the race of the Jews. See Rom. 1:16; Rom. 2:9, 10 and 3:9 and 10:12, &c.

**36. ***{what manner of saying is this}* What means he hereby? Such a *quaere* was among the disciples, ch. 16:17, 18, only that there was a desire to be informed of the truth, wherein Christ satisfied them: but here is a captious agitation of Christ’s words, without any serious care to understand their scope and meaning, therefore Christ here leaves them in suspense.

**37. ***{In the last day, that great day, &c.}* Wherein they were to have a solemn assembly, and to abstain from all servile works. See Lev. 23:36. The choice of this time imported, the end of those ceremonies was to bring men to Christ; and now being to depart home, he thus gave them to carry with them *ἐφόδια* *πρὸς* *σωτηρίαν*, *viands to salvation*, as Chrysostom notes.

*{Jesus stood and cried}* *ἔκραξε*: he spoke with a loud voice, that the multitude might hear him, and to show his authority and liberty of speaking to the people. The occasion of this speech concerning water, might possibly be that which some write; that the Jews went on this day with great solemnity, to draw water out of the fountain of Siloam, at the foot of Mount Sion, and to bring it to the altar, singing out that of Isaiah 12:3, *With joy shall ye draw water out of the wells of salvation, &c.* By this and many other traditions, they having perverted the law (which commanded a spiritual rejoicing) into superstitious clamors, Christ cried out, to recall them from earthly to heavenly waters.

**38. ***{he that believeth}* He cites not here any particular place of Scripture; but this may be gathered out of Scriptures, where mention is made of the effusion of the gifts of the Holy Ghost, as Joel 2:28; Isaiah 44:3 and 55:1, 11. So Acts 2:18 is interpreted: therefore the Syriac interpreter gives it, *as the Scriptures have said*.

**39. ***{this spake he of the Spirit}* The evangelist interprets the words of Jesus, taken out of—Joel 2:28; Isaiah 44:3.

*{the Holy Ghost}* The visible graces of God’s Spirit, spoken of by the forecited prophets, which did accordingly flourish in the church after the ascension of Christ. These several gifts were all the effects of one and the same Spirit (Eph. 4:4, 7, &c.), and therefore were usually called *the holy Ghost*, Acts 2:4, and 4:8, 31, and 6:3, 5, and 7:55, and 8:15, 18, and 10:44, 47, and 11:15, and 19:2, &c. As there is but one kind of water distilling on the herbs in rain, which in the effects thereof admirably varies in the production of fruits, plants and flowers, it showing itself (as Cyril, cat. 16, expresses it) *λευκὸς* *μὲν* *ἐν* *κρίνῳ*, *ἐρυθρὸς* *δὲ* *ἐν* *ῥόδῳ*, *white in Lily, but red in the Rose*: so is there but one Holy Ghost working varieties of gifts in men, for the edification of the church.

**40. ***{This is the Prophet}* Deut. 18:15, &c. See ch. 1:21.

**41. ***{but some said, Shall Christ come out of Galilee}* Such is the condition of truth, it ever meets with some opposers: had not their envy blinded them, they might have found that Christ was born at Bethlehem, and of the lineage of David: the wicked are quick-sighted to find out arguments from perverted and misunderstood Scriptures to strengthen their unbelief; but slow and dull to learn the truth, this is Satan’s malice, and men’s unhappy weakness to turn that to destruction which God in mercy gave for comfort and salvation.

**42. ***{hath not the Scripture said}* Matt. 2:5; Mic. 5:2. See also Ps. 132:11. Had not malice and envy blinded them, they might easily have been satisfied in this scruple: had they but inquired with a mind to believe; doubtless Jesus would not have concealed it from them: had they but called to mind, the wise men from the East, their inquiry, Herod’s rage against the poor infants, John Baptist’s testimony, &c., these or any of these might have satisfied them; but how little a thing will make them stumble, who are ready and willing to fall?

**43. ***{so there was a division}* *Σχίσμα*, a schism, or division, or rent. Augustine, tom. 7, *contr. Crescon. Gram.* l. 2, c. 6, intimates that a schism is, *Recens congregationis, ex aliqua sententiarum diversitate dissensio*, a new or late sprung-up dissension of the congregation or church, out of some diversity of opinions. A heresy he says is, *Schisma inveteratum*, an old schism.

*{because of him}* No wonder if there be schisms now among us, seeing, in the sacred presence of Christ, and when himself preached the most infallible truth of the Gospel, there were schisms, and divisions. See Matt. 10:5. The cause is in the secret corruption of men’s hearts, not in the Gospel of peace.

**44. ***{but no man laid hands on him}* See v. 30. Superstition is ever cruel, but God’s providence overrules all.

**45. ***{why have ye not brought him}* These greedy wolves were so impatient, that they were angry with their messengers, returning without him, not hearing or regarding why they took him not.

**46. ***{never man spake, &c.}* They admired the grace in his lips, Ps. 45:2. So Matt. 7:28, 29, but malice makes men so blind and stupid, that they cannot be sensible of any the most excellent graces in those they hate. See 1 Kings 22:8; 1 John 3:12. It is natural to judge them evil whom we hate: but such is the power of God, they who went with purpose to take him, were taken with his excellency; their malice being turned to love and admiration, and they made proclaimers of his divine excellency. See 1 Sam. 19:20.

**47. ***{are ye also deceived}* Blinded with envy, they would not admit that Jesus could preach anything worthy of admiration, therefore they conclude that they were beguiled by his fair speeches: pride ever thinks it cannot err, and that rather all the world is dark, than itself blind.

**48. ***{have any of, &c.}* Thus they allege man’s authority against God’s: supposing that the chair of rulers and Pharisees had infallibility entailed on it.

**49. ***{are cursed}* It was their high ambition which made them conceive all men bound to follow them, though to destruction, and a proud opinion of themselves and their authority, caused them to despise and curse all others dissenting from their impiety and unbelief. It is a dangerous paradox which Card. Bellarmine holds, *de Roman. pontif.* lib. 4, cap. 5, *Si autem Papa erraret praecipiendo vitia, vel prohibendo virtutes, teneretur Ecclesia credere vitia esse bona, & virtutes malas*—but if the pope should err by commanding vices, or forbidding virtues, the church were bound to believe that vices were good, and virtues evil: and an uncharitable presumption of the Council of Trent, to seal up their many canons with so many anathemas to them who dissent from their errors. See *Concil. Tridentin. concil.* tom. 9, Severin. Bin. See more on v. 51.

**50. ***{he that came to Jesus by night}* Ch. 3:2.

*{one of them}* A Pharisee, and probably one of their Sanhedrin, or great council.

**51. ***{doth our Law judge any man before, &c.}* Does our law condemn? See Deut. 17:8, 10 and 19:15. Their part it was to have instructed the people, not to upbraid their ignorance; and to pray for them and bless them, not to judge, damn and curse them: *Quid temerarius Dei tribunal ascendis?* says Optatus, l. 2. *Who art thou that judgest another man’s servant? To his own master he standeth or falleth*, Rom. 14:4. It was Constantine’s answer to the Donatists; *Petitis à me in seculo judicium, cum ego ipse Christi judicium expectem*; you desire judgment of me in this world, whereas I myself expect the judgment of Christ. So Rom. 14:10, *We shall all stand before the judgment seat of Christ*. Those whom God has set in authority, may and ought to judge of secular businesses; but of man’s final estate, Christ only is to judge. That judge which pronounces before he has heard both parties, is unjust, even where he gives a just sentence; but to condemn any man unheard, is an injustice, against which, the laws of God and nature caution; as also to execute any man uncondemned. See Salvian, *de gub. D.* lib. 8, end. Tertull. *Apol.* cap. 1. Lactant. *divin. Instit.* l. 5, cap. 1. See also Acts 25:16. Possibly Nicodemus said this, not only to dissuade them from such injustice, but also in hope, that if they would but hear Jesus, they should either be converted or convicted. Indeed, as even Julian could say in the cause of Numerius, who can be innocent if it be enough to accuse? Ammian. Marcellin. lib. 18.

**52. ***{art thou also of Galilee}* By way of reproach and contempt they named Galilee, though possibly they knew him to be no Galilean.

*{search and look}* They bid Nicodemus go learn, as intimating his ignorance, or reproving his incogitancy.

*{ariseth no Prophet}* They would have no man think Jesus so much as a prophet; therefore would they prescribe God’s power by custom. So slender a ground had they, as commonly all schismatics and heretics have, which is, at least to beg one principle, and to take some falsehood for granted, on which they would build the rest. As here: first, they make no question but that Jesus is a Galilean. Secondly, they conclude, that because God never had raised any prophet thence, therefore, he never would. The first was false, for Jesus was of Bethlehem, and the second in their sense not true: for God called thence divers eminent apostles; and so their superstructions were all false and impious; as that they ought not to follow Christ: that they who did, were ignorant of the law, and cursed: that they who were in authority, ought to restrain them by excommunication, or other punishments due to heretics, and to persecute Christ, and those that believed in him. Thus one absurdity or falsehood granted, many more will follow.

**53. ***{every man went, &c.}* Divided in opinion, and confounded in counsel, they could effect nothing until the time by God appointed. See Acts 28:29. Being at present frustrate of their hope to apprehend and destroy Christ; in great indignation they dissolved their malignant council, laying aside, not leaving their blasphemous malice.

## CHAPTER 8

**1. ***{The mount of Olives}* A hill near Jerusalem, on the east, so called, for the abundance of olives there growing: it was there Christ used to be in the night, Luke 21:37.

**2. ***{early in the morning}* Here is described the people’s diligence to hear the word, as Luke 21:38, as also Christ’s zeal to preach the same, that he might instruct and save them, and herein appears how little he was discouraged in his preaching the truth, by the blasphemous cavils of scribes and Pharisees: leaving his faithful ministers an example of diligence and constancy in their charge and ministration: if you will indeed be a servant of Christ, and preach the truth, look for contradiction; but let it not move you, be you constant, who know the reward.

*{all the people}* *πᾶς*, All, that is, many who had formerly heard him teaching in the feast of tabernacles, ch. 7:14, 37. Some of these came to be edified, and some to cavil.

**3. ***{and the scribes, &c.}* See on Luke 20:1. They had power to have censured her in their consistory, unto stripes, though the Romans permitted them not power of judicature in matters capital, but they brought her to Jesus that they might have whereof to accuse him: see v. 6.

*{brought unto him a woman taken, &c.}* Possibly the man (equally faulty, and liable to the same punishment, Lev. 20:10; Deut. 22:22) had escaped from them.

**4. ***{this woman, &c.}* They call him Master; now they meant to entangle him, as Matt. 22:16, whom they used to style a seducer of the people; and they who were judges, here act the part of accusers, which is against the course of equity.

**5. ***{now Moses in the Law}* Lev. 20:10, &c. They could remember and urge the law against others’ sin, but could not apply that to themselves which it says against injustice; to wit, condemning without cause, lawful witness, or hearing the parties accused. Concerning the law by them here mentioned; we must know, that it was so severe, that evil might be taken away from the land, and to terrify the sinners, by making offenders exemplary: see Num. 25:11. Sin is not known in impunity: that which they here did, was with purpose to ensnare Christ; they thinking, if he absolved her, he should appear to the people as an adversary to the law of God, and so not the Messias, but an impostor liable to a severe censure: but if he condemned her, he should seem to annul the very purpose of his coming, who professed to save them, and to propose grace and mercy, Ambros. ep. 76.

**6. ***{that they might have to accuse him}* As a breaker of Moses’ law, if he acquitted her, as they conceived he would, because he showed mercy to sinners; and, as they pretended, taught concerning the Sabbath, contrary to Moses, they presumed by their conceived dilemma to have taken him; but how vain is the wisdom of man, against the truth and wisdom of God, may appear in this example. See 1 Cor. 1:19, 20.

*{wrote on the ground}* Some think he wrote that which he answered, or some more secret things concerning the consciences of the informers: but seeing the Scripture speaks not what he wrote, we must not curiously inquire. Saint Ambrose, lib. 9, epist. 76, says, he wrote on the ground with his finger, who wrote the law on the tables of stone. For the rest we may say with Augustine, epist. 121, cap. 15, Init. *Est ergo in nobis, quaedam, ut ita dicam, docta ignorantia*: there is therefore in us, that I may so speak, a kind of learned ignorance: and as Jerome, *Melius est aliquid nescire securè, quam cum periculo discere*: it is better to be securely ignorant of something, than to learn it with danger.

**7. ***{he that is without sin}* See Deut. 17:7. As if he had said, she is guilty to be condemned; but you that accuse others look home, examine your own consciences whether you are not guilty of the like, or as great sins. *Quomodo enim feras alieni ultorem, & proprii criminis defensorem?—Nonne se magis ipse condemnat, qui in alio damnat, quod ipse committit?* Ambrose qu. s. How could you endure a revenger of another’s faults, and a defender of his own?—Does not he more condemn himself, who condemns that in another, which he commits himself? The Jews urged him again and again (while he seemed to slight their information), confident in his forbearance to answer, that they had now caught him in an unavoidable difficulty whatever he answered: Christ therefore replied with due respect to the law, maintaining the equity thereof: so answering, that they could not calumniate him for absolving the sinner, nor object against his doctrine of grace and mercy, freely justifying sinners; thus retorting the calumny, and striking the sleepy consciences of the malapert accusers, who vainly insulted, as if others’ vices were their virtues: teaching all who will be accusers of their brethren to begin at home; for as Gregory Nazianzen said, epist. 16, *Caesario fratri*: how shall they reprove others’ sin, who have not *ἀφ*’ *ἰδίας* *τὴν* *παῤῥησίαν*, that is, confidence in their own innocency? But as the most ravenous and hurtful birds, are commonly most quick-sighted; so are the most wicked most ready and skillful to find out others’ faults: the eye sees not itself, nor anything which is too near it, so neither see we our own faults, except set at some distance, and in another person: so David saw his fault in the person of the rich oppressor; till then he could not in himself, 2 Sam. 12. See on Matt. 7:5.

**8. ***{again he stooped down}* Thereby giving them with their convicted consciences, opportunity to bethink themselves, and to steal away, that they might not at present be further detected and shamed: possibly, they might, in that he wrote in the dust, read some discovery of their sins and shame, which they would not for the world should come to light, and knowledge of the people, of whose applause and good opinion they were extremely ambitious: sure it is, Christ knew all that they had done, could in a few characters write enough to shame them, and that they were so foully guilty that they were glad to be gone, for fear of further discovery as may be gathered, from v. 9.

**9. ***{being convicted by their own conscience}* Hearing such an unexpected answer, and out of hope to accomplish their design upon him, their consciences accusing them, and fearing to confess themselves sinners (lest their reputation with the people should thereby be impaired) or to bring their pretended innocency to the test before him, who as they might well suspect, might know and detect their secret failings; they chose rather to take the opportunity of his stooping down, to go away; though in that recess, indeed confessing his impregnable wisdom, and their own guiltiness.

*{beginning at the eldest}* Either (says Ambrose lib. 7, ep. 58) because they who had lived longest, had more sins; or because they being more prudent, first apprehended the force of the sentence. That which Theodoret says (in Jer. 17:1) is true: there is nothing more powerful than the conscience.

*{Jesus was left alone}* That is, in respect of all those accusers; not simply alone; for these things were acted before his disciples, and a great multitude of people: therefore, it is said, *and the woman standing in the midst*, *ἐν* *μέσῳ*, must relate to a company.

**10. ***{and saw none but the woman}* None belonging to that cause: no accuser, nor officer attending her.

*{where are those thine accusers}* Not being ignorant of their departure, but making manifest to all present, that he absolved her not contrary to the law, seeing the informers and witnesses deserted the cause.

**11. ***{She said, no man, Lord}* She answers to the second query, not weighing the first, seeing her accusers were gone: she knew, that condemnation ought not to go before conviction.

*{neither do I condemn thee}* *Neither will I condemn thee*, Ambrose reads, lib. 7, ep. 58. Others read with us, *κατακρίνω*, *neither do I condemn*. I will not use less clemency than they: see ch. 3:17. He said, not to dissolve the law, but to show mercy, and to call to repentance: and for that he would not take upon him to be a temporal judge, as Luke 12:14, as if he said, I leave the magistrate his right of judicature? And seeing they condemn you not, neither do I, who came to pardon sinners, and save sinners: he says not, no man ought to condemn you (that had been to pervert justice, to condemn the law, and to justify the wicked, for certainly there are many sins in adultery), but he says, *hath no man condemned thee?* He so shows mercy, as not thereby to abolish political order: and here is a sweet motive to repentance, that we may fly to his mercy, from whose justice we cannot fly. Fulgent. *ad Venant.* ep. 7.

*{go and sin no more}* This said he, that he might show them, *se homini pepercisse, &c.* (Augustin. *Macedon.* ep. 54), that he spared man, but was not pleased with man’s sin: this is true repentance, not only to lament, but to forsake our sin: the end why God shows mercy, is that we may serve him more holily. See Rom. 6:4; Luke 1:75, and this was here the greatest and most absolute condemnation of this woman’s sin: for where the guilty is put to death, rather the person is executed than the sin: but where the sinner leaves his sin, there is a discharge of the person whom Christ came to save, and a killing of sins which he came to condemn in the flesh, Rom. 8:3.

**12. ***{I am the light of the world}* Ch. 1:4, 5 and 9:5.

**13. ***{thy record is not true}* *ἀληθὴς* is here taken for authentic or worthy to be credited; that which the Hebrews call *נאמן* *neeman*, the Syriac *שרירא* *shariro* (that is, *firmum, validum*) of *שרר* *sharar*.

**14. ***{though I bear record}* Ch. 5:31. He admits the antecedent, but rejects the consequent: the sense being: I do indeed for their sakes who shall believe in me and be saved, declare and manifest myself unto them, which is to testify of myself; but it thence follows not that my testimony is not true, or ought not (as in cases betwixt man and man, and human interests) be taken for such. God’s word which you hear of me is infallibly true, and so to be taken, if all men in the world should depose to the contrary.

*{yet my record is true}* Because God is the only sufficient witness concerning himself. See 1 John 5:6, 7, 9.

**15. ***{ye judge after the flesh}* According to outward appearance, ch. 7:24.

*{I judge no man}* That is, I do not now judge; I now am come to teach and propose conditions of mercy and salvation to penitent sinners: or I judge not rashly, as you do.

**16. ***{for I am not alone}* He does not plainly and expressly affirm his Godhead (that was yet veiled under the infirmity of the flesh) but mentions his Father, whose deity they acknowledged, to intimate the power he had to judge, who yet spared them, and instructed them how to avoid the judgment to come: he was not alone, the Father and the Holy Ghost were with him, *nunquam enim potest à se Trinitas separari*. Ambros. *in Luc.* lib. 2, cap. 3, for the Holy Trinity can never be divided from itself. See Fulgent. *de fide orthod. ad Donat.* See 1 John 5:7.

**17. ***{it is also written in your Law}* Deut. 17:6. See Matt. 18:16. *ἐν* *τῷ* *νόμῳ* *τῷ* *ὑμετέρῳ*, in your law; understand, as if he said, that very law which God gave your fathers, and which you have received from their hands, in which you glory.

*{the testimony of two men is true}* That is, so taken in law, not that it is always so. See 1 Kings 21:13; Matt. 26:60; Acts 6:11, but this he says by way of concession: and by this secret antithesis he shows, how unjust they were who professed the law, according to which they took that for granted, which two men witnessed, but would not believe what God the Father and himself testified.

**18. ***{I am one that bear witness, &c.}* He declares that which he before said, *I am not alone*, verse 16. As God he testifies of himself (the Messias, God and man), and in the same act, the Father testifies with him, for they are one God, and their works are undivided. See on verse 16. Christ argues (as Lyranus notes) from the lesser to the greater: the witness of two men, according to Moses’ law, is sufficient to confirm a truth, therefore much more sufficient is the witness of two persons of the Holy Trinity; men may deceive and lie, but God cannot: he might here also have cited the same testimony of the Holy Ghost; but that doctrine was not yet opportune, and seasonable for them.

**19. ***{ye should have known my Father also}* For they are one God. See ch. 10:30.

**20. ***{These words spake Jesus}* See on Luke 21:4. In their treasury their offerings were kept, Mark 12:41. This shows his undaunted constancy in the presence of his enemies, and heat of their malice: no man yet meddled with him, because the providence of God limited all things to the appointed times, therefore they were yet restrained to teach us constancy and confidence in the truth. The devil himself cannot reach beyond the length of his chain (Rev. 20:1, &c.), or hurt the least of God’s children, more than, or before he permits, who will make all things work for good to them that love him, Rom. 8:28.

**21. ***{Ye shall seek me}* See ch. 7:34.

**22. ***{Whither I go}* See ch. 7:35.

**23. ***{ye are from beneath}* He shows here the cause of their blindness and unbelief: you are earthly and carnal, therefore apprehend you not the heavenly things which I teach you, no man is an apt disciple of Christ, whom God’s Spirit does not make so: hence is the rarity of faith in the world.

*{I am not of this world}* He denies not his human nativity, of the seed of Abraham; but he denies that he has any communion with sin, and the corruptions of flesh and blood: in which sense he says, the apostles were not of this world, ch. 15:9 and 17:16, though not free from sin by nature (Eph. 2:3), but by grace and regeneration; not absolutely (as Christ) but comparatively, and in respect of their justification by the merit of Christ. See Gal. 1:4; Phil. 3:20.

**24. ***{I said therefore unto you, &c.}* These words relate to the precedent declaration; the meaning is, the world perishes in sin and unbelief (1 Cor. 11:32). If you are of this world, and come not to me by faith for your justification, you must needs perish in your sins.

*{that I am he, &c.}* That I am the Messias and sole Savior of the world: this shows that there is salvation to none, but those who constantly hope for, and expect in Christ, whatsoever the prophets have foretold concerning the Messias.

*{ye shall die in your sins}* For there is salvation in none other, Acts 4:12. He only takes away the sins of the world, ch. 1:29. He only justifies sinners by suffering for them, paying the full price of their redemption, and making their peace with God (see Rom. 3:24 and 5:24 and 5:3 and 8:1, 2); therefore, if your sins be not done away in Christ, you shall in them die eternally.

**25. ***{even the same that I said}* The Son of God, and Savior of the world, as I taught you from the beginning.

**26. ***{I have many things to say, and to judge of you}* As if he had said, I could detect, and object against you many things, but because I see my word is to you, but as the sunbeams to the clay, more hardening you, I forbear now; yet my Father, who sent me to testify the truth to you, will once appear true in all that he has threatened unbelievers, and will vindicate his word against all impious despisers of the same. See 2 Tim. 2:13. It may be a comfort to all that constantly adhere to the truth of God’s word; though the world and generality of men oppose or contemn it, God will be true: as the ascending fumes and vapors may for a time obscure the light of the sun toward us, they cannot extinguish it, so may emergent heresies, and oppositions of men veil the truth a while, they shall never finally prevail against it.

**27. ***{they understood not}* He notes the stupidity of some of the hearers, who could neither understand promises, nor threatenings.

**28. ***{When ye have lift up}* That is, when you have crucified me, your own consciences shall be convicted, that I am the Son of God. See Matt. 27:54; Acts 2:37; Luke 23:47, 48, which he speaks concerning those who should afterward repent and believe, for the rest did not, nor to this day do acknowledge him such, and therefore perish in their sins.

**29. ***{the Father hath not left me alone}* That is, the power of the Spirit of God ever assists me in my ministry; it is your own fault, if you feel it not in your hearts, active to faith and obedience, 2 Cor. 4:3, 4.

**30. ***{many believed}* The bee sucks honey where the spider gathers venom: the word of Christ falls like the snow and rain (Deut. 32:1, &c.; Isaiah 55:10; Heb. 4:12 and 6:7), which makes the venomous weeds grow up more rank to the fire, and the wholesome herbs more fruitful for the owner’s blessing.

**31. ***{If ye continue in my Word}* It is the end which crowns all good actions. See Matt. 24:13; Rev. 2:10; Col. 1:23. It is not enough to begin well, *Non quaeruntur in Christianis initia, sed finis: Paulus male coepit, sed bene finivit: Judae laudantur exordia, sed finis proditione damnatur*. Jerome *Furiae*, ep. 21. In Christians, not so much the beginning, as the end is considerable: Paul began ill, but ended well: Judas’s beginnings are commended, but his end was damnable for his treason.

**32. ***{the truth shall make you free}* For otherwise, they were slaves to sin and error: the good man even in servitude is free, but the wicked, *etsi regnet, servus est*, though he reigns is but a slave, and that not unto one man, but that which is more grievous to so many masters, as he has sins. Prosper, out of Augustine *de C.*, lib. 1, cap. 18, and then is the will free, when it serves not vice and sin: that is the gift of God, which being lost, *nisi à quo potuit dari, non potest reddi*, can be restored by none, but him, who could first give it. Prosper *ex Aug. de C. D.* lib. 14, cap. 11.

**33. ***{they answered him}* They who were heard to calumniate, who believed not, though they had often been made vassals, and now were by the Romans, yet they conceived that by right they ought to have been free; and that (however degenerate they were) yet that they had the privilege of Abraham’s sons: not understanding, that only regeneration by the Spirit of God could make them free, and the true children of Abraham, Rom. 2:29; Luke 19:9; Matt. 3:9; Rom. 9:7, 8; Gal. 3:9, 29.

*{ye shall be made free}* That is, as if we were not so already? Thus are we generally secure under the title of Christians, whereas that sacred name shall more grievously condemn the wicked: and we may too truly say with Salvian *de gub. D.* lib. 6: *O deplorabile miseriam, quam dissimilis est nunc à seipso populus Christianus, id est, ab eo qui fuit quondam*. How unlike is the Christian people to itself, that is, to such as it was wont to be.

**34. ***{whosoever committeth sin}* You boast of your freedom: but sin is the greatest servitude (Rom. 6:16, 20; 2 Pet. 2:19), and the custom thereof, the most unhappy tyranny, and the bonds thereof most hard to shake off. Sin reigning, a man has a free will (says Fulgent. *de incarn. & gra.* cap. 19), but free without God, not free under God, and therefore *pessimè atque serviliter liberum*, wickedly and servilely free, because not freed by the free gift of God showing mercy.

**35. ***{the servant abideth not in the house, &c.}* This is the punishment and misery of the servants of sin: though they seem for a time to enjoy the privilege of God’s house, and to insult over, and persecute his children, yet are they not heirs, but must at last be cast out, as Ishmael was from Abraham’s family, Gen. 21:9; Gal. 4:30.

**36. ***{if the Son therefore shall make you free}* This he says, to show that whereas all men are born servants of sin, they are freed by grace only, and the Spirit of adoption, by faith in Christ: and that all other prerogatives are vain: what could the empire of the world advantage a servant to sin? The most happy freedom, is to be a servant of God, that sin may not reign in you. But from this servitude of sin, no man is freed but by the Son of God, Fulgent. *de incarn. &c.*, cap. 19, for by him *spiritualiter renascimur*. See Augustine ep. 157. See also the Epistle of the Council of Carthage, Innocent. ib. ep. 90.

**37. ***{ye are Abraham’s seed}* That is according to the flesh.

**38. ***{seen with your father}* What your father the devil teaches you. See verse 44.

**39. ***{works of Abraham}* You would be heir to him in faith, and obedience, the works of Abraham were faith and obedience, and the *fruit of the Spirit* described, Gal. 5:22, 23. See Rom. 4:9, &c.; Gal. 3:6. You that glory of your being sons of Abraham, believe as he did, for the carnal privilege will nothing advantage you. See Rom. 2:28, 29.

**40. ***{this did not Abraham}* Your works are contrary to Abraham’s; he loved and believed God, you through unbelief persecute the Son of God: he obeyed God, you serve the spirit of malice.

**41. ***{the deeds of your Father}* Your works speak you the sons of another father, whom you imitate.

*{we have one Father, even God}* We worship one God, who chose us to be his people, who often acknowledged us for his children, Exod. 4:22.

**42. ***{if God were your Father}* If you were indeed the sons of God by adoption, you would imitate him in love.

**43. ***{cannot hear my word}* That is, you will not hear and obey, being filled with malice and prejudicate opinion. See Matt. 13:14, 15.

**44. ***{ye are of your father the devil}* Not by nature, but by imitation of his malice. See 1 John 3:8. So Cyril, *Catech.* 7. Satan is the liar’s father, *οὐ* *τῇ* *φύσει*, *ἀλλὰ* *κατὰ* *ἀπάτην*, not by nature, but by fraud and deceit.

*{from the beginning}* From the beginning of the world he led men into sin and death, by his lying, Gen. 3:4, 5.

*{abode not in the truth}* He kept not his first estate, Jude verse 6.

*{he speaketh of his own}* According to his custom and disposition; if ever he speak truth, he borrows that, to the end that he may deceive the more.

*{he is a liar, and the father of it}* Liars are the devil’s children by imitation: herein they nearly resemble him: none would believe him whom they knew to be the devil; neither will they believe a notorious known liar: it is madness not to know, *quod mendacia non diu fallunt*, Cyprian lib. 1, ep. 3, that lies cannot long deceive; because ere long, none will believe him, no not when he tells the truth.

**45. ***{because I tell you the truth}* Thus he proves them Satan’s sons, not God’s, because they hated the truth, nor is there any more evident mark of a reprobate mind, than hatred of truth, and love of lies. See 2 Thess. 2:10, 11.

**46. ***{Which of you convinceth me of sin?}* This is a secret prepossession of an objection: if they should say, we disbelieve not because you speak truth, but because you violate the law; he challenges the accusers to show wherein: they could accuse, and did maliciously, but who can convince, or prove him to be a transgressor of the law? This word could fit the mouth of holy Jesus only, it is too high a challenge for any of the mere sons of men: in Christ only there was no sin. See Heb. 7:26. Such the law of sacrifices prefigured him. See 1 Pet. 1:19.

**47. ***{He that is of God}* This is a peculiar of those who are of God; who are led by the Spirit of God, and sincerely endeavor to do his will, they will hearken to his word; they will love to hear. See 1 John 4:6. You believe not, because you are not God’s sons, but Satan’s, who hates and resists the truth. See Acts 13:48.

**48. ***{Say we not well that thou art a Samaritan, &c.}* Thus grows unbelief and contempt of God’s word into open blasphemy: thus convicted impiety breaks out into railing: they accounted the Samaritans apostates, and impious; therefore when they would describe a wicked man, they called him Samaritan.

**50. ***{and judgeth}* And will avenge this blasphemy, wherewith you dishonor me.

**51. ***{He shall never see death}* Shall not die eternally: it is a Hebrew manner of expression, as also, *to taste of death*, verse 52; Heb. 11:5. See Matt. 16:28; Mark 9:1; Luke 9:27.

**52. ***{we know thou hast a devil}* Thus obstinate were they in their malicious blasphemy; and so high an opinion had they of themselves, that they concluded all must be true which they thought, who thought themselves infallible.

**53. ***{Art thou greater than our father Abraham?}* So injurious are they to God and to the saints, who use their name and memory to Christ’s dishonor: we can never make more honorable mention of them, than when we style them the servants of Christ.

**54. ***{if I honour myself}* If I only speak glorious and great things of myself, as you suppose; but my Father testifies of me, and honors me, by the prophets, by his voice from heaven, by many miracles by me done.

*{Ye say that he is your God}* They professed so (as also many now), but they denied God in their actions; as the apostle says, Titus 1:16, *they profess that they know God, but in works they deny him, &c.* No compliments can take the omniscient God, no disguises deceive him. See Jer. 3:4, 5; Matt. 7:21, &c.

**55. ***{ye have not known him}* Therefore is he not your God: you know him not, who dare so willfully disobey him: he that says, *I know him, and keepeth not his commandments, is a liar, and the truth is not in him*, 1 John 2:4.

**56. ***{Abraham rejoiced to see my day}* That is, my coming in the flesh, which he saw far off by faith, Heb. 11:13. (Prosper refers it to the day of his passion, figured in his son, *de promiss. & praedict.*, part 1, cap. 17.)

**57. ***{Thou art not yet fifty years old}* He was about thirty and three years old. See Luke 3:23.

**58. ***{Before Abraham was, I am}* I who according to my humanity am not fifty years old; according to my divine nature am eternal, and so before Abraham, and all the creatures: the deity has neither past, nor future, *sed semper esse habet*, Greg. hom. 18 in Evang. He says not to Moses, I was, but *I am hath sent thee*, Exod. 3:14.

**59. ***{Then took they up stones, &c.}* Such is the fury of blind zeal, and impatient error, they have no sober ears for truth, but hands quickly armed for slaughter. See Acts 7:57.

*{but Jesus hid himself}* How, we read not, but certainly God’s power so protected him; because the time of his passion was not yet come. See Luke 4:30. He could easily have destroyed all his enemies, but *qui pati venerat, exercere judicium nolebat*. Greg. in Evang. hom. 18. He that came to suffer would not now exercise judgment: it may teach us, to decline the fury of our enemies by withdrawing ourselves from their presence, if it may be, to reserve ourselves to better times and auditors. Certainly Christ stays not with the bloody and cruel; though in his absence they have the possession of the external, material churches.

## CHAPTER 9

**1. ***{Blind from his birth}* Born blind: this sets out the greatness of the miracle: they who are blind by some disease growing in the eye (any part of that curious fabric being hurt or disaffected) may possibly find some help by the art and industry of the physician: but those who are born blind, without eyes, or without the natural frame and disposition thereof, can be cured by none but the Lord of nature: therefore the evangelist mentions this, that we may know that this cure was *non artis, sed potestatis*, Ambros. *ad Bellic.* ep. 75, l. 9, not of art but power.

**2. ***{or his parents}* This they asked, being confident of his omniscience, otherwise they had in vain moved this question; and also knowing that all misery is the effect of sin (ch. 5:14), and that God often visits the parents’ sin upon their dear children, see 2 Sam. 12:14, &c.

**3. ***{Neither hath this man sinned, nor, &c.}* Which he says not *ἁπλῶς*, simply, for so, both he and his parents had sinned (as Chrysostom notes) but neither this man’s sins, nor his parents’ were a cause why he, rather than all other sinners, was born blind, but that God’s glory might appear in his cure.

**4. ***{While it is day}* While I live here, or while I have opportunity. This is a similitude taken from the common custom and condition of men unto whom God has appointed the daytime for labor and doing *כל* *מלאכתך*, Exod. 20:9, *omne opus tuum*, 70, *πάντα* *τὰ* *ἔργα* *σου*, *all thy works*. See Ps. 104:23. The present sense is, which Theophylact gives, *ἕως* *ὁ* *παρὼν* *βίος* *ἀντέχει*, while my present life lasts; which the apostle, Heb. 5:7, calls *ἡμέραις* *τῆς* *σαρκὸς* *αὐτοῦ*, *the days of his flesh*; that is, while yet he was in this world bearing the assumed frailties of human flesh and blood (for we may not think that being glorified, he has put off our flesh, and human nature), while he here lived to preach, do miracles, to call men to, and confirm them in the faith, and perform all those things, which according to the determinate counsel and will of God, were to be done for the accomplishment of man’s salvation; all which because it was limited to certain times and moments thereof, he compares the season and opportunity of working and doing the same, to a day consisting of certain definite hours.

*{when no man can work}* He calls the hour of the wicked, and power of darkness (to which he was in his passion, shortly to be subjected) the night, wherein he should not have opportunity of healing, and doing miracles for the confirmation of the Gospel, by his own visible and bodily presence: he did that after his death and resurrection by his disciples and their successors in the primitive church for a time: the words therefore express not an impossibility, but an importunity: *when no man can work*; that is, when it is unseasonable to work: it must teach us, while we have time, to improve it to God’s service; the time is coming on, wherein there shall be no faith, labor, prophesying, nor place for repentance. See Isa. 55:6; John 12:35, 36; 1 Cor. 13:8.

**5. ***{I am the light}* Ch. 1:9. He is always in the world, and still illuminates every one that comes into the world: but not now by a visible presence, and external operation, but by his divine and secret power. See ch. 8:12.

**6. ***{Spat on the ground}* Christ uses means, to man’s reason improbable, that his power might be the better seen in the cure.

*{anointed the eyes of the blind}* Or *spread the clay upon the eyes, &c.* That is, the place where the eyes should naturally have been, or upon those his lightless, and disaffected organs of sight: so God according to his omnipotent will works either by, without, or contrary, to ordinary means, and second causes; plastering whole and sound eyes with clay, might, to man’s reason, have seemed a means rather to blind, than to give a better sight.

**7. ***{go wash in the pool of Siloam}* Siloam was a fountain at the foot of Mount Sion. See on Luke 13:4.

*{which is by interpretation, sent}* Syr. *שלוחא* *Shiloucha*, or *Shilucha*: it is of *שלח* *shalach*, *misit, emisit, &c.* Thence *שלוח* *siloah*, Isa. 8:6, the waters, *השלוח*. 70, *τοῦ* *Σιλωὰμ*: see hereof John Drus. *praeterit.* l. 3, at Luke 13:4, ib. *annot. in N. T.*, and ib. at John 9:7, ib. *praet.* l. 4, John 9:7, and *ad Voc. N. T. com.* 1, *siloe*. (Is. Casaub. *excer.* 15, n. 37. Adricom. *Theatr. s. Jerusalem*, n. 200. Jerome in Isa. 8:6. Joseph. *de bel. Jud.* l. 7, c. 24. Plini. l. 31, c. 2, says, hereof *in Judaea rivus Sabbatis omnibus* *siccatur*)—these words of interpretation, are added to signify that Christ was (as the spiritual rock, 1 Cor. 10, so also) the Siloah, *sent of God* to illuminate and save men, to be the fountain of all good to believers: it was not the virtue of this water (wherein doubtless many had washed without any extraordinary effect) but his power who was sent of God that gave this man sight: and this bore a type of baptism, as Ambrose (forecited, verse 1) notes which was called of the Greeks *φώτισμα*. Clem. Alexand. *Paed.* l. 1. Nazianz. orat. 40. Cyril. cat. 1, 2. Chrysost. on Heb. 6:4, where the apostle calls the baptized *φωτισθέντας*, *enlightened*, as Ambros. says, *refuso lumine*, as if Christ the light touching the blind, infused illumination.

**8. ***{he that sate and begged}* Their law concerning beggars, and provision for the poor, Deut. 15:4 (as in other disordered governments), it seems was now not carefully executed. See Acts 3:2.

**9. ***{I am he}* As impiety is ingenious to conceal and obscure the works of God, so is faith and holy gratitude ingenuous and free to confess the same to God’s glory: their doubting occasioned this recovered man to proclaim the work of Christ on him; wherein as Chrysostom notes, *οὐκ* *ᾐσχύνθη* *τὴν* *προτέραν* *πήρωσιν*—he was not ashamed of his former blindness (no nor of his beggary), nor feared he the people’s fury, neither dissembled he the benefit received.

**10. ***{thine eyes opened}* *ἀνεῴχθησαν*, after the Hebrew manner of speaking, the eyes of the blind are said to be shut: and so their eyes are said to be opened, who of blind, are restored to light: sometimes we read of eyes, held, or opened in another sense, as Gen. 21:19; Luke 24:16. So Hagar’s were opened, that she might see that, which before, though present, and seeing, she could not see: and the disciples’ eyes were held; not that they might not see, but that they might not at present know Christ.

**11. ***{I went and washed}* He relates the truth simply and as it was acted; showing the effect of his faith and obedience, the receiving his sight.
