**12. ***{I know not}* Whether they asked, as admiring the cure, or to accuse him who had wrought it on the Sabbath, it is not evident though this seems most probable: it appears that though the cured man had learned his name who cured him, of others (as Mark 10:47; Matt. 20:30), possibly of those who led him away to Siloam, yet had he not yet seen Jesus, who likely went away from the place where he cured him, so that he found him not after he returned from the water, seeing.

**13. ***{they brought him to the Pharisees}* Whether to ingratiate with them or to inform them what admirable things Jesus (whom they persecuted) did, it is not manifest.

**14. ***{It was the Sabbath day}* See on Matt. 12:2, 5, 8. See ch. 5:9, &c.

**15. ***{again the Pharisees}* These busy enemies of Christ, intended by this reexamination of the man, to have found some advantage against Christ; but they did but condemn themselves the more, by how much more apparent their malice made this admirable work of Christ.

**16. ***{because he keepeth not the Sabbath}* See on ch. 5:9, 10.

**17. ***{A Prophet}* A man of God, of whom he must have such power. Surely he must needs be acknowledged a holy prophet sent of God, which he has abundantly proved by this his power showed on me, in doing this divine miracle of restoring sight to me who was born blind; this none but God ever did, or could do.

**18. ***{Until they called his parents}* Hoping that they being overawed with fear of their power and authority, would rather deny that their son was born blind, than by confessing a known truth, incur their displeasure: but the more they strive to obscure the truth, the more evident they made it: so God turns the counsels of the wicked against themselves, and takes the wise in their own craft. See 1 Cor. 3:19.

**19. ***{who ye say was, &c.}* They will not acknowledge that he was so born, but interrogate the parents in a threatening manner, which in effect was to bid them deny it, as they tendered their favor.

**20. ***{we know this is our son}* We cannot but know and confess this; they would not for fear deny their son: how wretched is it for fear or favor to deny our Savior.

**21. ***{he is of age ask him}* Of age to be a competent witness; and he can best inform you hereof, who has many years endured the misery of blindness, and now enjoys his sight: it may be the cure was not wrought in their sight, and therefore they more willingly put off the fuller answer to their son.

**22. ***{because they feared the Jews}* That is, the Pharisees and these who were of their consistory, or Sanhedrin, because they had already determined to excommunicate or put any man out of the synagogue that confessed him to be Christ; and it appears that this was published among the common people with severe threatenings, therefore these parents were afraid.

*{put out of the synagogue}* That is, cast out of the church as a notorious and contumacious malefactor, profane, and wicked heretic: which casting out, we call excommunication. See Matt. 18:17; 1 Cor. 5:2, 5; 1 Tim. 1:20. This shows, as the abuse of excommunication among them, so the use thereof in all ages.

**24. ***{give God praise}* Tell truth, that God may be glorified. See Josh. 7:19, as if they had said, remember, that you speak in God’s presence: thus (as hypocrites use) they pretended the honor of God, to color their own implacable malice against Christ.

*{we know}* They would have them in blind obedience to their authority, rest on their judgment (though it were against Christ himself) and believe that they could not err.

*{this man is a sinner}* By this they blasphemously meant, a notoriously impious man.

**25. ***{I know that whereas I was blind, I now see}* He said before he is a prophet, but perceiving the Pharisees so eager against Christ, refusing to speak of the person, he affirms the admirable work done.

**26. ***{They said to him again}* In these many reexaminations, they would have found him forgetful of what he had said, that by crossing his own testimony, he might have made it void: so extreme diligent is Satan, to overthrow, or obscure the truth.

**27. ***{ye did not hear}* You believed it not, Gr. *οὐκ* *ἠκούσατε*, *ye heard not*, you regarded not, you gave no credit to my testimony: why do you desire often to hear that which you will not believe, be it never so evident.

*{will ye also be his disciples?}* As if he had said, do you by these reexaminations desire to know more of him that you may believe in him and follow him as his disciples, as I, and all that are enlightened by God, find just cause to do? If so, I shall willingly answer again and again; if not, let it suffice that I have already clearly related the whole truth of this matter unto you.

**28. ***{we are Moses’ disciples}* Thus they boasted, but were indeed wicked hypocrites: on false principles they set their false superstructions: they took it for granted, that he could not be a disciple of Moses; that is, obey the law of God given by the ministry of Moses, who believed in, or was a disciple of Christ: whereas the truth is, that they which were the true disciples of Moses, did come to, and believe in Christ, the end of the law, which was a schoolmaster to bring them to him, Gal. 3:24. Therefore he told them, ch. 5:46: *had ye believed Moses, ye would have believed me: for he wrote of me*.

**29. ***{whence he is}* They knew his parents, but doubted of his authority, whether God sent him to us or not: we know not what commission, power, or authority he has to preach and do these things. We are certain that God spoke to, and wrought by Moses, whom we follow; this man we know not whence he is: much differently said Nicodemus, ch. 3:2, *Rabbi*, we know that *thou art a teacher come from God; for no man can do these miracles that thou dost, except God be with him*.

**30. ***{herein is a marvelous thing}* He might well admire their ignorance, seeing opening the eyes of the blind, was a foretold sign and mark of the Messias, Isa. 35:4, 5. So Matt. 11:5; Luke 7:20, &c., which they knowing that he now had done, and never man before had or could do, yet they could not (by reason of their malice which blinded them) understand and believe that he was the promised Messias, so many ages before described and marked out by God, by his opening the eyes of the blind, &c. See Matt. 11:5.

**31. ***{God heareth not sinners}* Ps. 66:18. Which may not be understood simply and universally: for God sometimes hears in judgment, as he sometimes denies in mercy, by this argument he would overthrow their blasphemous censures of Christ: God will not hear the prayers of such wicked men as you mentioned, see Isa. 1:11; Rom. 8:15; Ps. 50:16; James 5:16, therefore he cannot be wicked who obtains power of God to do such things. See ch. 3:2.

*{but if any man, &c.}* 1 John 3:22.

**32. ***{since the world began}* As if he said, that which comes to pass by nature, is commonly, or usually so, that which by art, often; but that which is done against the course of nature, above the power of art, and so rarely, must needs be by the immediate hand of God: and so consequently Christ is of God.

**33. ***{he could do nothing}* None of these extraordinary good things.

**34. ***{altogether born in sins}* They meant, a wicked rude idiot, as if he had by his affliction been marked for a notorious sinner; or a cursed man, as ch. 7:39.

*{Cast him out}* Or *excommunicated him*. See v. 22.

**35. ***{when he had found him}* They that are excommunicate for confessing Christ aright are thereby brought nearer to Christ: but they who are justly excommunicate, are thereby delivered to Satan, 1 Cor. 5:5; 1 Tim. 1:20, which is a terrible punishment.

*{dost thou believe?}* This he says, *οὐκ* *ἀγνοῶν*, says Chrysostom, hom. 58, not being ignorant, but being willing to manifest himself unto him, that he might believe and be saved.

**36. ***{who is he Lord, &c.}* Not yet knowing Jesus by sight, yet affectionately desiring to know him who had done him so great a favor: not ashamed of his ignorance, he prays to be informed that he might believe.

**37. ***{It is he that talketh with thee}* He says not, I am he; but it is he, as in a third person, intimating that the Deity of the Son of God was veiled in that form of infirm man which he saw: happy man who had his bodily eyes opened to see the Son of God in the flesh, but incomparably more happy in that his soul’s eyes were enlightened to apprehend and know him by faith to salvation; Christ who gave the first, now follows him with this new and greater beneficence. So do God’s mercies increase upon the elect.

**38. ***{worshiped him}* *Fell down and worshiped him*: so the Syriac interpreter has it.

**39. ***{for judgment I am come}* Meaning, the Pharisees arrogate all jurisdiction to themselves, as if they only saw all things; but I shall judge, or execute authority far otherwise: for I will enlighten those whom they account blind, and deliver them to darkness of mind, who think themselves the only knowing men.

*{they which see not}* That is, wretched sinners, who acknowledge and bewail their innate ignorance, shall be enlightened by the Gospel.

*{and that they which see}* They who think they are wise (see Isa. 5:21) as did the Pharisees, who arrogated to themselves the name of wise; anciently the prophets were called *רואים* *roim*, seers, 1 Sam. 9:9, let us go *הרואה*, 70. *πρὸς* *τὸν* *βλέποντα*: to the seer. So was a prophet called, *ὁ* *βλέπων*: so here *οἱ* *βλέποντες*; so Syr. *דחזין* which see of the Hebr. *חזה* *vidit*, whence also *חוזה* a seer, or a prophet, Isa. 30:10. The scribes and Pharisees vainly affecting such high titles (as Matt. 23:7, 24) are threatened, as Isa. 6:9. See Matt. 13:15.

**40. ***{are we blind also?}* What! will you say that we also are ignorant?

**41. ***{ye should have no sin}* That is, you should not be so much in fault, as now you are if you erred ignorantly; but it is a greater sin to condemn the known law of God, than to be ignorant thereof (see ch. 15:22; Matt. 15:14) or, you were nearer the cure but for your opinion of knowledge and sanctity; many had long since been wise, and good, but that they too soon thought themselves so. See Rev. 3:17; 1 John 1:8; 1 Cor. 3:18; James 4:17.

## CHAPTER 10

**1. ***{Entereth not by the door}* Christ compares the church to a sheepfold (as Mic. 2:12) into which God gathers his sheep; that is, the elect: and himself to the door: because there may be no entrance into the church, but through him: they who lead not this way, are no true pastors.

**2. ***{he that entereth by the door}* That is, by Christ: the priests, scribes, and Pharisees, as false prophets, came in sheep’s clothing, who within were ravening wolves, Matt. 7:15.

**3. ***{to him the porter openeth}* The meaning is; God allows none for pastors, but those who lead their hearers to him through Christ.

*{the sheep hear his voice}* The elect know and follow the voice of Christ in the word.

*{he calleth his own sheep by name}* He knows who are his, 2 Tim. 2:19, and their names are written in the Book of Life, Phil. 4:3.

**4. ***{when he putteth forth his own sheep}* See Ps. 23:1, &c.; Jer. 17:16; 2 Sam. 7:8. He leads them out to green pastures, that is to refection and comfort; he goes before them in doctrine and example: his sheep know his voice, by that discerning spirit, by which we are commanded to try the spirits, 1 John 4:1. So did the Bereans, Acts 17.

**5. ***{know not the voice of strangers}* They approve not, or hearken not to the voice of those who bring any other doctrine, Gal. 1:8; 2 John v. 10.

**6. ***{This parable}* See Matt. 13:3. Gr. *ταύτην* *τὴν* *παροιμίαν*, this proverb, or adage, 2 Pet. 2:22: a short, and common sentence, or saying: that which the other evangelists call a parable, Matt. 13:3, &c.

**7. ***{Then said Jesus again}* Or *therefore*, because they understood not what he said, he repeats it again, further expressing it to them.

*{I am the door of the sheep}* The meaning is, that in Christ is the sum of all doctrine wherewith the souls of the elect are nourished: therefore the apostle cared for nothing else, Phil. 3; 1 Cor. 2:2: and that no man can come into the kingdom of God, but through him: so as Gr. Nazianzen said, *orat.* 36, *ὁδὸς* *δὲ* *ὡς* *δι*’ *ἑαυτοῦ* *φέρων* *ἡμᾶς*: He is the way leading us (to eternal life) through himself: his saints and elect *per eum, intrant ad eum*, through his truth and merit to life eternal in him.

**8. ***{All that ever came before me}* Meaning the false prophets, who came not in by him, but by Satan and their own ambition: whosoever called himself the door or way to life; or all that went into the ministry and he sent them not: for he says not, all that were sent: for as Augustine says, *To.* 6, *contr. adv. leg. &c.*, l. 2, c. 4. He means here, all those *qui missi non fuissent*: who were not sent; whom the Prophet Jeremy reproves, ch. 11:14. So Beda says, *Cum illo venerunt, qui cum verbo dei venerunt, qui non per se venerunt*; they came with him who came with the word of God, who came not by themselves. So Clem. Alexandrinus, l. 1, *strom.*, *ἀλλ*’ *οἱ* *μὲν* *προφῆται* *ἅτε*, &c. The prophets truly, for as much as they were sent and inspired of God—*οὐ* *κλέπται* *εἰσὶ* *καὶ* *λῃσταὶ* *ἀλλὰ* *διάκονοι*—were not thieves and robbers, but ministers: by thieves and robbers are here meant, those who seek their own ease and profit, not caring for the flock of God.

**9. ***{I am the door}* As men come by the door into the house, so come we to heaven through Christ only: and therefore he said *I am the door*: that is, as a door by which only you may enter into life eternal. So c. 15:1, 5, *I am the true vine*: in figurative enunciations it is necessary to know that the analogical and representing sign, is put for the thing represented, and contrariwise sometimes: *I am the door, I am the true vine*; that is, these things have a kind of proportion or resemblance of me the only Mediator, and Savior: *this is my body, this is my blood*; that is, signs, and resemblances appointed of God to *show the Lord’s death till he come*, 1 Cor. 11:26.

*{and shall go in and out}* Not meaning going out of the church, by separation from the body of Christ; (for as Lactantius says well, l. 4, c. *ult.*) if any go out of Christ’s church, *à spe vitae ac salutis aeternae alienus est*; He is an alien from hope of eternal life and salvation but alluding to the use of a door: it is an Hebraism importing security of life and conversation, and actions: as Ps. 121:8.

**10. ***{have it more abundantly}* Which is in respect of the increases of God’s grace through Christ here, and the fullness of happiness in the life to come.

**11. ***{I am the good shepherd}* Isa. 40:11; Ezek. 34:23. Thus says, Cyril. Hieros., *cat.* 10. He is sometimes called a sheep or lamb; *διὰ* *τὴν* *ἀνθρωπότητα*: in respect of his incarnation; and also a shepherd: *διὰ* *τὴν* *φιλανθρωπίαν* *τῆς* *θεότητος*: for the benignity of his Deity.

**12. ***{Seeth the wolf coming}* By wolf he means false prophets and seducers, as Matt. 7:15, so Acts 20:29, or persecutors: some add hereto, Satan; *qui caulas fidelium, insidians circuit*, Gregor. *hom.* 14, *in Evang.*, who laying wait, goes round about the folds of the faithful.

**13. ***{the hireling fleeth}* That is, he that administers for his own profit’s sake, leaves the people to seducers, and destruction. See Rom. 16:18; Phil. 3:19; Ezek. 13:4, 5 & 34:2. Here are described three properties of false prophets, or hirelings. 1. They have no proper charge; they are but intruders and usurpers, whatever right they pretend to, or seem to have. 2. When the wolf, any danger or trouble comes; they fly and desert the cause; if not leaving the place, yet in connivance and silence; as Augustine says hereon, *fugisti, quia tacuisti*: you fled for as much as you held your peace: they betray souls for fear of men; *non mutando locum sed subtrahendo solatium, fugit: quia injustitiam vidit, &c. tacuit: fugit, quod se sub silentio abscondit*, Gregor. *hom.* 14. He flees, not by changing place, but by withdrawing (or withholding) comfort; because he saw wickedness and held his peace: he flees, in that he hid himself in silence. 3. They leave their people to the seducements of false prophets and impostors.

**14. ***{The good shepherd}* As v. 11. He knows his, 2 Tim. 2:19; John 13:18, but here is an Hebraism, *γινώσκει*, he knows, that is, he loves, approves, or holds dear, so Gregor. *q. s.* interprets *cognosco, hoc est, diligo*, I know, that is, I love. So the Hebr. *ידע* *jadah*, is used, Ps. 1:6 and 37:18. See also Matt. 7:23.

**15. ***{lay down my life for the sheep}* Hirelings betray the sheep to the wolf, but Christ *εἰς* *λύτρον* for a ransom: and so the faithful pastors, *εἰς* *μαρτύριον*, for a witness to the truth, lay down their lives for the sheep. See 1 Pet. 1:18, 19; Isa. 53:6, 8, 12; Acts 20:28; John 6:51; 1 John 3:16.

**16. ***{other sheep have I}* He means among the Gentiles, which then were strangers to the church of God. See Ezek. 37:22. So Hos. 2:23. *I will call them my people which were not my people, and her beloved, which was not beloved*, Rom. 9:25, 26. *Sic autem Moses Aethiopissam accipiebat uxorem*, Irenae., l. 4, c. 37. So Moses took the Ethiopian to wife, Num. 12:1, in a representation of that which was to come, making her an Israelite, who was an alien: so was the wild olive implanted, Rom. 11:17, &c.

*{and there shall be one fold}* One church of Jews and Gentiles; the partition-wall being broken down, Eph. 2:14 & 3:6 & 4:4. If but one church, *quomodo potest gregi annumerari, qui in numero gregis non est?* How can he be reckoned to the flock, which is not in the number of the flock? Cyprian, l. 1, ep. 5. See more there concerning the unity of the church.

**17. ***{because I lay down my life}* Isa. 53:12. Because I willingly perform his will, for man’s redemption becoming obedient unto the death, even the death of the cross. Phil. 2:8.

**18. ***{power to take it up again}* To raise my human nature from the dead, as being very God, the resurrection and the life, ch. 11:25.

**19. ***{there was a division}* As ch. 9:16: the Gospel reproves sin, and detects hypocrisy, therefore the impious world is troubled at the preaching thereof; and so men are divided into several factions, according to their different opinions.

**20. ***{he hath a devil, &c.}* So said they, ch. 7:20 and 8:48, *μαίνεται*: Syr. *insaniendo insanit*, or *omnino insanit*, an usual Hebraical *αὔξησις*: that which we say, *he is stark mad*; and lunatic: thus impudently blaspheme they the wisdom and mercy of God. See 1 Kings 9:11; Acts 2:13, 17 & 26:24.

**21. ***{not the words of him that hath a devil}* They that had received some tastes of God’s Spirit, relished a divine grace in his lips, in the words of sobriety and truth, and therefore loved and admired him, as ch. 7:46; Matt. 7:28, 29, assuming secretly that which they were sensible of, by denying an improbable collection of the malicious adversaries: as if they said, wicked, possessed, or mad men, do not use to speak thus.

*{can a devil open the eyes, &c.}* *δαιμόνιον*, a devil, or *δαιμόνιον* *ἔχων*, *he that hath a devil*: by this reason they refell the others’ blasphemous assertion, they intimate that the devil has no such power, as to be able to create eyes to him that was born blind; they might also say, neither any such goodness, as to be willing to do any good for man, except in order to some greater mischief: if then this work done exceeded all power of the creature, it remains that it was possible only to God the Creator: and then if God wrought in him, without controversy the same spoke in him; if his work could not be ascribed to the devil, neither ought his words: if the work was of a good God, how blasphemously do any say that the words proceeded from a malicious devil, who would gladly destroy and afflict all mankind?

**22. ***{the feast of the dedication}* Or *instauration*; which feast was not that we read of, 2 Chron. 7, &c. This was instituted by Judas Maccabeus, to be held eight days, in remembrance of their deliverance, and restitution of their temple and religion, which Antiochus had corrupted, 1 Macc. 4:59. See Beza, *not. major. ad loc.* and Tremellius on the same.

**23. ***{in Solomon’s porch}* That is, that part of the temple which was built again after the pattern of that porch which Solomon built.

**24. ***{make us in doubt}* Or *hold us in suspense*: indeed their malice and unbelief made them fluctuant and doubtful, his words and works evidently enough declared him to be the Messias, to believers; unto the malicious unbelievers, it no more appeared, than light to the blind: the fault was in themselves, who neither did, could or would see: for *their eyes have they closed, lest at any time they should see with their eyes—and should be converted*, &c. Matt. 13:15.

*{tell us plainly}* Not that they were willing to know and believe, but that they might have occasion to calumniate and destroy him, by accusing him of conspiracy against Caesar; forasmuch as they held this principle, that their expected Messias, Christ, must be an absolute monarch and glorious king. See c. 19:12. Therefore did he prohibit his disciples from divulging that which he had declared to them in private concerning this (see Matt. 16:20) and forbade others to speak of those great things which he had done for them, which otherwise would have testified as much; for his hour was not yet come. Note here how hypocrites shut their eyes, and require more light: they seem to cast an aspersion on Christ, as if he were in fault, as the cause of their unbelief because he had not dealt sincerely enough with them, in a clear revelation of himself to them, who desired nothing more than to know and acknowledge the Messias; whereas his works which they often saw, so evidently speaking him the Christ, they had yet decreed to excommunicate any that should confess him to be so, ch. 9:22. Thus some hypocrites accuse the Gospel of obscurity; and others the ministers teaching, of ambiguity, or some other defect; when, God knows, the fault is apparently in themselves.

**25. ***{the works, &c.}* As if he had said, what need there words, where works evidently testifying, are disbelieved? As suppose there had been, as you calumniate me, any obscurity in my words; yet the miracles which you have seen with your eyes, done by divine power, and exceeding all ability of men or angels, evidently convince your contumacy against God, who will not believe his messengers whom he has furnished with such divine virtue: these sufficiently witness that I am the Christ, and expected Messias, these things which I have done in your sight.

**26. ***{because ye are not of my sheep}* For faith is the gift of God, which he gives only to his. See Eph. 2:8. Spiritual deafness toward the word of God, is a certain effect of unbelief. See ch. 8:47 and 1 John 4:6.

**27. ***{my sheep hear my voice}* Those that are ordained to life eternal, hear, believe, and obey the Gospel which is the voice of Christ their shepherd.

*{I know them}* I own and acknowledge them for mine. See 2 Tim. 2:19; Ps. 31:7 and 1:6, &c. As they acknowledge, own, and follow, the voice of their Pastor, loving, reverencing, believing, and obeying him: so I acknowledge them for mine, loving, preserving, and giving them the grace of perseverance in the faith, that I may save them.

*{they follow me}* They obey my precepts, and imitate my example.

**28. ***{neither shall any}* Neither men, nor devils, nor any creature (see Matt. 16:18; Rom. 8:38, 39) neither by fraud nor violence, *οὐχ* *ἁρπάσει* *τις*, none shall be able to pluck them out of my hand: for as Augustine says, *To.* 7, *de corrupt. & gra.*, c. 7, *horum si quisquam perit, vitio humano vincitur Deus: sed nemo eorum perit, quia nulla re vincitur Deus*: If any one of these perish, then God is overcome by man’s sin; but God is not overcome by anything. See also the author of that book, *de fide ad Petrum Diac.*, cap. 35.

**29. ***{greater than all}* More powerful than all the creatures; for in his almighty power they move, and have their being, Acts 17:28.

**30. ***{I and my Father are one}* Two persons, but one God. He speaks this as he is God; one in substance, being, and deity, nature, glory, and power, &c. As God he says, I and my Father are one; but *secundum formam servi*, in respect of the form of a servant (his assumed humanity) he says, ch. 14, *my Father is greater than I*. Augustine, ep. *Pascent.*, ep. 174, *ib.* ep. 177. See also Fulgen. *de fide orthodox. ad. Donat. post med.*, Cyril. *cat.* 11, Hilar. *de Trin.*, lib. 7. *Etsi personis duo, potestate unum sunt*, Ambros. *in Luc.*, lib. 2, cap. 2, though they are two persons, they are one in power.

**31. ***{took up stones again}* As ch. 8:59, this shows with what mind they spoke v. 24. They would have him tell them plainly whether he were Christ, with purpose to stone him as a false prophet, Deut. 13:5; Lev. 20:16. Unbelief is the mother of fury: the spirit of Satan never discovers itself more evidently in men, than by stirring them up to persecute Christ, and the preachers of the Gospel.

**32. ***{for which of them, &c.}* Seeing you can object no evil by me done, and yet stone me, it appears that you persecute me for the good I have done you: the implacable enemy has in all ages stirred up persecution against the prophets for the good they did: no wonder that Christ bids us rejoice therein. See Luke 6:23; James 1:2, nothing more evidently marks them for dear to God, than the devil’s malice to them: as (Tertullian, *adv. Gent.*, cap. 5) said of their persecution under Nero, *We may, we also glory in such an avoucher of our condemnation: Qui enim scit illum intelligere potest, non nisi grande aliquod bonum a Nerone damnatum*; for he that knows him may understand, that nothing but some great good is condemned by Nero.

**33. ***{for a good work we stone thee not}* The wicked would fain seem, but not be just: they will never acknowledge any persecution of Christ or his, by themselves acted; their practice is to load them with pretended crimes, supposing thereby to justify themselves in their outrages; whereas they do by so much more make themselves guilty, by how much greater injury they do the just, by slandering them, and by hurting not only their persons, but also their good names: it was more injurious cruelty to put the skins of beasts on persecuted Christians; that they might thereby make their dogs worry them to death, than if they had slain them in their own habits: it is an undeniable courtesy in cruelty, to have one’s throat cut with a clean knife; to be condemned without foul and defamatory language, or under pretended and false crimes.

**34. ***{is it not written}* Ps. 82:6. That is, in the holy Scriptures (so ch. 15:25) which yourselves hold and profess, Hebr. *בתורתכם* *in your law*, *תורה* *torah*, sometimes imports only the five books of Moses; sometimes, the whole Scriptures, and the doctrine thereof, as here: sometimes, Moses, the Prophets, and the Psalms are distinguished.

*{ye are gods}* *אלהים* *elohim* in Scripture signifies either the true God, or something excellent; as angels, great and honorable men, princes, &c. Exod. 21:6; 1 Sam. 2:25; Exod. 4:16; 1 Sam. 28:13, because in government and judging the people (as Jehoshaphat told his judges, 2 Chron. 19:6) they judge not for man, but God.

**35. ***{unto whom the word of God came}* To whom God spoke in that Scripture; that is, to kings, his vicegerents, whom he calls gods, because they rule on earth for him.

*{cannot be broken}* *λυθῆναι*, *violated*, or *made void*, as Matt. 5:17, or destroyed, as ch. 7:23, or denied, as false and erroneous.

**36. ***{say ye of him}* Who is indeed, King of kings, and Lord of lords, Rev. 17:14. How injuriously, and blasphemously, do you say that I blaspheme, because I speak the truth, in affirming that I am the Son of God? You allow mortal men, kings, princes, and judges, to be called gods; because the Scripture styles them so, though they are in their own persons, wicked men; and why will you not acknowledge me to be the Son of God, concerning whom the same God of truth who indited the holy Scripture (2 Pet. 1:26) testified from heaven, that *I am his beloved Son in whom he is well pleased*, Matt. 17:5 & 3:17; 2 Pet. 1:17.

*{whom the Father hath sanctified}* If you grant this title (according to Scriptures) to princes who are but men, and too often the worst of men (the corruption of the best being in the effects thereof, ever worst and most dangerous) will you not permit it me, who am by nature *divina origine*, by an incomprehensible generation, the holy one of God; God of God, very God, as also very man?

**37. ***{if I do not the works of my Father}* That is, such works as none but God only can do; which indeed Christ did with the Father, by the same power and Deity: thus proves he himself the Son of God by nature; the substance, and essence of the Deity none can apprehend, the works and effects thereof, all ought to consider and understand; therefore Christ here, as it were, appeals to his own works done among them, that they might testify of him.

**38. ***{and I in him}* See ch. 14:10. See Hilar. *de trin.*, l. 7. He proves by his divine works done, that the Father is in him, and he in the Father; because they have one will, work and nature; though they are distinct persons, yet are they but one and the same God.

**39. ***{sought again to take him}* See ch. 7:32. Malice is restless and deaf; the voice of the most prudent cannot charm it: the more Christ labors to inform and convert them that they might be saved; the higher rises their ambition to destroy him, such is the invincible perverseness of unbelieving hearers, never satisfied with any teaching: when they hear high mysteries, they cry, *durus est hic sermo*, ch. 6:60. When mysterious things, they say, as v. 24, *tell us plainly*, and when he does tell them plainly, *I and my Father are one, then they took up stones to stone him*.

*{but he escaped}* By the secret power of his Deity; his hour being not yet come.

**40. ***{where John at first baptized}* John 1:28. Bethabara: there John first baptized, but afterward changed the place, ch. 3:23. This place Christ now chose, as remote, and safe from the Jews, so also to mind the people of those things which John testified of him, as v. 41.

**41. ***{John did no miracle}* He neither cast out devils, restored sight to the blind, raised the dead, &c. Yet the people believed him sent of God, and in doubt were they whether he were not the Christ, though he did no such works, Luke 3:15. Why then (might they say) should we not rather believe this to be the Christ, who has done so many and so admirable works; the rather for that John testified of this man (Matt. 3:11; John 1:26 and 3:27, &c.) and all that ever he said of him, the event shows to be true.

**42. ***{many believed}* For John’s testimony, and their own experience of Christ’s words and works, to which he remitted the unbelieving Jews, ch. 5:33, 36. Though the seed of God’s word seem sometimes to be bestowed in vain, yet in its season by God appointed, it shall appear in the elect and believers: wherever Jesus went he saw the fruits of his gracious labors; except only in the obstinate hearers, whom their own malice marked out to destruction. See 2 Cor. 4:3, 4.

## CHAPTER 11

**1. ***{Out of Bethany}* A village about two miles from Jerusalem, beyond the Mount of Olives, mentioned, Luke 10:38.

*{town of Mary}* Where they dwelt. The evangelist describes Lazarus by his two sisters, Mary and Martha, of whom the former evangelists had spoken, as zealous and excellent followers of Christ, and this history is exactly written, as being a great demonstration of the divine power of Christ, opportunely fitted to prevent the dangerous scandal of the cross, Christ’s death and passion, then instant.

**2. ***{it was that Mary}* Matt. 26:7. This is here spoken by way of anticipation; as if he said, the same of whom you shall read, ch. 12:3 (Matt. 26:6; Mark 14:4), there were divers of that name, therefore he here sets down this note of difference.

**3. ***{Behold, he whom thou lovest, is sick}* He of our family whom you love, is sick: he loved others also, as this evangelist, ch. 13:23, &c. They say not, our brother, as pretending to any merit of their own; nor do they say Lazarus, as relating to anything in him, for which Christ honored him with the name of friend, they seem only to remember him of his own love, which indeed being infinite and eternal, could have no cause but itself: a short; but an effectual message: a longer form of prayer is not prohibited, where need requires, but the main is, to unload our wants and desires into the bosom of Christ, and to desire his help; which they sufficiently did in this modest and implied supplication: they prescribe him not, but believe it to be enough to inform, so divine a lover, of their danger and want: their confidence was not in any length, or compass of their message, but in the surest ground of confidence, the love of Christ, who never deserts those he loves, and best knows how and when to relieve and deliver them.

**4. ***{not unto death}* Because he purposed to restore and raise him again presently.

**5. ***{Now Jesus loved, &c.}* This the evangelist inserts, the better to expound the message, v. 3. This family was religious, therefore Christ loved that therein, which himself had given; as God, the fountain of all good; as man, he had not only a reasonable soul, but also human affections, as sorrow, love, joy, anger, being in all like us, sin only excepted: thus loving, he justified the degrees of love, and honored that sacred life of this life, holy friendship, which can be only between the good and wise.

**6. ***{he abode two days still}* That Lazarus might die, and be buried, lest they should have said, *ὅτι* *οὔπω* *τελευτήσαντα* *αὐτὸν* *ἀνέστησε*, Chrysost. *hom.* 61, that he had raised him up, who was not yet dead: by this delay we may learn that he does not forget: or neglect us in our distresses: but only stays for that time, which shall best serve to advance his glory and our salvation.

**7. ***{after that, he said to his disciples}* After two days, v. 6. Lest his disciples might have thought that he neglected Lazarus, for fear of danger to himself.

*{into Judaea again}* Into the region of the tribes of Juda and Benjamin, (for, Judea is here strictly taken, see Matt. 2:1; Luke 6:4) whereof Pilate was governor. Luke 3:1. For they were at this time beyond Jordan, ch. 10:40, under the jurisdiction of Herod the Tetrarch. See Luke 13:31. He had a little before left Judea, ch. 10:39, 40, to avoid the persecution of the enraged Jews; and now lest his disciples should fear to return with him (who were to be principal witnesses of the ensuing miracle) he acquaints them with his purpose, and arms them against their fear.

**8. ***{lately sought to stone thee}* Ch. 8:59 and 10:31. It is but about two months past that they would have murdered you; and will you now cast yourself into the same danger again? Here were two great imperfections in their love to the person of Christ their Master, and zeal for his safety: first in that they considered not that Christ must once die for the elect to give them eternal life; secondly in that they feared that he might either be surprised at unawares, or suffer death against his will as being overpowered by men, though he had taught them the contrary, as before, so lately, ch. 10:17, 18. Chrysostom’s conjecture here, is probable, that they much feared their Master’s life and safety, but most their own.

**9. ***{are there not twelve hours in the day?}* The Jews, both summer and winter, divided the day into twelve hours: for they made them long or short, according to the length of the day, between sun and sun.

*{if any man walk in the day}* Meaning, that he that walks in his calling, and has the light of God for his guide, needs not fear: he that has but daylight, walks secure; how much more should you be confident, who have me with you: or he means, the appointed hour of my suffering is not yet come; no man can precipitate the day, nor any worldly power cut off the hours thereof, which God has appointed: we need not fear night before the appointed hour (see Job 14:5; Matt. 10:30), neither our ends before the determined time: Christ often escaped and fled from his persecutors; his ministry being not yet accomplished (who came to teach, as well as to suffer for us) that we might learn not to tempt God by any willful and needless exposing ourselves to our enemies’ malice.

**10. ***{he stumbleth, &c.}* He continues in his allegory, to show them that they had yet no cause of fear while they went with him, who was the light of the world, ch. 9:5: for, as Euthymius says, the power of Christ leads or protects them; perpetual fear of falling into destruction have they, who are not with Christ.

**11. ***{Lazarus our friend sleepeth}* The Hebrews were wont to call death a sleep, to intimate their assurance of the resurrection. See 1 Kings 2:10; 1 Kings 11:43 & 15:8, 24.

*{that I may awake him}* Herein he comforts them by declaring his power, who had the issues of life and death in his hands: it was as easy to him to raise the dead, as to awake one from sleep.

**12. ***{if he sleep he shall do well}* Or *shall amend or recover*: that is a good sign of health; why will you go into hazard, to awake him?

**13. ***{Jesus spake of his death}* Their fear of going again to Judea, made them not to understand (which they easily might) for who would go a day’s journey or more, to awake a sleeping friend? That Jesus spoke of Lazarus’ death; and his raising him up again.

**14. ***{Then said Jesus unto them plainly}* Without any allegory, or metaphor. *Lazarus is dead*: nor does he yet say, what he meant to do, will raise him again, by that divine power which in the appointed time shall raise the dead; but as speaking of some easy matter, *but I go, that I may awake him*, v. 11. Other circumstances he would not foretell them, but concealed them until the miracle done might speak its own excellency.

**15. ***{that I was not there}* That I was not bodily present at Bethany with Lazarus: in his Deity he was.

*{ye may believe}* That your faith may be strengthened, when you shall see me raise one that had been four days dead.

**16. ***{called Didymus}* A twin *Δίδυμος*, Suidas interprets *δίδυμοι* by *συμφυεῖς*, *simul nati*; so the interpreter of the Syriac *Thauma, qui dicitur, Gemellus*, *תמא* it is of the Hebrew *תאם* *taam*, *geminus fuit*: profane histories mention many of this name.

*{let us also go, &c.}* Which he speaks, either encouraging his fellow disciples to follow Christ: or intimating the danger they should run into, if they were in Judea with him.

**17. ***{Then when Jesus came he found, &c.}* *ἐλθὼν* *οὖν* *ὁ* *Ἰησοῦς*: *then*, or *therefore coming found*, &c., not that he found that by coming thither which he knew not before: but that so it was indeed as he had spoken concerning Lazarus’ death: which served to confirm the disciples in the assurance of his divine omniscience, when they reckoned the days: and of his omnipotence, when they saw him raise one to life who had been so long dead.

**18. ***{about fifteen furlongs off}* That is almost two miles. See ch. 6:19.

**19. ***{to comfort them concerning their brother}* Syriac interprets, *ut loquerentur cum corde earum*, an Hebrew manner of expression, for, to speak comfortably: as Isa. 40:1; 2 Sam. 19:7; 2 Chron. 30:22 & 32:6; Hos. 2:14. It appears that Lazarus’ death was divulged in Jerusalem, not without God’s divine providence, that the malicious Jews might not give out that Lazarus was not dead: and the evangelist mentions this company, to show the certainty of the miracle, whereof there were so many witnesses, and those not only of the neighbors and family, but also of Jerusalem.

**20. ***{Mary sate still in the house}* Martha being first informed of the approach of Christ, goes out to meet him in hope of some better comfort than any of her former visitors could speak, but Mary not yet hearing of his coming, sat still in the house, as a mourner with her comforters.

**21. ***{Lord if thou hadst been here}* This was an effect of her infirmity, wherein she came short of the Centurion’s faith. See Luke 7:7.

**22. ***{but now I know}* Now, though my brother be dead, yet do I believe that which you said to our messenger, v. 4, *this sickness is not unto death, but for the glory of God*, &c.

**23. ***{thy brother shall rise again}* Be of good cheer, your brother shall live again: he answers nothing to her complaint for his absence, he would give account thereof in his act of raising the dead, rather than in words; but to comfort her, speaks of his present raising him; so explaining that which he had said to his disciples, v. 11. Neither does he say, I will ask of my Father that he may rise; but, *thy brother shall rise*, that she might not think that he should obtain as a mere man, but that he also is the resurrection and the life. See v. 25.

**24. ***{I know that he shall rise again, &c.}* In the general resurrection, this it seems she said, because Jesus said indefinitely, *thy brother shall rise again*, not adding, now presently; taking it, as we all do, for a general comfort, that we shall be raised again, and not perish in death.

**25. ***{I am the resurrection}* That is, the author thereof; these words contain a secret or implied promise, (of which, v. 40) as if he said, I can and will now raise your brother again.

*{and the life}* Ch. 6:35. Giving life to all that live, the author of eternal life to the elect, which deservedly may be styled life; by my human sufferings, I shall purchase and merit it for them; by the power of my Deity, I both give it them, and preserve it to them.

*{he that believeth in me}* Faith is a spiritual resurrection of the soul; wherein believers begin to lead a new life. See ch. 5:2, 24, &c.; Eph. 2:5; Rom. 5:17; 1 Cor. 15:21.

*{though he were dead}* In body, yet shall he live body and soul to eternity, so incorruptible a seed is the word of God, by which we are born again, that *it liveth and abideth for ever*, 1 Pet. 1:23.

**26. ***{shall never die}* *οὐ* *μὴ* *ἀποθάνῃ* *εἰς* *τὸν* *αἰῶνα*, he shall not die eternally: he shall not die the second death (Rev. 20:6) which is a separation of body and soul from God eternally, Rev. 2:11.

**27. ***{I believe}* *πεπίστευκα*, *I have believed*, and now do believe: meaning, that it was with her, no new, or light opinion, but that in which she was, and had been constant: and so ch. 6:69; 1 John 4:16.

*{which should come}* As Matt. 11:3, meaning the Messias whom God promised by the prophets, Greek *εἰς* *τὸν* *κόσμον* *ἐρχόμενος*, which was to come into the world: this relates to God’s decree, and promises made to the fathers, and believers of all ages before Christ’s incarnation, that he that should come, because God, (all whose words are yea and amen) had so determined and promised by all the prophets.

**28. ***{she went her way, and called, &c.}* Very likely Christ bid her so do though the evangelist for brevity’s sake, does not mention it.

*{secretly}* Whispering to her, that the Jews might not hear, as Euthymius thinks; Jesus would not go into the house before he had done that admirable work for which he came; that the Jews accompanying Mary and following her out of the house (v. 31) to the grave of Lazarus, might (though against their wills) be made witnesses of the miracles.

*{and called for thee}* Commanded me to call you.

**29. ***{she arose quickly}* Not acquainting the Jews with the occasion of her sudden leaving them, who came to comfort her; probably she knew the disaffection of some of the Jews then present, toward Christ (see v. 46) and therefore would say nothing to them concerning his coming; neither did she yet know what Christ would command or do for them.

**30. ***{was not yet come into the town}* Into Bethany: see on v. 28. Therefore went Mary (having knowledge of his arrival) out to meet him.

**31. ***{she goeth unto the grave}* *εἰς* *τὸ* *μνημεῖον*, to the tomb or monument of her brother Lazarus, which was according to the manner, out of the town; and their custom was to build them so, as that there was passage into them, when the covers were taken off. See ch. 20:5, 6, 8, 11 and here v. 38.

*{to weep there}* To sit by the grave, and ease her grief with weeping.

**32. ***{Lord if thou hadst been here, &c.}* These sisters’ grief was one, and their infirmity of faith alike, v. 21. Possibly the sisters, in Christ’s long delay, had spoken these things one to another: her words were brief, the rest she supplied with tears.

**33. ***{he groaned in his spirit}* Such sympathy and compassion had he, to weep with those that wept (see Rom. 12:15) that he inwardly groaned, or from the heart.

*{was troubled}* *ἐτάραξεν* *ἑαυτὸν*, *he troubled himself*, considering the misery of man subject to death by reason of sin. Christ had human affections, and perturbations of mind, without any sin: much like the agitation of some clear and pure water in a glass, so did they move his human soul, but stirred up no pollution of sin in excess or defect.

**34. ***{where have ye laid him}* He asks not because he was ignorant (for he that knew of his death being absent, knew also where they had buried the dead) but for their sakes who were present, attending to show him the place, expecting what he would do, he asks (says Nazianzen, *orat.* 35) for he was man; but he raised him from the dead, *Θεὸς* *γὰρ* *ἦν*, for he was also God.

**35. ***{Jesus wept}* As man, he wept, as God he raised the dead; there was human affection (as v. 33) here divine power: there he wept as man, but far above man was he, when he commanded the dead to come out of the grave bound hand and foot. Ambros. *ep.*, l. 7, 47. See Hilar. *de trin.*, lib. 10, near the end. *ille flevit, ne tu homo diu fleres*, says Ambros. *de fide*, l. 2. He wept (and suffered all things as man for you) that you, O man, might not weep long, or to eternity.

**36. ***{Behold how he loved him}* They judge of his love by his tears, as of the cause by its effect; we most bewail that loss, which we loved most.

**37. ***{Which opened the eyes of the blind}* Ch. 9:6. Some think that they here question his doing that miracle, as if they intimated, either that the man was not indeed born blind; or that there was some collusion, or they dreamed of some defect of his power. See Ps. 78:19.

**38. ***{again groaning}* As v. 33. Christ answers not their words, with words, but groans, who meant presently to answer to the satisfaction of the well-meaning, and to the cavillers’ conviction by a wonderful work in raising Lazarus from the dead.

*{in himself}* In his Spirit, v. 33.

*{a stone lay upon it}* To defend the dead from injury; as the manner among them was. See Matt. 27:60. This made the miracle more evident, and freed it from suspicion of any compact or imposture.

**39. ***{take ye away the stone}* He could have commanded that away by the unseen ministry of angels; but he would have them eyewitnesses of that which he did, lest they should have said here, as of the blind man cured, ch. 9:9, *he is like him*, but not the same.

**40. ***{See the glory of God}* That is, a miracle which I shall do by the sovereign power of God, whereby he shall be glorified.

*{then they took away the stone}* As Jesus had commanded; most probably the whole company then present, must in the opening of the vault, wherein was the body so many days dead, find sufficient assurance that he was indeed dead, by the loathsome smell of that, which God’s providence so disposed to the greater evidence of the miracle; and to the confirmation of the faith of the resurrection from the dead.

**41. ***{father I thank thee}* Jesus being in one person both God and man, calls him Father, as God, and gives thanks as man.

**42. ***{I knew that thou}* It is not new or unexpected, *I knew thou always hearest me*. So it is said, Heb. 5:7, that *he was heard in that he feared*; yet he tasted of that cup which he deprecated, Matt. 26:39. Note here that God even then hears us, when he gives not to our will and desire, but to his own ever blessed will, which we should ever submit to, and most desire.

*{that they may believe}* This expresses that which he said, *because of the people*, they might possibly think that I do these things by magical power, as the blasphemous Pharisees said, but I thus said, that they may believe that you sent me, and work by me.

**43. ***{with a loud voice}* That all might hear: he whispered not as enchanters use; but cried aloud, that this voice might be a *symbolum* of that trumpet which shall once awake and call the dead out of their graves, 1 Cor. 15:52; 1 Thess. 4:16; John 5:28, 29.

*{Lazarus come forth}* It was now only the carcass of Lazarus, a lifeless body, until the Lord of the spirits of the just, caused the soul to return unto it again, thus quickens he the dead, and calls those things which be not, as though they were, Rom. 4:17.

*{come forth}* Of the sepulcher; thus calls he the dead, as if they lived; (so do they to God, who are dead to men) this shows what he meant by waking Lazarus, v. 11: arise from the dead, and come out, that all these present may see you truly and really raised from the dead.

**44. ***{came forth}* The bands giving so much way that he might come forth: which, it seems, Christ would not so much loose, but that his present friends might see that they were, as they had tied them when they buried him.

**45. ***{Believed on him}* That he was the very Messias and Savior of the world, sent of God: thus were they strengthened in Lazarus’ infirmity, and God glorified in his death and raising again, as v. 4.

**46. ***{but some of them went, &c.}* Euthymius conjectures that they were some of them who said, could not this man, &c., v. 37, so invincible is malice, if he could and should evidently restore sight to the blind, and raise the dead; neither would they so believe: these not only believed not, but went to the Pharisees, as it were, to admonish them, to take some course with Jesus, that others might not believe in him, yet did they herein but cast on oil to quench the fire; they served as witnesses to condemn their own and the Pharisees’ unbelief and obstinacy; but they more confirmed the truth of the miracle which believers testified, and unbelievers confessed. So their sealing and guarding Christ’s sepulcher rendered the truth of his death and resurrection more evident, and undoubted, it is a dangerous thing to err from the truth; there is in man a mischievous self-love, putting him on to defend his errors, by erring more. Arnobius, *adv. Gent.*, l. 6, noted it well: *quod semel sine ratione fecistis*, &c. *That which you have once done without reason, you defend, lest you should seem to have once been ignorant: you think it’s better, non vinci, not to be overcome, than to yield to a confessed truth.*

**47. ***{Then gathered the chief priests}* See Matt. 26:3. When they heard that Jesus was come again, and contrary to their will and authority, still wrought such evident miracles, so near them; they were cut to the heart: their envy made them mad against him. Some of the spectators of that which Christ did at Lazarus’ grave, had related the whole matter and manner of his raising the dead Lazarus; therefore they call the Synedrion or great council to advise what they should do to Jesus.

**48. ***{If we let him thus alone}* Thus, endeavor they to resist God’s will by their counsels: too common a practice among men of this world; here they betray a marvelous blindness, and desperate rage against Christ; why consider you fighters against God, how easily that power which raised the dead, can overthrow all your designs, and counsels against him: how easily can he smite you all dead, that but commanded, and the dead came forth of the grave? Men can by their power (God so permitting) kill the living, but God only can raise the dead: what shall your success at last be, who strive with your maker?

*{take away both our place and nation}* That is, they will dispossess us of our country, as the Chaldees have formerly done, thus they pretended a necessity of state against Christ; whereas Tertullian says well, *certè nulla necessitas praetendi potest delinquendi ab iis, quibus una incumbit necessitas non delinquendi*, certainly no necessity to sin can be pretended by those, on whom lies one necessity not to sin.

**49. ***{that same year}* For that present time he was suffragan. See c. 18:13 and on Luke 3:2.

**50. ***{nor consider}* See c. 18:14. He pretends to some insight into state interests, and yet knew not what he said himself: he thought he had now engrossed the spirit of counsel and policy; and therefore moves by an argument from the safety of the state; but it seems grounded on that former result of the Sanhedrin which was:—*if we let him alone—the Romans shall come*, &c., v. 48. Caiphas presently answers, as it were resolved upon the question, rather let one man die, than the whole nation of the Jews perish, if you know not that, you know nothing, neither consider things expedient.

*{and that the whole nation perish not}* That is, rather than the whole nation should perish: Caiphas took it for concluded, that either Christ, or their nation must perish, and as he foolishly thought, that of two evils he designed the least to be chosen; that is, that Christ should rather perish, than their nation.

**51. ***{But being High Priest that year}* He spoke not this by his own spirit or instinct, but God would have this prophecy come from the high priest’s mouth, to leave the Jews more inexcusable in their unbelief, he being so public a person.

**52. ***{gathered together in one, &c.}* That he might gather the church, the elect of the Jews and Gentiles, dispersed by the providence of God, distributed, and dwelling in every nation of the world. See ch. 10:16 & 12:32; Eph. 2:13.

**53. ***{from that day forth they took counsel}* They all unanimously consented to Caiphas’ advice, as if that had been an oracle, sufficient to take away all scruple of conscience, and to assure them, that they might justly destroy Jesus: so wretchedly can Satan blind the malicious, that they shall be confident they do wondrous well to persecute God’s elect to death. See ch. 16:2. They took counsel: *συνεβουλεύσαντο*, by a common consent of the whole Sanhedrin.

**54. ***{Walked no more}* Conversed no more publicly, because his hour to suffer, was not yet come. See ch. 7:30 and 8:20.

*{near to the wilderness}* Far from the malicious Pharisees at Jerusalem.

*{a city called Ephraim}* Some read *Ephrem*, *Εφρὴμ*: Jerome *de loc. Hebr.* reads as we, *Ephraim juxta desertum*—see Joh. Drus. *ad voc. N.T. com.*, 1, and *praeterit.*, lib. 4, *in loc.*

**55. ***{To purify themselves}* To prepare themselves by prayer, sacrifices, and paying of vows, or other legal ceremonies; which though (as being carnal rites) they could not sanctify (Heb. 9:9, 12), yet the faithful both acknowledged their sins, and (under these shadows) that purification which was to be accomplished in them by the sacred blood of Christ.

**56. ***{then sought they for Jesus}* For he used to come up before the feast days, to teach the people.

**57. ***{knew where he were}* They thought, because they saw him not yet at the feast, that he had hidden himself for fear, but he came in now more publicly, and with greater solemnity than at any time before, and five days in the temple, and city freely spoke and conversed before their faces; neither could they hurt, or apprehend him until the time appointed, wherein he was to be offered up a living and consummatory sacrifice for our redemption and salvation.

*{he should shew it}* To the high priests and elders, that they might, according to the decree of their council, apprehend him, yet we read of none that did or could obey this mandate of theirs, but that son of perdition, Judas that betrayed Christ.

## CHAPTER 12

**1. ***{Six days before the Passover}* *πρὸ* *ἓξ* *ἡμερῶν*, &c., *before six days of*, &c. Here is a traduction of the word *πρὸ*, before, as if it were written, *ἓξ* *ἡμέραις* *πρὸ* *τὸ* *πάσχα*, as we give it, *six days before the Passover*, Tremellius says it is a Syriac manner of speaking.

**2. ***{There they made him a supper}* In the house of Simon the leper, for there Mary anointed him with precious oil: Lazarus is here mentioned as a trophy of Christ’s victory over death; and therefore is reckoned among the guests, to express the certainty and reality of his raising from the dead; that we may know that it was no vision, or appearance of things which were not: and that he did not presently die again, but lived to eat, drink, and converse with men, as formerly; that believers might have certain testimony of the divine power of Christ in his resurrection, and that the enemies might also thereby be convicted.

**3. ***{of spikenard}* It was a custom in those parts, to use precious odors and ointments at solemn feasts. See Mark 14:3 and Augustine, *de doctr. Christian.*, lib. 3, cap. 12; Plin., l. 13, c. 3.

*{anointed the feet}* The other evangelists say she poured it on his head, Matt. 26:7; Mark 14:3, whereas this evangelist says, *she anointed his feet*, we must know that both are true: for she anointed as Augustine says, *de consens. Evang.*, l. 2, c. 79, *non solum caput, sed & pedes Domini*: this seems to have been beyond the usual custom, to anoint the feet, Plin. *nat. hist.*, l. 13, c. 3, notes it among the Romans, for a kind of foreign luxury: *vidimus* (says he) *etiam vestigia pedum tingi*, and further, that P. Licinius Crassus, and L. Julius Caesar, being censors, made a decree, that none should sell *unguenta exotica*, foreign ointments: but these things were much used among the Eastern people; and here, as Mary did it in thankful acknowledgment of the mercy of Christ showed them, as also of her love to him; so also he justifying her fact, declares the use of God’s creatures, good and respectively lawful, where the abuse is condemnable.

*{and wiped his feet with her hair}* This was a sign of her officious humility; but it is probable which some conjecture, from Luke 7:38, that the order of things here done is inverted: for either she first wiped his feet: and afterward anointed them, as Luke 7:38, or else she should have more anointed her own hair, than Jesus’ feet.

**4. ***{One of his disciples}* The other evangelists speak of more; this names only Judas, possibly because he was the author and beginner of the murmuring; and it may be (which Augustine conjectures) the rest were simply led thereto, *propter pauperum curam*: for, and because of the true care they indeed had of the poor, which Judas only pretended to, for a veil of his covetousness: so easily are good intentions abused to schism and faction: and so commonly do hypocrites disguise and cloak their most impious designs with fair shows of religious charity. Judas is here called *Ἰούδας* *Σίμωνος* *Ἰσκαριώτης*, *Judas son of Simon* (*ἀπὸ* *τῆς* *Καριώτου*, as some some read) or *Cariot*, or *Iscariot*.

**5. ***{Sold for three hundred pence}* See on Matt. 18:28 and on Mark 14:5.

**6. ***{he was a thief}* That is, as Nonnus interprets *φύλαξ* *κακὸς*, a bad keeper of the stock, from whence he impropriated, to lay up for himself, that he might make himself an estate, and store for his subsistence, when he had forsaken Christ, as it is likely he had already designed; because he had often heard Jesus mention his suffering and the dispersion and persecution of his disciples at hand.

*{had the bag}* Ch. 13:29. He had charge of the purse and stock of money wherewith they that administered to Christ furnished him to buy necessaries: see Luke 8:3. Gr. *γλωσσόκομον*, *a purse or bag*, *linguae speciem habens*, says Aemilius Portus, interprets Suidas, hereof Judas was treasurer or steward: Jesus knew his mind, yet committed that office to none other of the twelve: Judas was fittest for the bag; it is sin and pity to impropriate the faithful ministers of Christ, or with any secular employments to distract them from the ministry of the word and sacraments: Judas was discontented, for that this precious ointment had not rather been brought in ready money and put into his bag, that he might have shared therein. The hypocrite’s own private interest crossed, is that which troubles him, when he pretends the cause of piety and religion, for which he takes as much care, as Judas did for the poor.

**7. ***{hath she kept this}* Thus the providence of God had disposed her pious intention, which was to honor Christ with the best of her substance, who esteemed nothing too good or costly to bestow on him; though possibly she might not at present think of his death and burial near, which he knew, therefore said he, *let her alone*; she has not bestowed this cost in vain, it is instead of embalming and preparation of me to my grave, who must very shortly die; she shall not then perform this office, permit her now: see Matt. 26:12; Mark 14:8.

**8. ***{the poor always}* See Matt. 26:11. Thus he answers Judas’ objection; and this verb plural *ἔχετε* shows, that he had led some of his fellow disciples into this murmuring.

**9. ***{not for Jesus’ sake only}* They would fain satisfy their own curiosity, by seeing whether that were true which they had heard reported of Lazarus’ raising from the dead: they would either congratulate, hear, see, and ask him some questions concerning the truth of his death, and possibly of the state and condition of departed souls in the other world. It may well be, that many before this time had come from Jerusalem to see and inquire of Lazarus; but now they came to see Jesus and Lazarus together, both the dead man raised, and the author of the miracle, the restorer of his life.

**10. ***{that they might put Lazarus also to death}* Such is the mischievous reach of malice: now they had nothing to calumniate, neither the violation of the Sabbath, assistance of Beelzebub, &c. This stings them so, that they consult how to suppress the very memory thereof, by destroying him whom Christ had delivered from death.

**11. ***{because that by reason of him many, &c.}* Because many saw, heard, and conferred with Lazarus, whom many knew dead four days, and saw raised from the dead again by the voice of Jesus calling him out of his grave: therefore, convicted by so evident a miracle, they withdrew themselves from them who conspired to destroy Jesus, and believed on him as the Messias.

**12. ***{on the next day}* Matt. 21:8. The next day after that supper at Bethany, mentioned v. 2. It seems to have been the fifth day before the Passover. See v. 1.

**13. ***{took branches of palm trees}* They took, *τὰ* *βαΐα*, boughs or branches of palm trees: this they were wont to do in token of rejoicing and victory. See 1 Macc. 13:51. *They entered with praise, καὶ βαΐων*, &c. So 2 Macc. 10:7. *They bare branches and fair boughs, ἔτι δὲ φοίνικας, and palms also, and sang psalms unto him who had given them good success.* So Rev. 7:9, *καὶ* *φοίνικες*, *and palms in their hands*. The Jews were commanded to take such branches at the feast of tabernacles, Lev. 23:40, *כפת* *תמרים* *cappoth temarim*, boughs of palm trees; which the Greek, in some copies give by the word *βαΐα* here used, in others *κάλλυνθρα* *φοινίκων*: the Chaldee by *לולבין* *lulabin*, as growing out of the heart of the tree: it was in use also among other nations to carry the branches of this tree in their hands, in sign of victory. See Pausan. *in Arcadic.* and Cael. Rhodigin. *lect. antiq.*, lib. 5, c. 6. The palm tree is green and flourishing, Ps. 92:13, the leaves thereof fall not (Plin. *Nat. Hist.*, l. 16, c. 20 and so like the saints, Ps. 1:3) of tall and upright stature: hereto the church is likened, Cant. 7:7, 8. With these branches in their hands, the Israelites went about the altar, and said *הושיעה* *נא* *Hosanna*, *Save now I beseech thee Lord*, Ps. 118:25. This now the people (though at another time of the year) by God’s providence did, to show that all the legal feasts were to have their accomplishment in Christ who was shortly to triumph over all spiritual enemies: and that the honor of those solemnities did of right appertain to him.

**14. ***{when he had found}* Greek *εὑρὼν*; the sense is, *having gotten, or obtained*. So Luke 1:30, *εὗρες* *χάριν*, he sent his disciples to fetch it for him, Matt. 21:2, &c.; Luke 19:30.

**15. ***{fear not}* Zech. 9:9; Matt. 21:5. The evangelist here cites not the very words of the prophet, Zech. 9:9, where we read *הריעי* *jubila filia Jerusalem* (the verb *רוע* is either a shouting for joy, as conquerors used, or sorrow, as the conquered) which the 70 give, *χαῖρε* *σφόδρα*, *θύγατερ* *Σιὼν*, &c., *vehemently rejoice*, &c. John here following the main drift of the prophecy in its application to the present occasion, gives it, *μὴ* *φοβοῦ* *θύγατερ* *Σιὼν*—*fear not, &c.*, for besides that rejoicing and fear are incompatible, and mutually exclusive of each other, the princes and people of the Jews could not but be troubled at these extraordinary voices, and acclamations of the multitude, v. 13, sounding out in the open streets of the imperial city, that name of king, suspected and hated of the Romans, under whose power and government the Jews now were: probably therefore they feared the Romans’ sword, if they rebelled against them, or the heavy exactions of a new king; therefore God warned them by the holy prophet, long before, that they should not need to fear any of these things by their spiritual King Messias, who should not at all be ambitious of secular honor nor covetous of worldly riches, and therefore he should be no tyrant, cruel, rapacious, hard or terrible prince, but a meek, humble, just, holy, and beneficent man, who should not come to pillage, or judge and revenge, but to save them; they ought therefore rather to rejoice and shout for joy, than to fear and be troubled at his approach.

*{daughter of Sion}* That is, *church of Israel*, used to assemble for God’s public worship in their solemnities, in the temple built on Mount Sion in Jerusalem.

*{behold thy King}* Christ your spiritual and eternal King: this his humility showed that his kingdom was not of this world, and consisting in external and secular pomp, magnificence, or outward things.

**16. ***{these things understood not his disciples}* Being commanded in that manner to fetch the ass, they were so far from doing it to the end that the Scripture might be fulfilled, that they understood not these things.

*{they remembered that these things were written}* Some refer this to the disciples (and that most probably) that they acted, but for present, understood not that it was to fulfill any Scripture, but after that Christ was ascended, and the Holy Ghost given, to lead them into all truth, and to bring all things necessary to their remembrance: others refer it to the multitude in entertaining him so as was prophesied: in either we may observe the truth of God fulfilled; and the sincerity of the Gospel, free from all compact and fraud in so much that the very actors, and witnesses of others’ actions therein historified, for present knew not what these things meant.

**20. ***{certain Greeks among them}* Either proselytes or heathens converted to the true religion professed in Israel; as 1 Kings 8:41, or Jews of Greece; that is, whose ancestors were Jews, though themselves were bred and born in Greece; for these also came up to the feast. See Acts 6:1.

**21. ***{Sir we would see Jesus}* That is, speak to him, or hear him in private: no doubt but they had heard much of his admirable works; and probably they might sometimes see and hear him in the multitude; but they desired favor to be admitted to his presence.

**22. ***{Andrew and Philip told Jesus}* As not presuming to bring any to him without his leave.

**23. ***{that the Son of man should be glorified}* He answers not to the information of his disciples, or the desire of the Greeks, but that which was of importance to his hearers; that the time of his death, resurrection, ascension, and manifestation to the world by the Gospel preached, was now at hand, and so might possibly intimate that the Grecians should more happily be satisfied very shortly in hearing and believing the Gospel declaring him their Savior and Redeemer, than in seeing him now in the form of a servant and man of sorrows.

**24. ***{except a corn of wheat}* He takes up this allegory to prepossess an objection which some might have made when they should have seen his death and passion: how said Christ, the Son of Man should be glorified, when now we see him dead and buried? Why, consider that which is near, and familiar unto you: *Frumentum si servas perdis, si seminas renovas*, Greg. *hom.* 32, *in Evang.* If you keep the grains unsowed, you lose your crop; but by casting them into the ground you renew them. The death of Christ was as sowing of corn, which seeming lost, proves our greater advantage.

**25. ***{he that loveth his life}* *ὁ* *χαριζόμενος* *αὐτῇ* *παρὰ* *τὸ* *δέον*, he that indulges thereto more than he ought, Chrysost. *hom.* 66, or if the love of this life, ease, or pleasures, let him from coming to Christ (see Matt. 10:39 & 16:25; Mark 8:35; Luke 9:24 & 17:33) or, shall, to save it, deny me.

*{he that hateth his life}* That sets not dear by his life for Christ’s sake, as Acts 21:13. He that is ready to lose his life, *Qui retinendae vitae non nimis cupidus est*, Euthymius. So that he may seem to hate, or not value it when the truth and honor of Christ requires his exposing it. See Matt. 10:39; Luke 14:26.

**26. ***{let him follow me}* That is, imitate me, in my ready obedience of God, to the death.

*{and where I am, there also, &c.}* For there shall neither be error in the way, nor end in death, where any man follows me; he cannot but be happy that is with Christ.

*{him will my Father honour}* Making him a coheir with Christ, Rom. 8:17.

**27. ***{now is my soul troubled}* Out of a deep sense of God’s wrath against sins, for which he was to suffer, he flies to prayer, yet prefers his Father’s will and glory, and our salvation, before his own life.

*{save me from this hour}* Of death now instant. See Luke 22:42; Matt. 26:39; Mark 14:35. *ταῦτα* *τῆς* *ἀνθρωπίνης* *φύσεως* *τὰ* *ἰδιώματα*, Chrysost. *hom.* 66. These are the infirmities of human nature; but without sin, because in subjection to God’s will.

*{came I unto this hour}* That I might in the appointed time suffer for the redemption of your elect.

*{glorify thy Name}* Let your holy name be praised and magnified through the world for man’s redemption wrought in my passion.

*{then came there a voice from heaven}* A voice was heard from heaven. See Matt. 3:17. Or therefore came there a voice, because Jesus thus prayed, according as the apostle said, Heb. 5:7, *who in the days of his flesh, when he had offered up prayers and supplications, with strong crying and tears unto him that was able to save him from death, and was heard, in that he feared*. God therefore answered from heaven, *I have both glorified it, and I*, &c., as to comfort him against the sorrows of instant death, so to remove the scandal of the cross.

*{I have both glorified, &c.}* I have hitherto glorified my name by you in the many divine works done by you, and I will in your death, resurrection and ascension glorify the same, as also by the mission of the Holy Ghost, preaching of the Gospel, conversion and salvation of the elect, both of the Jews and Gentiles, to the uttermost part of the earth, the end of time, and all eternity.

**29. ***{said it thundered}* Many of them so said. This shows that it was no obscure whispering, but loud as thunder, though the unbelievers understood it not.

*{others said, an angel spake to him}* They who heard the words, said an angel spoke to him, so conjectured they, though they heard them delivered in the person of the Father, to whom he prayed.

**30. ***{came not because of me}* Not only for me to comfort me, but also to instruct you.

**31. ***{now is the judgment of this world}* The reformation and restoring of those things which were out of order.

*{the prince of this world}* Satan (so c. 14:30) shall be overcome by my death, Heb. 2:14.

**32. ***{if I be lifted up}* That is, crucified and *taken away* (c. 3:14) from the earth; as the Syriac gives it *ܐܬܪܝܡܬ* by *אתרימת* *etherimeth*, which is from the Hebrew word *רום* *rom*, which signifies both *lifted up*, as Gen. 7:17; Gen. 31:45, and *taken away*, Dan. 8:11.

*{draw all men unto me}* All the elect of all sorts, not only Jews, but also Gentiles: *Ex omni gente & conditione*—some of every nation and condition:—He says all, *Quia nemo salvus fit, nisi quem ipse traxerit*, No man is saved, but he whom Christ draws. Fulgen. *de incarn. & gra. D. Jesu*, c. 31. I will draw all the elect, as captives, *ὑπὸ* *τυράννῳ* *κατεχομένους*, Chrysost. *hom.* 66, held by some tyrant.

**33. ***{What death}* What manner of death; to wit, the death of the cross. This had he in express terms often spoken unto you his disciples, Matt. 20:19. Therefore some think that he now thus spoke to a multitude present, not plainly saying that they should crucify him, but yet so intimating it, that when they that believed or were to believe should see it, they might know that he who foreknew it, and revealed it to others, offered up himself a voluntary sacrifice.

**34. ***{We have heard}* Ps. 110:4. Some of them that were present, said, *We have heard out of the Law*, that is, our scribes and doctors taught us out of the Scriptures, for so Christ calls the Law, c. 10:34.

*{out of the Law}* The Scriptures, Ps. 89:36, 37; Ezek. 37:25. See on c. 10:34.

*{that Christ abideth for ever}* That he shall continue amongst the living: this they collected out of misunderstood Scriptures. See Ps. 72:17; Ps. 89:30. They marked not those places which concerned the state of his humiliation and death, as Dan. 9:26; Isa. 53; Ps. 22:17, 18. Had they well compared the one with the other, it would have better appeared to them, that Christ was by death to enter into his glory.

**35. ***{yet a little while the light}* He confirms that which he said concerning his death; and secretly taxes their willful blindness, who neither in his doctrine nor miracles understand him to be Christ, therefore resolves them not concerning that they asked, but intimates that both Christ and the Gospel, which now, as a clear light shined among them, should shortly be taken from them and they delivered up to their own obstinacy and ignorance of the means of salvation. See Matt. 21:43; Acts 13:46; Rom. 11.

**36. ***{departed and hid, &c.}* After his teaching in the day, now when it grew toward night (Mark 11:11) he went out of Jerusalem to lodge at Bethany, Matt. 21:17. Secretly with his disciples, that his enemies who laid wait for his life, might not know where to find him: for though we read not here that they took up stones, yet he that knew their hearts, saw them now growing hot, and ready to kill, therefore would he not stay there until it broke out into action. Chrysost.

**37. ***{yet they believed not}* That is, many of them believed not, some did.

**38. ***{Lord who hath believed, &c.}* Isa. 53:1; Rom. 10:16. This interrogation imports as often in holy Scripture, such a vehemency of speaking, as is with admiration, as if he said, Lord, how few will believe! It is strange that so few believe the evidence of the truth constantly affirmed by us all; certainly coming to pass, which according to your word, we preach unto them.

*{the arm of the Lord been revealed}* That is, the Gospel, which is the power of God to salvation to every one that believes, Rom. 1:16. By *זרוע* *zeroah*, an arm, the Hebrews usually expressed power, virtue, aid, relief, &c. See Luke 1:51.

**39. ***{therefore they could not believe}* This is spoken to show us, that contempt of God’s word, and willful shutting the eyes against the light of the Gospel, takes away all ability of believing to salvation. See Matt. 13:14, 15. *They could not*; that is say Chrysostom and Theophylact, they would not. They will, not indeed believe, nor is it in a wicked man’s power *velle credere*, to be willing to believe: for as faith is the gift of God (Eph. 2:8) so is a true and hearty desire thereof which God bestows on them only whom he will therein satisfy and make blessed, Matt. 5:6, and justly gives not unto those who undervalue his mercies, and despise his ordinance.

**40. ***{he hath blinded their eyes}* See on Matt. 13:14, 15; Rom. 1:21, 28; 2 Thess. 2:10, 11, 12.

**41. ***{when he saw his glory}* Isa. 6:1, 2.

*{and spake of him}* Of Christ. The evangelist collects that the prophecy and doctrine of Isaias concerned ages then to come: for he saw the glory of Christ under the Gospel, and accommodates it to those times of which he spoke: the vision indeed was in the year wherein King Uzziah died, Isa. 6:1, yet was it of things appertaining to the time and kingdom of Christ under the Gospel: see on Matt. 15:7.

**42. ***{put out of the synagogue}* That is, excommunicated, so c. 9:22. The vanity of Pharisees’ argument to dissuade men from believing in Christ, c. 7:48 (*have any of the rulers, or of the Pharisees believed on him?*) seems here answered: yes, *among the chief rulers also, many believed on him*: as also a further objection which might be made; why do they not profess that their faith openly? Why, for fear of the active malice of the Pharisees, lest they should excommunicate them, and so they should lose their credit and reputation with the people.

**43. ***{they loved the praise of men}* C. 5:44. To be honored of men, and to justify themselves before them. See Luke 16:15. The evangelist here notes the cause why few of the great men sincerely professed the Gospel, not because their consciences were not sufficiently convicted; but because they loved their own ease, honor, and riches, more than God’s honor, or their own salvation. So dangerous a thing it is for fear of men to dissemble faith. See Heb. 11:27; Matt. 10:33.

**44. ***{he that believeth on me, &c.}* This is not simply to be understood, but with reference to the Jews’ sense, who took him to be mere man: to them he says, the way to faith in God, is to believe in me; and that he spoke not of himself, as v. 49, 50, but that which God the Father gave him commission and command to speak. See Luke 10:16. And that it is the greatest injury to God, to disbelieve the Gospel, as if his truth were not sufficient for us: and, lastly, that they who hear the Gospel, must be confident, that therein they hear not men, but God, whose word and truth it is.

**45. ***{he that seeth me}* For he is the lively image and pattern of his person, Heb. 1:3, and one God with the Father, c. 14:9.

**46. ***{I come a light}* C. 3:19.

**47. ***{if any man}* C. 3:27.

*{judge him not}* I condemn him not now, but he shall be judged at the last day: I came now to call sinners to repentance, to preach the Gospel of peace.
