*{they may be made perfect in one}* Being joined to the Lord, and so united to him; and one to another, as true members of the mystical body of Christ, they may be partakers of the divine nature, 2 Pet. 1:4. See also 1 Cor. 6:17; 1 John 1:3; John 6:57, 58.

*{may know that thou hast sent me}* See on v. 21. That men seeing their godly conversation, who profess to be my disciples, may thereby know that I am of God.

*{and hast loved them}* That is, your grace, love and favor towards them, which directs and preserves them.

*{as thou hast loved me}* That you love them for my sake: all the effects of God’s love are directed to us through his love to Christ.

**24. ***{Father I will, &c.}* Ch. 12:26 & 14:3: that is, I desire and pray, that the elect may be united to me their Head, in the eternal blessedness in your kingdom of glory, coheirs with me thereof.

*{be with me}* In eternal glory and happiness, in which I shall shortly be with you.

**25. ***{the world hath not known thee}* The greatest part of the world, has not known you as they ought, (see Acts 17:23; Rom. 1:21, 28) in the unity of the Trinity: I only have known you, and they to whom I have revealed you, and your counsel, Matt. 11:27; John 8:55 & 7:29 & 10:15.

**26. ***{thy name}* Your mercy and goodness, by the doctrine of the Gospel which I have preached unto them, and the spirit of regeneration, whereby they may understand that which I have taught them.

*{that the love, &c.}* That you may love them for my sake as you have loved me their Head: and that they may by faith fall in their hearts, that love wherewith you love me, and themselves in me. See Rom. 5:5.

*{and I in them}* Dwelling in their hearts by the spirit of faith and sanctification, Eph. 3:17. *Perfectius eis notus*, Euthymius; being more perfectly known to them.

## CHAPTER 18

**1. ***{He went forth with his disciples}* Matt. 26:36. To show that he went out willingly to accomplish our redemption, understand by *ταῦτα*, these words, all that which this evangelist here records from the thirteenth chapter hitherto.

*{over the brook Kedron}* Mentioned, 2 Sam. 15:22; 1 Kings 15:13. It is (say some) the name of the valley, as well as of the brook running through the same betwixt Jerusalem and the Mount of Olives: it is a shallow brook: the Syriac translates it, *ad transitum pedis*; that is, a ford which may be passed over on foot. See on Luke 22:39. Over this brook and valley, went David and his soldiers weeping, up the Mount of Olives, 2 Sam. 15:23, 30.

**2. ***{Judas, &c. knew the place}* This he says, to show that Christ hid not himself: for in the night where could he more easily and certainly be found, than in that place, to which he often resorted in the night, Luke 21:37. This place Judas well knew, and that he should there find Christ, and Jesus knew that Judas would seek him there; therefore that he might according to his Father’s will, be found by Judas and suffer for the sin of man, he that could have avoided Judas’ finding, by going to some other more remote place with his disciples in the night and darkness undiscovered, would go into that very garden which he used to frequent, and Judas well knew.

**3. ***{having received a band of men}* Matt. 26:47; Mark 14:43; Luke 22:47, which he had obtained of the governor of the temple or rather which the priests and elders had entreated Pilate to assign for their assistance; that they might the more surely lay hold on him, and more securely bring him away with them: now though most probably their own Centurion commanded the company, yet because Judas was to bring them to the place where Jesus was, it is said that Judas received a band of men, &c.

*{cometh thither}* Into the garden, into which Christ entered, being now to expiate and satisfy for that sin also, which Adam (sinning in a garden) brought upon the world.

*{with lanterns and torches}* Jesus is apprehended as a malefactor, that we may be acquitted as innocent.

**4. ***{went forth}* That is, from that part of the garden where he was, towards them who came to apprehend him.

*{whom seek ye?}* He asks not as doubting, but to amate the guard, and that he might rather appear presenting himself, than be taken, as by force; seeing he wittingly and willingly offered up himself for our sins, as was prefigured in the law. See Exod. 35:5, 21, 22, 29; Lev. 22:21, 23; Num. 15:3 & 29:39; Deut. 16:10.

**5. ***{they answered him, Jesus of Nazareth}* They say not, we seek you, but Jesus, &c., as speaking to some third person: it seems then that they knew not Jesus present, speaking to them, though manifested with lights, and marked out by Judas’ treacherous kiss, and though often seen specially of the high priests’ servants, who ever noted him with a malicious eye. All which (as also the following words) shows, that he willingly offered himself.

*{Judas also, &c.}* Judas perplexed, in that, that the sign of his treason took not, and that he had in vain discovered himself, who probably hoped, that having saluted Jesus, the guard would presently have fallen in and laid hands on him, and so in the tumult, himself should not be discovered; he came no over to the disciples, from whose society he was departed, but stood with the enemies of Jesus.

**6. ***{fell to the ground}* No wonder: for as Augustine notes, *Deus latebat in carne*, the Deity was veiled in his humanity: Christ did this to make them know, that the arms, multitude and power they brought, could do nothing except God permitted: and to teach all obstinate enemies of his church, that they shall not be able to stand before his majesty judging in his glory, seeing they could not in his state of humiliation, and when their hour was, that they were permitted to judge him, then sustaining the person of a servant, and in their censure, a delinquent.

**7. ***{then asked he them again}* After that the same power which cast them down, permitted them to rise again.

*{they said, Jesus of Nazareth}* They persist in their obstinacy notwithstanding their present experience of a divine power casting them down at the word of Jesus: the most evident miracles can neither convert nor work upon the obstinate, until God turn their hearts.

**8. ***{let these go their way}* For he alone was to suffer for our redemption. He showed his love to them, unto the last: if you seek me only *ἐμαυτὸν* *παραδίδωμι*, Chrysostom, I yield myself to you.

**9. ***{that the saying might be fulfilled}* These are the evangelist’s words, concisely speaking, omitting these words, *this he said*, or *this was done*, that the saying, &c.

*{of whom thou givest me}* Ch. 17:12.

**10. ***{Simon Peter having a sword}* Matt. 26:41.

*{the servant’s name was Malchus}* He names the party, and where he was hurt, for the evidence of the story.

**11. ***{put up thy sword}* Christ prohibited him to smite, though in defense of his own sacred person; and he healed him that was hurt, though his enemy: to teach us first to do good to them, who persecute and hurt us. And secondly, that zeal must be limited by the bounds of every man’s own calling: we may not use the sword to private revenge. See Deut. 32:35; Rom. 12:19. Thirdly, that he willingly tasted that bitter cup of his passion. Fourthly, that he needed no external defense; he could have commanded legions of angels. Lastly, that no misinformed, or misplaced zeal is justifiable.

**12. ***{and the captain}* *χιλίαρχος* (see Acts 21:31), the tribune; some think these were such as we call high marshals, Henr. Bebel. *de Mag. & sacr. Rom.* A colonel or commander of a thousand soldiers, (of whose office and institution, see Fenestella *de Magistrat. Rom.*, cap. 16) so the interpreter of Suidas gives it also; so he says *σπεῖρα* is a troop, or cohort: we may understand this command to be of a regiment, or cohort, as the Romans called it. For as Vegetius, lib. 2, cap. 6, *de re militar.*, says, *In una legione decem cohortes esse debere*. So ibid. cap. 12, in one legion must be ten cohorts, the first whereof being the chief, consisting of 1105 footmen, and 132 horsemen, and being of the best quality, were commanded by the tribune; and were called *cohors miliaria*. See also Pomponius Laetus, *de Magistrat. Rom.* and Ra. Volaterran. *de Magistrat. & sacr. Rom.* However strong or full soever this armed company was, with those auxiliaries which the priests sent out with them, they could not take Christ until he yielded up himself, which this, *οὖν*, *then*, or *therefore*, seems to point at: then they took him, when he yielded; or therefore, because he rendered himself; otherwise, they were cast on the ground by his word only.

*{and bound him}* As a malefactor: this they did to please their impious governors: and this Jesus suffered to fulfill, that which preceded in his types, Isaac, Gen. 22:9, Joseph, Gen. 37:24, Sampson, Judg. 16:9 and the legal sacrifices which were bound and laid on the altar, and to deliver us from the bonds of death and hell, Ps. 116:3; Matt. 2:13.

**13. ***{to Annas first}* See Luke 3:2. Some conjecture that his house was in the way from the garden: others, that because no matter of great moment was acted at Annas’ house, besides his examining Jesus concerning his doctrine and disciples, v. 19, and that illegal and impious act of one of the servants smiting Jesus v. 22, therefore the other evangelists omit this part of the history, not so much as mentioning Annas, but so following the series of the history, as if from the garden they *led Jesus first to Caiaphas*, Matt. 26:57; Mark 14:53; Luke 22:54, but here it is manifest, that they led him to Annas first. See on Luke 22:6.

*{high priest that same year}* Annas was high priest; and this office was for a time of life by God’s ordinance, Caiaphas was his suffragan, which office might be for a year, and then another might succeed him therein (see on Luke 3:2). The suffragan was the *Vicarius*, or deputy of the high priest.

**14. ***{now Caiaphas was he}* Ch. 11:50.

*{expedient that one man should die, &c.}* So excellent is truth, that it does not only give some light to the dim eyes, but also sometimes break out of the enemies’ tongues: so Caiaphas prophesied: it was indeed expedient that this one man should die, that all men might not die eternally: indeed, *ἡ* *σωτηρία* *τῇ* *οἰκουμένῃ* *ὁ* *θάνατος* *αὐτοῦ*, Chrysostom, *hom.* 82. His death was salvation to the world: but this blind prophet understood not his own words.

**15. ***{Simon Peter followed}* Matt. 26:58; Mark 14:55; Luke 22:54.

*{that disciple was known}* It seems it was not John who wrote this Gospel, as some think, but some other of Christ’s disciples; as has been said formerly.

**16. ***{spake to her that kept the door}* Desired her to let in Peter.

**17. ***{art not thou also one, &c.}* Matt. 26:69; Mark 14:66, &c.; Luke 22:56, 57.

**18. ***{for it was cold}* Being in the night, near morning, and springtime, Matt. 26:58; Mark 14:54; Luke 22:55.

**19. ***{the high priest then, &c.}* See on v. 13.

*{of his disciples, and of his doctrine}* Probably he questioned him, why he gathered so many disciples, and what he taught them? All to find something whereof to accuse him of sedition or innovation.

**20. ***{In secret have I said nothing}* Nothing but that which I have, for substance, publicly preached, in the temple and synagogues.

**21. ***{why askest thou me?}* Whom you will not believe.

*{ask them which heard me}* Many strangers, many enemies heard me: examine these witnesses.

**22. ***{with the palm of his hand}* *ἔδωκε* *ῥάπισμα*, Syr. *struck Jesus upon the cheek*. See Matt. 26:67; Mark 14:65.

**23. ***{but if well, why smitest thou me?}* Thus he convinces his adversaries of injustice: complaining as infirm man, yet being also God omnipotent, so that he could easily have avenged it. But as Ambrose says, *Serm.* 20, *in Psalm* 118, *Quæri maluit, quam vindicare*: to give us example, he had now rather complain, than revenge. The impious servant smites the most holy Lord: this was not judgment, but violence.

**24. ***{now Annas}* Matt. 26:57. See on Luke 22:60.

**25. ***{they said therefore unto him}* Matt. 26:69, 70.

**26. ***{one of the servants}* Matt. 20:73; Mark 14:70; Luke 22:39.

**27. ***{then Peter denied again}* Matt. 26:74; Mark 14:71; Luke 22:60.

**28. ***{then led they, &c.}* Matt. 27:2; Mark 15:1; Luke 23:1. See Is. Casaubon *exercit.* 16, N. 76.

*{the hall of judgment}* Or *Pilate’s house, or Pretory*.

*{themselves went not, &c.}* Acts 10:28. It was not lawful by their tradition to accompany the Gentiles: especially, being to eat the Passover. This they make conscience of; but none to kill the Lord of life. See Acts 10:28 and 11:5.

*{but that they might eat the Passover}* That is, those sacrifices which they usually were to offer, at that feast of the Passover; (as some say) because the paschal lamb, they were (by the law) to eat the even before. Deut. 16:1. And certainly Christ with his disciples, had eaten the paschal lamb the even before he was apprehended, and led to Pilate, as it is clear, Matt. 26:17, &c.; Mark 14:12, &c.; Luke 22:7, &c. Others think otherwise, and that *Jesus ate not the Passover* the same day on which the Jews ate it, (against which the forecited places seem too express:) they say, because that they find no express place of Scripture clear enough, whence it may be gathered, those sacrifices to be offered on the seven days of unleavened bread, were ever called by that name: Deut. 16:2. *Thou shalt sacrifice the Passover* (*פסח*) &c., *צאן* *ובקר* of the flock of sheep, and herd, or bullocks. See hereof Num. 28:29, 14 and Deut. 16:2, &c. And you shall find these Passover-offerings expressly called *פסחים* *pesachim*, the plural of the word *פסח*, *πάσχα*. See V. Shindlar *Pentegl.* in *פסח*; 2 Chron. 35:8, 9, 70, *εἰς* *τὸ* *φασεκ*, &c. So Chrysostom understands *τὸ* *πάσχα*, *τὴν* *ἑορτὴν* *πᾶσαν*, the whole time of the solemnity of that feast of Passover. But what place of Scripture shows expressly, or by necessary consequence, that Christ ate the Passover, not on the same day on which the Jews did eat it? The first day of the feast of unleavened bread, Christ ate it, Matt. 26:17, &c.; Mark 14:12, &c.; Luke 22:7. Then it was by the law to be eaten. See Num. 28:16, &c.; Deut. 16:1, 2, 3, 4, *At even*, Deut. 16:6. And how can it appear that the Jews did not now observe the law on this behalf? Or that (as some think) their strict caution against defilement, making them uncapable (by the law, or their tradition) of eating at the feast, of their offerings, must needs relate only to that day on which the Passover was to be offered and eaten, or the first and last days of the feast? Seeing every uncleanness (whether determined by the law, or conceived to be so, by their tradition) did make them for the time incapable of eating things offered in sacrifice to the Lord, as Lev. 5:1, 2, &c.; Lev. 7:19, 20, 21; Num. 18:11, 13; Num. 19:20, or in their opinions so, if it were but conceived such by their own traditions.

**29. ***{what accusation bring you, &c.}* So Matt. 27:23; Mark 15:14. He said, *What evil hath he done?* For he knew that they had delivered him out of envy, Mark 15:10. Therefore though they had accused him as a perverter of the people, yet he also publicly professed, *I find no fault in this man*, Luke 23:4, 14. So also here, v. 38.

**30. ***{if he were not a malefactor, &c.}* As if they said, we are godly people, and would not accuse any but a malefactor: there is no cause why you should suspect our charity, or question our integrity (yet which of the prophets did they not persecute? Acts 7:52), so blind and presumptuous is hypocrisy: but if it be enough to accuse, who shall be innocent?

**31. ***{judge him according to your law}* This he spoke, either because he conceived it to be a matter of their religion, and so fit for the cognizance of their Sanhedrin: or ironically he derides their fury against right and equity; for the Romans permitted them no power of judicature in capital matters, as they confess, nor did they condemn any man to death for favor, or unheard. See Acts 25:16.

**32. ***{that the saying of Jesus}* How he should die condemned by a Gentile, (Matt. 20:19) and be crucified, John 12:33, for that was then the use of the Romans, and as the most grievous, so the most ignominious kind of death.

**33. ***{then Pilate, &c.}* Matt. 27:11.

*{judgment-hall}* Or *Pretory*, v. 28.

**34. ***{sayest thou this thing of thyself}* Is this your own prejudicate opinion of me? Or is this the objection of my accusers? Have they informed you so, or suggested anything to induce you to think, or suspect so?

**35. ***{am I a Jew}* What care I for the King whom the Jews expect? Or for their religion? I am no Jew: I ask not for any interest of mine own: it is the instigation of your own countrymen; they question whether you are their expected King Messias, and convent you as the author of sedition, under pretense of your affectation of that title.

**36. ***{my kingdom is not of this world}* Not that he governs not in heaven and earth: for though his kingdom be not of this world, *tamen rex est, etiam in hoc mundo*, Bernard *in Epiphan.*, *serm.* 2. When Pilate had openly declared whose accusation it was; Jesus plainly tells him, that his kingdom was not secular, but spiritual; not of this world. So that he nothing intrenched on Caesar’s right; which he says, not that he might avoid death, but to show that he was unjustly accused.

**37. ***{Thou sayest that I am, &c.}* It is an Hebrew manner of concession; meaning, I am, as Mark 14:62, or you gather by my words that I am a king.

**38. ***{What is truth?}* What truth do you speak of? Whatever Pilate’s meaning was in this query, it is more improbable that he did not therefore inquire concerning truth, with a purpose to be instructed thereby, for he not so much as expected Jesus’ answer, but presently went out to the Jews.

**39. ***{But ye have a custom}* Matt. 27:15.

*{one at the Passover}* At the feast of Passover: not on the day wherein the Passover was to be offered, and eaten, that was now past (see on v. 28), but at the feast, which lasted divers days. See Is. Casaubon *Exercit.* 16, N. 31.

**40. ***{then cried they all again}* Mark 15:8, 13, 14; Luke 23:18; Acts 3:14. All his eager and malicious enemies: the major part of the people then present; otherwise it is very likely, that those that consented not to Jesus’ death, did not so cry out. See Luke 23:23.

*{Not this man, but Barabbas}* Such is the judgment of an impious world, condemning holy Jesus; and acquitting a murderous Barabbas, *τοὺς* *μὲν* *ὁμοτρόπους* *αἰτοῦσι*, Chrysost. *hom.* 83. They require and acquit their like.

## CHAPTER 19

**1. ***{Then Pilate therefore took Jesus}* Matt. 27:26.

*{and scourged him}* That is, caused him to be scourged by his officers; possibly hoping thereby to appease the fury of the Jews. The Prophet Isaiah 53:5 foretells the effect, *that with his stripes we might be healed*.

**2. ***{a crown of thorns}* The crown is a sovereign ornament; and so was the purple robe (see Pompon. Laet. *de Magistr. Rom. de Rege*) in so much as that it was once accounted treason, for any private man to wear it: the Romans admitted it to kings (so says Jerome *in Isai.* 61, it is *insigne regii decoris*, &c.), great men in triumphs wore it, and others at sacrifices, and funerals: so that although they did this in derision of Christ’s kingdom, yet it did as it were proclaim him a king, and a sacrificer, by his death triumphant. See Is. Casaubon *Exerc.* 16, N. 79.

**3. ***{Hail king of the Jews}* Or *God save thee O King*, Syriac *שלם* *לך* *shelom lech*, *peace be to thee*, it was a common manner of salutation among them. So Judg. 6:23, *שלום* *לך* *shalom lecha*. So Judg. 19:20; 1 Sam. 25:6; 1 Chron. 12:18; Luke 10:5.

*{smote him with their hands}* Or *gave him stripes with rods*, *ῥαπίσματα*, *ῥαπίζειν* is of *ῥαπὶς*, *virga*, a rod, or switch. See on ch. 18:22.

**4. ***{I bring him forth to you}* For they would not enter into the Pretory. See on ch. 28:18. Therefore he caused Jesus after he had been scourged, to be brought out unto them; who were waiting before the Pretory, to attend what should finally be done to him.

*{I find no fault in him}* See ch. 18:38. You accuse him of many things, but upon serious examination thereof, I find in him no crime, no cause why I should condemn him; this shows that Pilate slighted all that they urged concerning his affecting to be a king; as either proceeding out of their envy and malice, or Christ’s otherwise pretending to a kingdom, than could at all be prejudicious to Caesar, and the state of Rome, had he, either by any secret, or public practices of Jesus, by drawing a dangerous party, providing arms and treasure, or casting out seditious words against Caesar or the state, (which things Pilate being the Roman deputy, would diligently inquire after) perceived any endeavor of Jesus, in their sense, to make himself king, he durst not have pronounced Jesus innocent, lest himself should have been impleaded, guilty of confederacy with him.

**5. ***{wearing the crown of thorns}* So bare he, and took away that curse of the earth, Gen. 3:18. This may have an allusion to that sacrifice taken by the head in the thorns, which Abraham found upon the same mountain, whereon Christ began his passion. See Gen. 22:2; 2 Chron. 3:1 & Clem. Alexand. *Paed.*, l. 2, c. 8.

*{behold the man}* Behold what a king you are afraid of: or this he said to move them to compassion, whom they saw and knew causelessly torn with whips.

**6. ***{they cried out saying, crucify him, &c.}* *ἐκραύγασαν*: it imports, an importunate confused vociferation or yawling out; such as troubled crows make, when they cry altogether: so Eustat. *κράζειν* and *κρώζειν*, is said *de cornicibus & corvis*. So Clem. Alexandr. *ad Gent.* uses the word, *οὐ* *κρώζοντα*, *οὐ* *κλάζοντα*, &c. They who could allege nothing against Christ, cry him to death with noise of multitudes, by so much more violently reasonless, by how much more reason the judge alleged to appease them. So cried the multitude against Paul, Acts 22:22. So cried the Ephesians for their Diana, Acts 19:28. So the heathens were wont to cry in any public calamity, *Christianos ad leonem*, cast the Christians to the lion. Tertul. *adv. Gent.*, cap. 40.

*{take ye him and crucify him}* This expresses Pilate’s indignation at their clamorous proceedings, as Chrysostom notes: he permitted him not to their power to kill, which the Romans gave them not: but in effect denied to be a servant to their unjust passion, which the following words seem to intimate.

**7. ***{We have a law}* By their law a blasphemer ought to be stoned to death, Lev. 24:16, which might justly have taken hold on themselves, it could not on Christ; for he is indeed the Son of God: but here appears the daring impiety of the wicked, wresting laws to their own lusts, thereby to take away the righteousness of the most innocent, and to condemn even Christ himself.

**8. ***{he was more afraid}* Hitherto the innocency of Jesus made him fear to condemn him, though so many confused outcries sounded in his ears; sometimes the law, sometimes necessity of state, and Caesar’s right was pretended: yet fluctuant between these, and his own fear, his conscience told him that Jesus was innocent: and now a sharper scruple touches him, lest he should condemn one more than a man, and that prove true which they objected against him for a crime, that he was indeed the Son of God.

**9. ***{whence art thou?}* Of what parents? The Jews’ last article, and Jesus’ own former speech, ch. 18:36, 37, that his kingdom was not of this world, increase his jealous fear: Jesus knowing that Pilate would at last comply with the Jews’ fury; and being now to die, not for himself, but for us, answered not to this, as they who plead to be delivered.

**10. ***{Knowest thou not that I have power, &c.}* By this, Pilate condemned himself, when confessing that he had power to release him, he would yet without any fault crucify him, to ingratiate with the Jews: *mala potestas licere quod noceat*, Ambros. *in Psal.* 118, *serm.* 20, it is an evil power to be able to mischief.

**11. ***{except it were given thee from above}* Except God gave it you (as ch. 3:27, 31) or permitted it to you, however great your authority, and the strength of the wide-spreading eagle’s wings, the Roman empire be, you could do nothing at all against me, did not God for the present permit you to do that which is in order to the performance of his holy will for my suffering, to redeem his elect.

*{hath the greater sin}* *Greater sin than thine*, the Syriac has it, as comparing Pilate’s sin in condemning Christ for fear, with the high priest’s sin, delivering him up to Pilate for malice; or he taxes the Jews in that they sinned against God, for whom judges must judge, and whose office it is to defend the innocent: and against Pilate, whose authority their importunity abused and misled; and against holy Jesus the Lord of life, whom for envy they delivered.

**12. ***{Whosoever maketh himself a king}* That is, pretends to be a king.

**13. ***{the pavement}* *Λιθόστρωτος*, a kind of rich pavement of stones artificially wrought and joined together. See Plin., lib. 36:25, cap.; Ezek. 40:17 and 42:3. It is called *רצפה* *ritspah* of *רצף* *stratus lapidibus*, Syriac *ריצפתא* *דכאפה* *ratziphatho deciphe*, a pavement of stone: but in Hebrew it is here called Gabbatha, [*Chaldaea forma est vox, significat autem altum & editum*, &c. Vatabl.] of *גבא* *gabah*, which signifies, to be raised up, or made high: the word here used by the Greek *λιθόστρωτος*, is compounded of *λίθος*, a stone, and *στρώννυμι*, to strew or pave, and imports a place raised (as tribunals used to be, that the judges might with convenience see and hear, and be seen and heard) and curiously paved with stone. See C. Rodigin. *Lect. ant.*, lib. 17, cap. 9. The evangelist here uses the word *γαββαθᾶ*, made of the Hebrew.

**14. ***{It was the preparation}* *παρασκευὴ* *τοῦ* *πάσχα*, a day wherein they prepared themselves, and those things which were necessary to that solemnity: or a *parasceve* of the Passover; one day of the feast, which Mark 15:42 is plainly said, to be the *προσάββατον*, the day before the Sabbath. See also v. 31.

*{about the sixth hour}* That is, about noon. If we compare this place with Mark 15:25, it will appear that Mark saying it was the third hour when they crucified him, reckoned according to the Jews’ account; who began their artificial day from sunrising, but John according to the Roman account in use with us, who begin our day from midnight, and so this sixth hour was their first, our sixth in the morning; between which and the third hour, (our ninth) in the morning, were acted many things omitted by the evangelist, and related by the rest, as his sending to Herod, Luke 23:7, &c., he questioning with him in many words, the chief priests accusing him before Herod: Herod’s soldiers arraying him; and mocking him, sending him again to Pilate, and which took up the three hours, between Pilate’s first sitting, and his giving the final sentence on Jesus, and the execution thereof in fastening him to the cross.

*{behold your king}* This he speaks in scorn.

**15. ***{We have no king but Caesar}* They acknowledge that the scepter was now departed from Judah, and therefore had not malice blinded them, they would have understood that mark of the Messias coming at that very time. See Gen. 49:10.

**16. ***{then delivered, &c.}* Matt. 27:26; Mark 15:15.

**17. ***{and he bearing the cross}* Which they had prepared to crucify him on. Crucifying and bearing the cross, was a capital punishment; and putting to death after the Roman manner: they made him bear it till they found Simon of Cyrene, whom they compelled to carry it for him. See Luke 23:26. See note thereon.

*{Golgotha}* The place where they executed malefactors, on the north side of the Mount Sion, Hieronym. *de locis Hebraicis*, Euseb.

**18. ***{two other with him}* According to prophecy, Isa. 53:12, *he was counted with the transgressors*.

**19. ***{And Pilate wrote a title}* Christ upon his cross is entitled king, by his hand, whose mouth condemned him for aspiring to a kingdom. See Matt. 27:37; Mark 15:26; Luke 23:38.

**20. ***{Hebrew, Greek, and Latin}* God’s providence so disposing it, that many might read it; for unto these feasts at Jerusalem came men of several nations. See Acts 2:5. It was the fashion of the ancients to signify to the public, the cause of any delinquents’ sufferings, either by the voice of a crier, or by setting it up written in capital letters. See Spartian. *in Severo*, Lamprid. *Alexand.*, Sueton. *Caio*.

**21. ***{Write not the king of the Jews}* They were afraid that this would be too much honor to Christ, and ignominy to themselves among the nations, when they should hear that they had crucified their king; therefore they desired that the title might be so altered, that Christ might thereby seem condemned for his ambition, not their importunate malice.

**22. ***{What I have written, &c.}* That is, I will not change it; for so it seems their custom was: the public tables containing the decrees of their proconsuls, once published, might not be reversed. See Apulei. *Florid.*, l. 1, *sub finem*.

**23. ***{Then the soldiers}* Matt. 27:35; Mark 15:24; Luke 23:34. The soldiers of the Roman garrison, who put him to death.

*{woven}* Of one piece without seam. See hereof Is. Casub. *Exerc.* 16, num. 117.

**24. ***{They parted my raiment}* Ps. 22:18. They made four parts of his raiment, beside the seamless coat, which they agreed not to make useless by dividing it, but to cast lots for it, which of them should have it whole to himself, they cast lots for the other four parts, according as they had agreed for to divide, but here they again put the coat to another particular lot. See Matt. 27:35. This part of the history, John more exactly records than the other evangelists to show, that as all other things foretold by the prophets, concerning the sufferings of the Messias, so even this concerning the parting of his garments, was fulfilled in Christ’s passion, declaring him to be the promised and expected Messias and Savior of the elect, to whom all the prophecies so punctually agreed.

**25. ***{Cleophas}* Or *Clopas*. See Luke 24:18.

**26. ***{whom he loved}* John, who wrote this Gospel, ch. 21:24.

*{Woman behold thy son}* Meaning, I commend you to his care and charge, that he may keep you in your age: a singular pattern of piety, amidst such torments to be careful of his dear mother.

**27. ***{unto his own home}* *εἰς* *τὰ* *ἴδια*, as c. 1:11. Syriac interpreter, *apud se*, Epiphanius *haeres.* 78:10, gives it, *πρὸς* *ἑαυτὸν*, *unto himself*, to his house, care and custody.

**28. ***{that all things were now accomplished}* That all things were thus far forth accomplished in the great work of our redemption.

*{that the Scripture might be fulfilled}* Ps. 69:21. As all other prophecies, in every particular, concerning the sufferings of the Messias were exactly fulfilled in Christ’s passion, so was this, their giving him, thirsting, vinegar to drink.

**29. ***{a vessel full of, &c.}* They were wont to give those who were to be executed, vinegar, or sharp wine mingled with myrrh (Mark 15:24); some say to stupefy them; it may be to keep those from fainting, whom they would longer torment: the soldiers gave it him instead of drink, out of barbarous inhumanity, and impiety mocking him, but God’s providence turned it (as all other their actions, with whatsoever intention designed) to the clear demonstration of his truth really fulfilled in Christ.

*{put it upon hyssop}* Not that hyssop grew in those parts so high, as to be used in place of a reed, as some think; see Is. Casaubon *exercit.* 16, N. 126. See 1 Kings 4:33, where it is reckoned among the small plants: probably they bound hyssop to the reed or cane (mentioned Matt. 27:48) and put the sponge filled with vinegar on the top of the same, that they might reach it to his mouth; as that which (as some physicians affirm) being used with vinegar would keep from fainting: possibly for some such end they made their *Hyssopites*, Plin., lib. 14, cap. 16, hyssop wine. See Plin. 21, cap. 1, *sub finem*; Dioscor., lib. 5, cap. 50. This herb was in use, in their sacrifices and lustrations with water, Exod. 12:22, *you shall take* *אגודת* *אזוב* *aguddah ezob*, a bundle of hyssop, 70. *δεσμὴν* *ὑσσώπου*, *and dip it in the blood, that is, in the bason, and strike the lintel*, &c. See Lev. 14:4; Num. 19:18. Because this sprinkling with an hyssop bunch was the last act in their ceremony of cleansing, David for a sign of his sins’ remission, prays, Ps. 51:9, *cleanse me with hyssop*, *οὐδεμίαν* *ἄφεσιν* *ἁμαρτημάτων* *εἰργάζετο*, *αἴνιγμα* *δὲ* *τι* *σημαίνει*, says Suidas, hyssop could not work any remission of sins, it signified a riddle or mystery: certainly neither could the blood of bulls or goats herewith sprinkled, take away sin; but that only which was hereby signified, the sacred blood of Christ. The apostle expresses the ceremony, Heb. 9:19, 20. *Moses took the blood of calves and goats, with water and scarlet wool, and hyssop, and sprinkled both the book, and all the people, saying, this is τὸ αἷμα τῆς διαθήκης, the blood of the testament which God enjoined unto you*: now the law had but *a shadow of good things to come*, Heb. 10:1. Suidas therefore says well, *τύπος* *ἦν* *ἐκεῖνα* *τῶν* *σωτηρίων* *παθημάτων*: these were types of (Christ’s) saving passions. Christ therefore said, in the institution of the seal of the new covenant, *this cup is, ἡ καινὴ διαθήκη ἐν τῷ αἵματί μου, the new testament in my blood*, Luke 20:21, now presently to be shed, the old testament signifying the same, was that before mentioned, Heb. 9:19, 20. Whatever the soldiers intended in this act of theirs, of binding the hyssop, or shedding Christ’s blood, God’s providence disposed it all so, that we may herein learn that comfortable doctrine concerning the cleansing of our sins typified under the law, but performed in the blood and passion of Christ.

**30. ***{it is finished}* That is, man’s redemption; and all the ceremonies of the law, are fulfilled and ended in the exhibition of that truth which they prefigured: he speaks here of that which presently should be; and in the yielding up his ghost was accomplished. See ch. 17:4.

**31. ***{for that Sabbath was an high day}* *Μεγάλη* *ἡ* *ἡμέρα*, a great day, that is, a very solemn day, in respect of the concurrence of a double celebrity: the Sabbath, and a day of this feast of Passover: (see Num. 28:17, 18. So here ch. 7:37, the last day of the feast is said to be, *that great day of the feast*) so that they might not take him down thereon.

**32. ***{brake the legs of the first}* The soldiers which crucified them came and broke their legs to dispatch them, after they had obtained it of Pilate, v. 31, that they might not remain upon the cross on the Sabbath; therefore when they saw Jesus dead already, they saved that needless labor: this did God’s providence dispose so, because he was shortly to rise again: for though he could as easily have made his bones whole, as cure his heart-wound, and rise again from the dead; yet other concurrent difficulties hindering the faith of infirm men, he would not have this added thereto.

**34. ***{there came out blood and water}* They pierced his side, to prove if he were indeed dead, or otherwise to make him sure; this water and blood forthwith issuing out showed that he was truly dead, the pericardium which keeps that water about the heart, being pierced: thus was fulfilled that which was foretold, Zech. 12:10, *and they shall look upon me whom they have pierced*, as v. 37. Thus came Jesus by *water and blood*, 1 John 5:6. Thus was there *a fountain opened to the house of David, and the inhabitants of Jerusalem* (even to all the elect) *for sin and for uncleanness*, Zech. 13:1. This was done that the enemies might not object that he rose again, because he was but half dead on the cross, and so, taken down, revived: to testify the contrary truth, John so seriously affirms (v. 35) the certainty of his death, his heart-blood streaming out, which he saw that stood by the cross.

**35. ***{He knoweth that he saith true}* He knows exactly, and certainly, who was so near an eyewitness, that he could not be deceived therein, as they easily may, and often are, who take up things upon the bare report of other relaters.

*{that ye might believe}* That is, that Jesus was truly dead, his heart being thus pierced.

**36. ***{that the Scripture might be fulfilled}* Exod. 12:46; Num. 9:12; Ps. 34:20, that those types might be accomplished in Christ the truth which they prefigured. So it appears that Christ our Passover is offered, 1 Cor. 5:7, and that he is that only Lamb of God which takes away the sin of the world, ch. 1:29, and that which was here done fell not out by chance or the counsel of man, but by God’s all-disposing providence, working by her impious murderers of Christ, that which his own hand and counsel had preordained, as Acts 4:28.

**37. ***{they shall look on him, &c.}* Zech. 12:10. Whom they thus wounded on the cross. This the Psalmist (speaking of the parting of his garments, &c.) records also, Ps. 22:17, *they look and stare upon me*: and in the judgment, *every eye shall see him, and they also which pierced him*, Rev. 1:7. Happy shall they be who with the eye of faith now look up to him, as the wounded Israelites to the brazen serpent; as those happy heart-wounded converts, Acts 2:37, at Peter’s sermon, look back on him whom their sins have crucified, in true faith and repentance for *the remission of those their sins*—that they may be saved.

**38. ***{after this Joseph, &c.}* See on Matt. 27:57 and on Luke 23:50, 51.

*{but secretly}* Before Christ’s death, secretly; but now he declared himself to be one of Christ’s disciples.

*{for fear of the Jews}* Who had determined to excommunicate any that confessed Jesus to be the Christ, ch. 9:22.

**39. ***{Nicodemus which at first, &c.}* Ch. 3:1, 2.

**40. ***{in linen clothes, &c.}* Because they had not time then perfectly to embalm him, the Sabbath being so near. This was done by God’s providence, that these men should give Christ an honorable burial, that it might be more remarkable; which would not probably have been so, if some poor or obscure persons had performed this office: and had his own apostles, or any of them been so daring as to have done it, the adversaries would possibly have said, that they had conveyed him away, and not indeed buried him: but this objection was taken away, and the Jews were therein convicted by the testimony of some of their own; and those honorable persons.

**41. ***{was a garden}* In a garden the first Adam fell, in a garden the second Adam was buried, and rose again; as we fell in Adam, we shall rise in Christ. See Is. Casaub. *exercit.* 16, N. 134, 98.

*{wherein was never man yet laid}* God’s providence so ordaining it; lest any caviller should say, that it was some other body not Christ which rose again: or that he rose again by some other’s virtue, not his own, as 2 Kings 13:21. *in aliena sepultura ponitur, qui pro aliorum moriebatur salute*, Augustine *de temp. serm.* 133, he was laid in another man’s sepulcher, who died for others’ sins, not his own.

**42. ***{the Jews’ preparation day}* Verse 14. John speaks here, as of a thing already antiquated and abolished: for the Lord’s day, our Sabbath, was observed by the Christians when he wrote this Gospel. The Jews’ preparation, was our Friday, and their Sabbath our Saturday, and the Lord’s day, which we observe from the resurrection of Christ, their first day of the week, ch. 20:1; Luke 24:1; Mark 16:1, 2; Matt. 28:1; 1 Cor. 16:2; Rev. 1:10.

## CHAPTER 20

**1. ***{The first day}* See on ch. 19:42; Matt. 28:1; Mark 16:1, which is with us the Lord’s day: *τῇ* *κυριακῇ* *ἡμέρᾳ*, on the Lord’s day, Rev. 1:10.

*{when it was yet dark}* She departed from home before day, and by that time she came to the sepulcher, the sun was about to rise, Mark 16:2. This was the *first day of the week*, *τῇ* *δὲ* *μιᾷ* *τῶν* *σαββάτων*, here *τῇ* *μιᾷ* is put for *τῇ* *πρώτῃ*: the one day; that is, the first. So Gen. 1:5, *יום* *אחד* *jom echad*, 70. *ἡμέρα* *μία*, the first: the same Gen. 8:5 gives, *חד*, *τῇ* *πρώτῃ*, *the first*. So Dan. 9:1, which manner of speaking the apostles also retained in the Greek, Matt. 28:1; 1 Cor. 16:2. See Is. Casaub. *exer.*, c. 16, N. 170.

*{unto the sepulchre}* These women named, Mark 16:1, came to embalm him; which, for want of time they could not before do. See ch. 19:40.

*{and seeth the stone taken away}* For the angel descended and rolled it back, Matt. 28:2.

**2. ***{other disciple whom Jesus loved}* Ch. 13:23 and 21:20.

**4. ***{the other disciple did outrun Peter}* Both made haste to know what the matter was (*οὐδὲ* *γὰρ* *φέρει* *τὴν* *ἀναβολὴν* *ὁ* *ζῆλος*, Nazianzen *orat.* 18, zeal (and love) brooks no delay), but with unequal strength: John being young, and so of better agility, came first to the sepulcher: see on Luke 24:12.

**5. ***{and he stooping down}* *παρακύψας*, the word is of that which signifies to hold down the head to see, or look into, James 1:25, *whoso παρακύψας looketh into that perfect law*, &c. The door of this sepulcher was low, and the vault of such depth digged into the rock (in manner of a cave) Matt. 27:60, that without stooping down they could not see into it: it may have this moral; the mysteries of our salvation accomplished in the death, burial, and resurrection, of our Savior, can be looked into by none but those who stoop to see: they admit no eyes of the proud to view them.

**6. ***{Then cometh Simon}* See on Luke 24:12.

**7. ***{the napkin that was, &c.}* See Luke 24:12. It was now light enough to see the white clothes there lying. See Matt. 27:59; Mark 15:46; Luke 23:53; John 11:44, and thus God’s providence had disposed, that even these small, and things, to man’s judgment not much considerable, should serve to the evidence of the truth of Christ’s resurrection, and the refutation of the false report of the Jews, that his disciples stole away his body out of the sepulcher; for in all reason and probability, they would not have carried him away naked but in the linen in which Joseph and Nicodemus had interred him.

**8. ***{and believed}* That Christ’s body was taken away according to Mary’s report: for as yet they knew not the Scripture, that he must rise again from the dead, v. 9.

**10. ***{went away again}* They made haste away, while yet but few were stirring about, for fear of the implacable malice of the Jews.

*{to their own home}* Where they before were, and from whence they ran to the sepulcher: finding there nothing certainly appearing to their sense, but only their missing of the body of Jesus, which possibly they might think was stolen away. See v. 13. They returned to the rest of the disciples, wondering with themselves how these things could so be, and what would be the issue, Luke 24:12, relating to the rest, that Jesus’ body was not in the sepulcher where it was laid, Luke 24:24, and silently expecting the event.

**11. ***{But Mary stood without, &c.}* Peter and John made haste away, lest notice should have been taken of them, as Christ’s disciples; and here the weaker sex showed more strength of resolution.

*{weeping}* Not yet understanding the mystery of Christ’s resurrection, she vented her grief with tears.

*{she stooped down}* See on v. 5. Either moved by the splendor of the angels, or some noise by them made. She was not yet satisfied with others’ report, but as in the loss of that which we dearly love, conceives hope against sense and reason; and, as Gregory says, *hom.* 25, *amanti semel aspexisse non sufficit*: a lover is not satisfied with one look: nor looked she in vain, to whose better information God sent his glorious ministering spirits.

**12. ***{seeth two angels}* Whether she knew them to be angels, or supposed them to be men, it is not here expressed: in white clothes, or garments.

*{the one at the head, &c.}* That is, at that part of the sepulcher where the head of Jesus was laid when they buried him.

**13. ***{Woman, why weepest thou?}* That is, weep not: it is not now a time to weep, but to rejoice: they said more unto her, which the other evangelists mention; but this omits: this is a speech mixed of reproof and comfort.

*{she saith unto them}* Surcharged with sorrow she considers neither who these glorious creatures were, nor much what they said to her, but minds only the occasion of her own present sorrow.

*{they have taken away my Lord}* Friends, or enemies, I know not whether, have conveyed away the body of my dear Lord Jesus, here buried: so too much passion makes one for present uncapable of reproof or comfort.

**14. ***{and saw Jesus standing}* Hearing some behind her, or perceiving by the angels’ gesture, or countenances, some other present, she looked back.

*{knew not that it was Jesus}* She saw, but knew not him she sought. See Luke 24:16. Mark 16:12. Matt. 28:17. Probably, he would not suddenly be known to her, lest now rising from the dead he might have affrighted her, which this, her slowness to believe (being before instructed concerning his death and resurrection, and now again by the angels) justly deserved: yet see the mercy of Christ; she first found him, who most affectionately sought him.

**15. ***{Jesus said unto her}* He well knew why she wept, and for whom she wept, but thus he takes occasion to manifest himself to her.

*{Sir, if thou hast borne him hence, &c.}* Probably she might suppose that he had overheard, what she answered the angels, therefore she replies as presupposing it: *if thou hast borne him hence*; yet she did not aspire to the thought of Christ’s divine power to raise him from the dead: so hard is it to shake off the deluding dreams of flesh and blood.

**16. ***{Mary}* In this familiar and usual compellation, she understood that it was Jesus which spoke to her.

*{Rabboni}* Sudden joy intercepted what she might more desire to say: this has the force of an interrogation; Is it my Master? My Lord Christ? See on ch. 1:38.

**17. ***{touch me not}* She being too much addicted to sense, Christ would teach her to lift up her mind to the consideration of his state of glory in heaven, where we must find him now with the eye of faith, not bodily senses, 2 Cor. 5:16. According as Augustine says to Paulinus, ep. 59, he would intimate, *tactum spiritualem, i.e. accessum fidei, se requirere*: that *he now requires a spiritual touching*; that is, *that we come to him with faith*, whereby we believe him to be on high with the Father: and he would now show her, that she must attend another office, which was to testify his resurrection from the dead: as if he had said, you need not make so much haste to touch my feet: I am not yet ascended, neither shall I so quickly, but you may be assured, by seeing, hearing, and touching me, that it is I who now am indeed risen from the dead; the pledge and assurance of your life and resurrection. See on Matt. 28:9.

*{my brethren}* The disciples. See on Matt. 28:10. Notwithstanding they had been scattered, and Peter had denied him in his great infirmity; yet without exception to him, he calls them his brethren.

*{my Father, and your Father}* Mine by nature, yours by adoption. Cyril. Hieros. Catec. 7 and 11.

**18. ***{Magdalene came and told, &c.}* This evangelist records not how Jesus appeared to her with the other women as they were going to tell these things to the disciples, which, Matt. 28:9, is recited.

**19. ***{Then the same day}* Mark 16:14. Luke 24:36.

*{when the doors were shut}* Shut (either because it was night, or for fear of the Jews) but he could make them open to himself, as he afterward did for Peter, Acts 12:10.

*{Peace be unto you}* This was an Hebrew manner of salutation; who by peace, meant all prosperity. See on Matt. 28:16.

**20. ***{He shewed unto them his hands, &c.}* Which were pierced with nails fastening him to the cross: thus confirms he them by all means, that they might be assured, that it was he who was indeed risen from the dead; lest otherwise they might have thought it had been but some apparition, or some other body, and not he. See on Luke 24:40.

**21. ***{As my Father hath sent me}* He gave them a mission, and charge before, but as preachers to warn the Jews to hear Christ: but now he sends them as apostles, and ambassadors into the other nations: committing that ministry to their execution, which himself had performed in teaching: he appoints them and their successors his surrogates in his absence. See Eph. 4:11. And that their authority to preach, might be understood to be God’s own ordinance, he tells them, that as his Father sent him, so he sends them to preach to all nations in his Name. See ch. 6:38. Mark 16:15. Matt. 28:19. Christ speaks the same truth by the apostles and ministers of the Gospel, which himself preached when he was conversant with men on earth.

**22. ***{he breathed on them}* By this sign prefiguring what they should receive; to wit, the gifts and graces of the holy Spirit, proceeding from the Father and the Son.

**23. ***{Whosesoever sins ye remit}* Matt. 18:18. This power is equally given to all the disciples, and their successors respectively; first in respect of the doctrine of faith, and repentance, as the key of heaven committed to their ministry, which (according as it is received and obeyed) binds or looses; if we believe and repent here on earth, God forgives in heaven; and it is the minister’s office to preach the same in general, and to apply it particularly, for the comfort and instruction of the truly penitent. Secondly, in respect of the power of excommunication and absolution, which is in discipline, that the spirit might be saved in the day of the Lord, 1 Cor. 5:5.

**24. ***{called Didymus}* See on ch. 11:16. Thomas recited under the character of *one of the twelve*, is spoken of ch. 11:16. We may not understand this with relation to the number of the apostles now being; (for Judas was gone to his own place, and Matthias was not yet chosen into his office, Acts 1:25, 26) but that which had been, when Jesus had first chosen twelve apostles, according to the number of the tribes of Israel.

*{was not with them}* Probably he was not yet returned (after they had been dispersed for fear) to the company of the apostles.

**25. ***{I will not believe}* So dangerous is it to be once absent from holy assemblies where Christ will be present (Matt. 18:20) to indulge to our own sense, to measure things divine and spiritual by our own carnal reason, and to give ourselves laws of believing.

**26. ***{his disciples were within}* Within the house where they remained, verse 19. For they or some of them, having been well known to divers in Jerusalem, they could not stir much abroad without great danger; the heat of the Jews’ rage being yet very little abated; (neither could they before go away out of the city, the feast of unleavened bread being not ended) now Thomas, however weak, and disbelieving their report and firm testimony concerning the resurrection of Christ, the main and fundamental article of our belief, yet would he not separate from their conventions.

*{Jesus came, &c.}* On the eighth day after his resurrection; that is, the next Lord’s day. Where Jesus was in the mean time, the Scriptures being silent, we may not curiously inquire: more safely note that Thomas being then with them it appears that neither did he in unbelief separate from the church representative, nor did they desert him, or abstain from communion with him, for so great an infirmity as was expressed in his professed unbelief: therefore Jesus came to them who has promised the blessing to the unanimous, Ps. 133 and Matt. 18:19.

**27. ***{reach hither thy finger}* Christ made such use of Thomas’ present unbelief, that he thereby left the truth of his resurrection, more certain and undoubted unto us: mark the admirable clemency of Christ, for one soul’s sake, he would appear again, and show his wounds to all the rest.

**28. ***{my Lord, and my God}* Here Thomas confesses his faith (which Christ approves, verse 29), *veram in illis cicatricibus patientis ac resurgentis virtutem divinitatis agnoscens*. Fulgent. de incarn. et Grat. D. Jesu &c. 11. *Acknowledging in those wounds of him that suffered and rose again the true power of the deity*; without which he could not have risen again. Thus Thomas spoke, as one coming to himself again: and this abrupt speech imports a vehement admiration, at Christ’s mercy, and his own stupidity and dullness to believe: it contains a short, but absolute confession of faith; he collecting from his resurrection, that Christ was the very Messias, Redeemer, and Lord of all (see Rom. 1:4) and with an explicit faith, professing his own interest therein: so far did his faith now excel sense, on which he before so much depended, that he who felt only the manhood, believed and confessed the united Deity of Christ: so soon can God change pertinacious unbelief into saving faith. See Acts 9:2. Gal. 1:23, 24. Thomas twice emphatically repeats, *my*, to declare that he now spoke out of deep and serious sense of faith. So God permits his to infirmity, and some slips; as that in their recovery, themselves and others, are more confirmed. See on verse 27. *plus enim nobis Thomæ infidelitas, ad fidem, quam fides credentium discipulorum profuit, &c.* Greg. hom. 26 in Evang. *For Thomas his doubting more advantages our faith, than the faith of the believing disciples*: if Thomas had not doubted, we had not been so assured that it was indeed the same Christ, who was crucified, dead and buried, and risen again; his doubting, put that out of doubt.

**29. ***{they that have not seen}* Which shall hereafter believe through the preaching of the Gospel, though they see me not in the flesh, or these wounds now showed you.

**30. ***{many other signs}* Ch. 21:25. He did these in the presence of his disciples to confirm them; after his resurrection, we read not, that he ever appeared to the wicked, or showed himself to them after his resurrection from the dead: he reserves that to their greater terror in the day of judgment. See Rev. 1:7. Acts 10:41.

*{which are not written in this book}* As being sufficiently recorded by the other evangelists: or forasmuch as God knew that to be sufficient which is written. See 2 Tim. 3:15.

**31. ***{But these things are written}* These things which Jesus taught and did: as a father (says Optatus l. 5) having many children, while he lives, and is present with them, governs them himself, and needs no will and testament; but drawing towards his end, fearing lest after his decease, his children may fall out, he calls witnesses, *voluntatem suam de pectore morituro transfert in tabulas diu duraturas*; *out of his dying breast he translates his will, on long-lasting tables, if any controversy happen, non itur ad tumulum, sed quæritur testamentum: et qui in tumulo quiescit, tacitus de tabulis loquitur vivus*: they run not to the grave (of the dead) but seek the will and testament; *and so he that rests in the grave, in those silent tables speaks his mind, as if he were alive*: he who made his will is in heaven; yet thus, his children consult with him: so Christ being bodily present with his church, did teach and govern them; but at last ordered that his will and testament should be written, for a constant rule of faith and manners, to all his children on earth, until his coming again to judge.

*{that ye may believe}* For, faith in Christ, and our salvation thereby, as it was the only scope and end of Christ’s doing of miracles, so was it of the evangelists recording the same.

## CHAPTER 21

**1. ***{Sea of Tiberias}* The sea of Galilee (John 6:1), so named from that adjacent province, or city of that name near to it: of old it was named *כנרת* *Chinnereth*. Num. 34:11. Deut. 3:17. Josh. 11:2 and 12:2. Chaldee *גינוסר* *Ginosar*: 70. *Κενερέθ*, *Χενερέθ*. See on Luke 5:1. John 6:1. Jerome de loc. Hebr. calls it, *Chennereth* the bound of Judea, in the tribe of Nephtalim: Herod called the city Tiberias, in honor of Tiberius Cæsar, Jerome ib. They since called it Tabaria. Adricom. Theatr. ter. S. Zabulon, Ptolemy, Strabo, and other Greek authors, call it as in the Gospel *Γεννησαρέτ*. Matt. 14:34. See Tremellius’ note thereon. Joseph de bel. Jud. l. 3, c. 18, calls it Genesar. Pliny l. 5, c. 15. This lake Genesar is sixteen miles long, and six miles broad; beset with pleasant towns: on the west thereof stood Tiberias; it abounded with divers sorts of fishes: the river Jordan runs through it. Adricom. q. s. The apostles, according to the command of Christ, and the admonition of the angels, after his resurrection, the days of unleavened bread being ended, leaving Jerusalem, went to Galilee. See Matt. 26:7. Matt. 26:32.

*{on this wise shewed he}* He sets down the time, place, persons, occasion, and manner of Christ’s appearing to them, to show the evidence and certainty of his resurrection.

**2. ***{Nathanael of Cana}* See ch. 1:49. Some think this was he who is called also *Βαρθολομαῖος*, Bartholomew, the son of Tholomew, of *בר* *bar*, a son, and *תלמי* *thalmai*. Num. 13:23. Judg. 1:10. 70. *Θελαμί* or *Θολμί*.

**3. ***{I go a fishing}* For though they were fishers of men, they were not yet sent out, neither had they received those visible gifts of the Holy Ghost, which were to furnish them out to that great work of preaching the Gospel to all nations; moreover, they wrought in their callings, to supply their present necessities, as Paul also sometimes did, 2 Thess. 3:8. See Luke 24:49. Therefore he acquaints the rest with his purpose, that they might join with him; which they accordingly did.

*{we also go with thee}* We will go with you, and join with you in fishing: for albeit the word be of the present tense; yet because it relates to an action not present, but future, it is well interpreted by the future. It may be the evangelist, who was one of them, would hereby express their present assent and readiness of mind upon the first motion of Peter, as also their certain resolution to go.

*{that night they caught nothing}* Thus the providence of God disposed it, for the more clear evidence of the ensuing miracle: had they caught anything the power and presence of Christ had not so clearly appeared, in this sudden success: all labor and endeavor of man is vain, unless God bless it with success, Ps. 127:1. See Gen. 11:7, 8. Hag. 1:6, 9, &c. Deut. 28:13, &c. God often exercises the patience of his: should all our labors succeed well, how few would beg God’s assistance? How seldom would men acknowledge his blessings?

**4. ***{When the morning was now come}* He expected until these disciples (weary with successless labor, and out of hope to get anything) would leave work; to declare that the opportunity and time which God takes to supply and relieve his, is then, when there appears to them least hope of help from second causes.

*{but the disciples knew not, &c.}* Either because it was not yet clear day, or because of the distance, they being so far from shore; but specially because Jesus would first be known by the miracle; and then by his countenance.

**5. ***{children have ye}* Or, *Sirs*.

*{have ye any meat?}* *Have you taken anything?* *Have you anything to eat?* As the Syriac has it: he asks as one who came to buy; but his purpose was in his eating, to testify his resurrection from the dead, and to give them assurance thereof by a further manifestation of himself unto them.

**6. ***{cast the net on the right side}* Thus he counsels them, as some ordinary person; the *right side of the ship*, is that which is one right hand of him that sits at the helm, which the seamen call the *starboard side*: he assigns one certain part of the ship, *ne fortuita videretur captura*, Euthymius: lest it should (rather) seem that they had taken such a draught by chance, than divine providence.

**7. ***{it is the Lord}* It is Christ who has thus admirably blessed our labors in this draught of fishes.

*{girt his fishers coat unto him}* Some close linen coat, which hindered not his swimming.

*{for he was naked}* Before he put on this, he had something on; for we may not simply understand that he had none other clothes on: 1 Sam. 19:24, it is said that Saul stripped off his clothes, and laid down naked all that day and all that night; that is, he put off his kingly robe, not all other his clothes. So walked Isaiah the Prophet, *naked, and barefoot*, Isa. 20:2; that is, without his *Prophet’s garment*. We may not think he went stark-naked to the discovery of his shame, and contempt of his person: and so Peter here was naked; that is, had not on that usual outward garment with which he used to go in public: except we shall understand, that he in his work of fishing had only the coat here mentioned on his otherwise naked body, which, hearing that it was the Lord, he presently girded so close to him, that it might not hinder his swimming ashore, and so hide his nakedness: but this shows with what diligence they had labored without success, until Christ came to them.

*{cast himself into the sea}* He leapt overboard to swim ashore, that he might come to Jesus (which the Syriac adds) impatient of the delay, which the ship must necessarily make, being to bring such a draught to shore: *serum æstimans si cum cæteris navigio perveniret*. Ambros. in Luc. sub. fin. Esteeming it too late to come with the rest in the ship: he valued no gains, but to come to Christ.

**8. ***{dragging the net with fishes}* *σύρω*, is properly to sweep the ground, as they do who fish with *trawls*, or draught-nets, whose plummets sink the lower part of the net to the ground, lest the enclosed fish should escape.

**9. ***{they saw a fire of coals there, &c.}* It seems while they were laboring in an ordinary calling, that Jesus (ever mindful of his) prepares food for them by an extraordinary way, against they came ashore, weary and hungry, so that they find there all necessaries ready: nor need we doubt who provided or how; he being present, who prepared Israel a table in the wilderness, who fed so many thousands with five loaves and two fishes: all creatures obey him; he gave them here an example of his divine providence, which never fails those that trust in him. See Exod. 34:28. 1 Kings 19:8. Heb. 13:5.

**10. ***{bring of the fish, &c.}* That they might not neglect his blessing, and lose that which he gave them, nor think that it was a vision: he bids them bring of the fish which they had now caught; it was his blessing, and their labor: his beneficence, and their propriety: so we say *our daily bread*, yet pray God to give it: that is ours, in which Christ gives us, a Divine, and civil right.

**11. ***{Simon Peter went}* Not he alone, but he with the rest: he chiefly is named, (who in zeal to enjoy the presence of Christ, waived the usual means of coming to him) and is now instructed to use the ordinary means to receive, and save God’s blessings, bestowed on him. Christ would not distract us from, but bless us in our lawful callings, that we may therein acknowledge his merciful providence. See 1 Tim. 4:4.

**12. ***{Come and dine}* Not that he now wanted food, but this was to assure them of the truth of his manhood then present, that they might not think it to be only a dream or *phantasm*; but that they might be evidently assured of his resurrection from the dead.

*{knowing that it was the Lord}* He so evidently manifested himself to them, that they durst not question the certainty of his resurrection and presence with them.

**13. ***{taketh bread}* They knew him in the manner of his breaking of bread. See Luke 24:35.

**14. ***{this is now the third time}* That is, the third time that he appeared to his disciples together, for otherwise he had more often appeared to some singly, ch. 20:16. Luke 24:15.

**15. ***{lovest thou me more than these}* More than you love these; or more than these love me: one reason of this query might be; for that Peter leaping into the sea to come to him before the rest, might seem to intimate so much: but the main is, because Christ had remitted a great sin of his. See Luke 7:47.

*{feed my lambs}* That is, the souls committed to your charge: for this is a sure argument of the minister’s love to Christ, if he conscionably labor to edify those whom Christ (the great shepherd of our souls) loves, and for whom he died, they love the world, not Christ, who seek their own gains, but neglect their auditors’ salvation; this Peter now learned, and afterward faithfully taught it others in the like ministration to them given in charge, 1 Pet. 5:1, 2, &c.

**16. ***{feed my sheep}* The repetition of the same charge, shows the importance thereof; and the necessity of our endeavor in this duty, which Christ so often, and so seriously enjoined him and all other faithful pastors and ministers of the Gospel, in and by him.

**17. ***{he said unto him the third time}* This often repetition of the charge does not only occasion Peter’s threefold confession, opposed to his thrice denying; but chiefly declares to the other apostles, that Peter was not to be rejected from their fellowship, office, and communion, for his threefold fall; being graciously received again by Christ, in his threefold confession of his love and obedience to him: and thus is washed off his former sin, confirming him to his apostleship and preaching the Gospel.

*{Peter was grieved, because, &c.}* Remembering how he had fallen, and fearing lest he should so fall again as he had done when he promised great constancy; or doubting that Christ (to whom he was better known than to himself) taxed him of dissembling: we grieve to have our fidelity questioned. Peter had been more cautious at first, had he well observed Christ’s words, Matt. 26:34. But he had not then so much experience of himself, as now: now he cautiously appeals to Christ’s omniscience; he presumes no more; most dangerously they fall, who most presume.

*{Lord, thou knowest all things}* *Τὰ* *παρόντα* *καὶ* *τὰ* *μέλλοντα*, Chrysost. *All things present and future*: what I now am, and what I shall be: thus modestly answers he now, having had dear experience of his infirmity and failing.

**18. ***{girdest thyself}* This he spoke, alluding to the manner of those eastern countries, where wearing long loose garments, they could at their pleasure, gird them to their bodies, to prepare themselves for labor or travel, and withal, in the next words, to the binding of Peter, as a prisoner for execution, and suffering martyrdom for the testimony of the truth of the Lord Jesus.

*{whither thou wouldst not}* *Τῆς* *φύσεως* *λέγει* *τὸ* *συμπαθὲς*, *καὶ* *τῆς* *σαρκὸς* *τὴν* *ἀνάγκην*, &c. Chrys. *He speaks the affection of nature, and necessity of flesh and blood*; intimating that the soul unwillingly parts from the body, however willing, by the comfort of the grace of regeneration, we are in Spirit.

**19. ***{he should glorify God}* This he said that we may know, that to suffer for Christ’s sake is *δόξα* *τῷ* *πάσχοντι* *καὶ* *τιμὴ*, *the glory and honor of the sufferer*; in that he honors God in suffering, which is the end for which we were created and redeemed. See 1 Cor. 6:20. Rev. 4:11. And those who honor God, will he assuredly honor, 1 Sam. 2:30.

**20. ***{Whom Jesus loved}* Ch. 13:23 and 20:2.

**21. ***{What shall this man do?}* That is, shall he not also feed your sheep? Shall he be divided from me? Or how shall he die?

**22. ***{What is that to thee}* He slights this curious unprofitable question of Peter: admonishing him not to trouble himself about others, but to look to himself: if I would have him live unto my second coming; what concerns it you? Follow you me in that I command you.

**23. ***{then went this saying abroad}* The evangelist here corrects this error rising from the misunderstanding Christ’s words concerning himself; lest when he died the malicious adversary should hence take occasion to asperse the truth of Christ, whom the very disciples understood not, but mistook in that matter: man is naturally prone to mistake: the more to be admired, is their overdaring ignorance of themselves, who think they cannot err: so high an opinion of a man’s self concludes him, neither good, nor wise.

**24. ***{This is the disciple which testifieth}* Hitherto he mentions himself under a third person: now he declares himself, an ear-witness, and an eye-witness, of that which he wrote for the greater certainty thereof. The apostles’ doctrine is certain, his modesty is observable; he calls himself a disciple, a learner, who excelled the most accomplished doctors, of the ages since: John Baptist called himself the voice of a crier in the wilderness, ch. 1:23. Peter and Paul, those great lights of the church and apostles of Christ called themselves, *servants of Christ*, Rom. 1:1. 2 Pet. 1:1, &c. What Spirit rules in those vainglorious prelates, who think no titles high enough for them?

**25. ***{there are also}* Ch. 20:30.

*{the world itself could not contain}* Herein he follows the vulgar manner of speaking, as ch. 12:19. The meaning of the *hyperbole* is, there would be very many books: moreover, he here glances at the depth of some mysteries, in the words and actions of Christ, which the world could not have apprehended, had they been written, which are to be put on account to those things which are reserved to be revealed unto us in the world to come; *When that which is perfect is come, and that which is in part shall be done away*, when we shall *know, even as also we are known*, 1 Cor. 13:10, 12.

*{Amen}* See Luke 24:53. Mark 16:20. Matt. 28:20 and John 3:11.
